# PEP 805 implementation status

This branch implements **ThreadGroup scheduling, object-state foundations,
Python instance/class and builtin set freezing, and shallow transfer through TransferBox and
Channel** from
[PEP 805](https://peps.python.org/pep-0805/), following the first step of its
[implementation appendix](https://peps.python.org/pep-0805/appendix-implementation/).
It is not a complete reference implementation. Object access can now be
validated explicitly in C, but ordinary VM and C API reference acquisitions
have not yet all been connected to that validation.

## Implemented

* Experimental owner-aware finalization and weakref callback dispatch. Foreign
  LOCAL finalizers run in their owner group; protected finalizers acquire their
  native protecting mutex. Weakref callbacks retain their registration group.
  Internal stops defer cleanup until execution resumes. Internal worker joins
  finish cleanup before reporting signal-handler exceptions, with bounded waits
  to service interrupts that arrive between native waits. Shutdown scheduling,
  worker pooling and complete native callback affinity remain unfinished.

* Internal per-thread held-mutex ID registry, including nested acquisitions,
  most-recent-first lookup and removal of a single matching acquisition.
  Protected-reference checks consult this registry rather than ThreadGroup
  membership. Storage is reserved before registration and freed with the thread
  state. Native primitive Lock context acquisition and release now register
  protective ownership. Lock.protect() makes shallow protected copies of
  unique temporary exact lists, dicts and sets. The initial transition requires
  an owning context; subsequent explicit acquire/release and protective fork
  reset are rejected. Ordinary cross-thread release remains supported.
  Native RLock also supports protection, retaining one held-ID registration
  through nested contexts until the outermost release. Its ownership-saving,
  restoring and fork-reset methods reject protective locks. Mixed manual and
  context acquisitions cannot be adopted for protection until fully released.
  Addition of exact native locks forms synchronized compound contexts, with
  canonical ordering, deduplication, reverse release and acquisition rollback.
  Pure Python instance copies (object as the native base) use copy.copy,
  detach their namespaces and protect both the instance and its dictionary.
  Python bound methods inherit protected receiver state. Namespace replacement
  requires the same protecting lock. Python fallback RLock, other native
  layouts and complete escaped-reference validation remain unimplemented.
  The argument-finalizer call-result regression now passes; these foundations
  must not be treated as complete race-free execution.

* Specialized tuple/list UNPACK_SEQUENCE checks all extracted references with
  _CHECK_UNPACK_ACCESS. The check remains after Tier 2's unique-tuple stealing
  substitution. Generic unpacking uses checked iterator acquisition; UNPACK_EX
  additionally validates all tail values before moving them out of its
  temporary list. Starred rest lists remain shallow containers.

* Conservative compiler checks for fast locals assigned within with/async with:
  all reads use LOAD_FAST_MAYBE_UNPROTECTED (including unbound-local handling).
  Inlined comprehension saves use LOAD_FAST_AND_CLEAR_CHECK, preserving NULL
  and leaving the local unchanged on an access error. Tagged pseudo-stores are
  recorded before CFG optimization; checks are inserted before fused/borrowed
  loads. The mask covers all locals, including EXTENDED_ARG indices. The
  Borrowed checked loads preserve the compiler's reference-lifetime analysis. The
  optimized dataflow analysis described in the appendix is still pending;
  this version deliberately also checks reads inside the context and after
  safe reassignment. General call/return and evaluation-stack lifetime checks
  remain incomplete.

* Experimental sys.monitoring.StopTheWorld context object: global cooperative
  pauses work in free-threading and GIL builds, including nested contexts,
  exception unwinding, GC, internal function-mutation pauses and temporary
  detachment. Parked threads release their GIL/group locks before waiting to
  reattach; threads created during a pause cannot enter Python until restart.
  The pause owner bypasses ownership/protection access checks, retaining
  ordinary immutability checks. The monitoring namespace is synchronized.
  This is not complete debugger support: broader call-boundary and evaluation
  stack lifetime checks and lifecycle coverage remain. Fork and interpreter creation/switch/destruction are rejected inside
  a pause. Native machine-code JIT and Windows are unvalidated.

* PyObject_GetAttr/GetAttrString and GetOptionalAttr/GetOptionalAttrString
  validate returned attribute references, including legacy tp_getattr and
  the optional generic/type/module fast paths. HasAttrWithError propagates
  access errors; legacy HasAttr reports them as unraisable and returns false.
  Import initialization reads an exact module's spec internally without
  exposing it through the checked public attribute API. GenericGetAttr and
  the VM stackref getter also check results; deferred references stay rooted
  while errors are constructed. LOAD_ATTR variants use _CHECK_ATTR_ACCESS,
  retained after Tier 2 constant folding. Inlined property/getattribute frames
  now use the general Python return access checks. Specialized LOAD_SUPER_ATTR variants
  retain the result check after Tier 2 method constant folding. Super lookup
  checks descriptors before binding or invoking them, and its getattribute
  slot checks returned values. Other direct slots and broader call-boundary
  integration remain separate work.

  Enabling these VM checks exposes unresolved Thread integration: local
  callback functions and stderr stored in shared Thread objects can still be
  rejected. Thread.start now snapshots cross-group invocation containers and
  the standard exception dispatcher retains its fallback objects in a shallow
  immutable tuple (see the later Thread invocation section). The native default
  hook now falls back to a local raw stderr printer when the selected stream
  cannot be accessed (see Thread exception output below). VM-created read-only
  closure bindings now permit synchronized functions, with reclassification
  when bindings become mutable (see Read-only binding implementation below).
  The prior ThreadGroup and
  public attribute suite successes do not describe the current whole branch.

* Python-implemented anext checks its input iterator and the object returned by
  __anext__, using a private native helper in the bootstrap module. Its
  no-default marker is frozen. Native sentinels implement shallow freezing;
  __module__ then becomes unwritable, and repr/reduce validate referenced names
  and custom representations before returning them.

* Native Context instances are synchronized, ContextVar instances and
  Token.MISSING are immutable, and contextvars/_contextvars have synchronized
  namespaces, with a synchronized native copy_context entry point.
  Stored/default values retain their own states.
  ContextVar.get (including the public C API), context subscription/get,
  Token.old_value, ContextVar repr and HAMT value comparison check access.
  Context snapshots use the existing native locking and immutable HAMT
  storage; concurrent entry remains prohibited. Tokens remain local.

* os has a synchronized namespace. Its default environment mappings use the
  native synchronized namespace base and a shared SynchronizedDict backing
  store. Encoding callbacks have no captured local cells. Thread construction
  can read the current PYTHON_PARALLEL value across groups, including runtime
  changes and replacement with a shared mapping. Native environment mutation
  and snapshot functions have not been blanket-declared synchronized.

* threading's weak thread registry uses synchronized native-backed instance
  storage, a SynchronizedSet and a closure-free removal callback. Iteration
  snapshots the set; registry copies install their own removal callbacks.
  Newly allocated exact weakrefs to synchronized targets are synchronized;
  subclasses, proxies and propagation to pre-existing references remain
  separate work. Weakref calls and PyWeakref_GetRef check the live target's
  current access state, including after it becomes local again.

* Condition, Event, Semaphore, BoundedSemaphore, Barrier and the Python RLock
  fallback use the native synchronized namespace base already used by Thread.
  Condition's waiter queue is SynchronizedList. Lock methods are resolved in
  the calling group rather than retained as creator-owned bound methods.
  Existing lock protocols still protect compound state transitions; sharing
  does not recursively change user-supplied callbacks or locks.

* LOAD_DEREF validates cell contents before creating the output stack reference,
  in both interpreter tiers. LOAD_FROM_DICT_OR_DEREF validates its result too.
  PyCell_Get and cell_contents apply the same access check; internal raw cell
  helpers retain their contracts. Empty-cell behavior is unchanged. Cell
  comparisons check contents before invoking comparison callbacks. PyCell_Set,
  cell_contents assignment/deletion, STORE_DEREF, DELETE_DEREF and frame-locals
  cell updates validate ownership and mutability before changing the cell.
  Readonly-closure classification and cell provenance remain incomplete.

* Python classes support `type.synchronize()`: their actual namespace becomes
  SynchronizedDict in place, preserving dictionary aliases and ordinary type
  slot/cache invalidation. Synchronization is shallow; instances, bases and
  namespace values retain their own states. Native metaclass storage requires
  explicit support. Synchronized classes can subsequently be frozen; freeze
  entry serializes the state check and transition using the class critical
  section. Special-attribute setters recheck mutability after audit hooks.
  Reentrant MRO changes and the remaining concurrent mutation boundaries still
  need auditing. The Python classes defined by threading opt into this support,
  and thread-name allocation uses its shared registry lock.

* LOAD_GLOBAL checks resolved values in generic, specialized module/builtins,
  and Tier 2 execution. A separate post-load check survives promotion of a
  global to an inline constant. LOAD_FROM_DICT_OR_GLOBALS also validates
  the resolved value, including its exact-dict global/builtin fast path and
  values obtained after resolving lazy imports. Remaining name-load paths,
  attributes, cells and native acquisition boundaries still need coverage.
  sys and _thread have synchronized namespaces and native entry points;
  immutable native types exported by _thread are declared immutable. Thread
  registration dictionaries are synchronized, and the monotonic clock entry
  points used by threading are declared synchronized. ExceptHookArgs is frozen,
  and a shared exception hook is exercised across groups. Thread construction
  from worker groups still needs integration.

* `PyObject_GetIter`, `PyObject_GetAIter`, and the VM's direct GET_AITER
  path validate newly returned iterators before exposing them. Builtin iter
  and aiter inherit the C API checks; generic custom iteration inherits
  PyObject_GetIter. VM self/virtual iteration reuses an existing stack reference.
  Await processing checks its input before invoking __await__ and validates
  the iterator returned by that slot. GET_ANEXT also checks the intermediate
  awaitable before processing it. Access errors retain their original type.
  Python-level anext return boundaries and other direct slot consumers still
  depend on the remaining general acquisition checks.
  The internal Python audit-hook registry uses SynchronizedList so audit events
  can iterate it across groups. Initial publication is serialized and readers
  acquire the published pointer atomically.

* `PyIter_Next`, `PyIter_NextItem`, and builtin `next()` validate returned
  items before publishing references. Rejected items are consumed and their
  newly acquired references released; access errors are not exhaustion and
  are not replaced by next's default value. Generic/instrumented `for` loops,
  virtual iteration, specialized list/tuple loops, and Tier 2 inline iterator
  calls now validate items too. Other direct iterator-slot consumers and
  inlined generator/async iteration still need coverage.
  `PyIter_Send` validates yielded and returned values in its native
  slot and fallback paths, including callers in generic SEND and asyncio.
  Specialized virtual SEND validates list/tuple items in both interpreter
  tiers before publishing them to the delegating generator.
  Direct generator/coroutine send, next and throw results, generator close
  return values, and unwrapped async-generator values are also checked.
  These checks run after suspension/completion and do not inject access errors
  into the producer. END_SEND checks completed generator/coroutine return values
  in the receiving frame, including before STOP_ITERATION monitoring callbacks.
  END_FOR checks discarded generator completion values too, before monitoring.
  Inlined yield transfer checks values after returning to the receiving frame;
  Tier 2 uses a conservative pre-transfer guard with a Tier 1 fallback.
  Producer-side PY_YIELD/PY_RETURN monitoring still needs separate integration.

* Newly created dictionary views and mapping proxies inherit their mutable
  container's state/owner; wrappers of immutable mappings start local to their
  creating group. Forward/reverse list and dictionary iterators, and set
  iterators, use the same inheritance. Synchronized container iterators serialize
  next-position updates with the iterator and container locked together. List
  state restoration and dictionary/set copy/pickle snapshots also take the
  iterator lock. Propagation to existing wrappers when a container changes
  state remains pending.
* Native SynchronizedSet and `PySynchronizedSet_New` share set storage and
  internal locking. Exact local sets support `synchronize()`, moving storage
  and emptying the source without rehashing elements. Small-table relocation
  and deferred-reclamation flags reuse the existing set storage-swap helper.
  Synchronized sets can be frozen in place, retaining aliases and weakrefs.
  Subclasses and state changes of existing iterators remain pending.
* Native SynchronizedList and `PySynchronizedList_New` share the list layout
  and internal locking implementation. Construction, list operations, the C
  API, copy/pickle, and GC retain shallow element ownership. Exact local lists
  support `synchronize()`, moving their storage into a synchronized list and
  emptying the source. Sort-time transfer is rejected, and failed allocation
  leaves the source unchanged. Subclasses and state changes of existing
  iterators are still pending. Path configuration now creates `sys.path` as SynchronizedList,
  separately for each interpreter.
* Each interpreter creates its initial `sys.modules` as SynchronizedDict.
  Import-error cleanup, interpreter shutdown, and pickle's module search
  recognize its builtin layout while retaining generic mapping behavior for
  user replacements. Module objects stored in the registry keep their own
  ownership and shareable states; registry sharing does not share its values.
* Exact Python modules support `synchronize()`: their actual namespace is
  converted in place to SynchronizedDict before publishing synchronized
  module state. Existing namespace aliases and functions' global dictionaries
  retain identity. Native extension state and module subclasses require
  explicit support and are rejected. Module reinitialization serializes
  internal name updates. Thread/function namespace integration is described
  below; other native extension modules still need their own sharing policy.
* The initial `builtins` module, its actual namespace, and its native function
  entries are synchronized. The `threading` and `copy` Python modules opt into
  synchronized namespaces. The Shareable enum class and its members are frozen;
  querying the state returns an existing member without running Enum.__call__.
  Other modules
  retain their own ownership; synchronization is not transitive.
* Native `SynchronizedDict` construction, including `PySynchronizedDict_New`,
  uses the dict layout and its internally synchronized operations. Instances
  are dict subtypes with synchronized state and no group owner. Python
  construction, `fromkeys`, copying, pickle, and the dictionary C API are
  supported. Freezing converts the same object into a frozendict, including
  when requested by another group. This is a foundation, not full synchronized
  container support: remaining view/iterator state propagation,
  remaining object namespaces, and complete access checks remain pending.
  `dict.synchronize()` moves an exact local dictionary's storage into a new
  synchronized dictionary and empties the source, preserving element identity.
  Split instance namespaces are detached before moving. Foreign local sources,
  immutable/synchronized sources, and subclasses are rejected. Source watcher
  callbacks run before the move, followed by state/access revalidation.
* `PySequence_GetItem` and `PySequence_GetSlice` validate their results,
  including user-defined indexing callbacks. The checks are shallow and
  preserve callback exceptions while releasing rejected new references.
  `PyObject_GetItem` also checks mapping and `__class_getitem__` results,
  including callers of `PyMapping_GetItemString`. The exact-dictionary path
  of `PyMapping_GetOptionalItem` checks owned results too. Specialized builtin
  list/tuple integer subscriptions and dictionary subscriptions also check their
  results, including the dictionary known-hash uop. `PyDict_GetItemWithError`
  validates its result while holding a temporary strong reference, then returns
  the usual borrowed reference. `PyDict_GetItem` and its string-key variant
  reject inaccessible values through their existing unraisable-exception
  reporting and suppression path. The borrowed known-hash helper and its
  Unicode-key wrapper also validate results. Raw internal lookup and iteration
  paths still need separate checks.
  `PyDict_GetItemRef` and its string-key variant check owned results;
  optional mapping lookup now delegates to that common boundary.
  Inlined Python `__getitem__` calls validate their return values in both
  interpreter tiers, before exposing them to return-monitoring callbacks.
  Ordinary nested calls do not inherit this protocol-return check.
* Exact native method, classmethod, member, getset, and wrapper descriptors
  have immutable state. Their referenced objects retain their own states;
  this does not implement access checks for every descriptor field.
* Native primitive `Lock` instances are synchronized, including the locks
  used internally by Condition/Event to notify threads in other groups.
  Exact native `RLock` instances are synchronized too; subclass instances
  retain local ownership. Recursive-mutex counts use atomic reads/writes so
  cross-thread repr cannot race with the owner's count updates. Public
  protective and compound locks remain unimplemented; the held-ID registry
  and conservative with-local checks are only supporting foundations.
* `PyList_GetItem`, `PyList_GetItemRef`, and `PyTuple_GetItem` validate access
  to returned elements. Borrowed-reference rejection retains container
  ownership; strong-reference rejection releases only the acquired reference.
  Function creation and exception-group handling propagate the new possible
  failures instead of dereferencing NULL.
  Additional NULL/error propagation checks cover resource limits, termios
  attributes, thread-local cleanup, decimal signal lists, and Windows COM
  argument extraction. The decimal and Windows-specific paths are not yet
  runtime-validated in this Linux environment.
* The seven `PyFunction_Get*` C APIs validate returned references against the
  current group's access permissions. Rejected borrowed references are not
  decrefed, and NULL results preserve existing exception/no-value semantics.
  Direct `FunctionType` construction accepts frozen global namespaces.
* Functions without modifiable captured bindings or cell bindings rebound by
  nested code are synchronized. VM cells record known nonlocal writes;
  read-only captures permit synchronized functions while acquired values
  retain their own access checks. Rebinding initialized cells or connecting
  writer code reclassifies existing readers with the world stopped. Mutable
  local captures determine the owning group; conflicting owners deny access.
  Publicly created CellType/PyCell_New cells remain conservatively mutable.
  Function code and closure replacement integrate with this classification.
  See Read-only binding implementation for validation and remaining limits.
* `threading.Thread` now uses a native internal base with synchronized
  dictionary storage, weakrefs, and GC traversal/clearing. Thread instances,
  main/dummy threads, and Python subclasses start synchronized. Opaque extra
  C subclass storage keeps the instance local. Namespace replacement through
  Python or the generic C API converts ordinary dictionaries in place.
* Exact native `threading.local` objects are synchronized, while each thread's
  attribute dictionary remains local to its group. Internal registry/storage
  and cleanup references are shared; private identity tokens are immutable.
  Python local subclasses retain local state.
* Native `_thread._ThreadHandle` wrappers are synchronized, with no group
  owner. Identifiers, completion, and concurrent joins already use the native
  handle's mutex/event/once machinery. The common allocation path covers
  unstarted handles, joinable-thread creation, and existing-thread wrappers.
  Event/Condition sharing remains pending.
* Native `threading.ThreadGroup` objects with optional names.
* `Thread(group=...)` and a read-only `Thread.group` property.
* Read-only `sys.main_thread_group`, preserving the ordinary module type of
  `sys`. Each interpreter has its own main group.
* Default selection of the main group, or a new group for each thread when
  `PYTHON_PARALLEL` is nonzero. `-E` and `-I` ignore the environment setting.
* Serialization of attached threads within each group, including threads
  created through the low-level C APIs (which default to the main group).
* Independent groups can execute concurrently in a free-threaded build with
  the GIL disabled. The ordinary build still uses its global interpreter lock.
* Release and reacquisition of a group's execution right when a thread detaches
  and reattaches, including blocking I/O and stop-the-world GC pauses.
* Scheduling requests at the interpreter's existing evaluation-breaker checks,
  fork reinitialization, and release of execution rights when a daemon is
  permanently parked during finalization.
* A 32-bit owner ID and 8-bit sharing state in the object header in both builds.
  Process-wide owner IDs are not recycled, including across interpreter
  reinitialization. Exhaustion fails allocation instead of wrapping.
* Allocation-time ownership of local objects and immutable states for exact
  immutable builtin values. Primitive subclasses remain local. Static
  allocation alone does not declare an extension object immutable.
* The read-only `__shareable__` attribute, `threading.Shareable`, and the two
  access exception classes.
* `PyObject_CheckAccess`, its internal checked-reference helper, and extension
  contracts `PyObject_DeclareImmutable` / `PyObject_DeclareSynchronized`.
  These declarations validate ownership; they do not implement locking or
  freezing for arbitrary extension objects.
* The builtin `freeze()` protocol: special-method lookup of `__freeze__`,
  ownership validation before calling user code, and propagation of errors.
  Immutable builtin values return themselves. Custom implementations may
  supply their own `__freeze__`.
* In-place freezing of Python instances whose MRO consists of Python-defined
  classes, `object`, and supported immutable builtin bases (`int`, `float`,
  `complex`, `str`, `bytes`, `tuple`, `frozenset`, and `frozendict`), including
  slots and managed or traditional instance
  dictionaries. Exact dictionaries become `frozendict` in place, preserving
  previously obtained aliases. Split storage detaches from shared keys and
  inline values. Dictionary subclasses still use a shallow snapshot and need
  a separate implementation to preserve their aliases.
  Attribute assignment/deletion, direct member-descriptor writes, `__dict__`
  replacement, `__class__` assignment, and specialized attribute stores reject
  writes. The generated tier-two instructions retain these checks too, but a
  JIT-enabled build has not yet been tested. A separate header flag prevents
  reentrant writes during preparation; allocation failure restores mutability.
  Other native bases still require their own freezing implementations.
* Shallow freezing of Python-defined classes with `@freeze`, including classes
  with Python-defined metaclasses. The namespace becomes a `frozendict` in place,
  including the public `__dict__` and `PyType_GetDict()` results. Existing
  dictionary aliases and existing mapping proxies retain the same namespace.
  Ordinary attribute writes and direct type/getset-descriptor writes are rejected.
  Bases, subclasses, and instances are not implicitly frozen. Method-cache
  invalidation preserves inherited-method and slot updates from mutable bases.
  Lazy annotations use a separate private cache rather than modifying the
  frozen namespace; annotations referring to the decorated class need not be
  evaluated before that class has been bound to its name.
* The `__module__` global is initialized to the module itself for Python and
  native module creation, and reset on explicit module reinitialization.
  Cached single-phase extension namespaces exclude the original self binding,
  so a new module retains its own identity when reusing the cache. Uninitialized
  shells produced by calling `ModuleType.__new__` directly still have no
  initialized namespace attributes.
* Exact Python module freezing preserves the module and namespace identities,
  including function globals and other dictionary aliases. Attribute changes
  and reinitialization are rejected; lazy annotations use a separate GC-visible
  cache. Extension modules and module subclasses require explicit support.
* Native `threading.TransferBox`: shallow copying of local inputs, temporary
  unowned state of the copy, and ownership assignment to the group calling
  `claim()`. A sink can restrict which group claims the value; a rejected claim
  leaves the box available. Sink changes and claims use the same object
  critical section, so only one caller can claim it. Immutable and synchronized inputs
  retain identity. Copies returning the original or an aliased local object
  are rejected. The type supports `TransferBox[T]` annotations.
* Immutable-state initialization when the compiler's set intrinsic changes a
  uniquely referenced set into a frozenset, as well as ordinary allocation.
* Exact builtin sets freeze in place to frozensets, preserving aliases,
  weakrefs, and existing iterators. Type publication stops other interpreter
  threads. Saved mutation methods reject writes, including no-op operations.
  Hash/comparison/iteration callbacks that freeze a set cause an in-flight
  mutation to stop before further writes or table swaps. GC clearing remains
  available for unreachable cycles. Explicitly frozen sets reject C API
  insertion even with a unique reference; constructing a fresh frozenset in C
  retains its existing filling API. Set subclasses still need their own
  implementation; generic mutable-native-base freezing remains pending.
* `dict` and `frozendict` use the same C struct and allocation size, as required
  by the appendix's collection design. The hash cache is part of
  `PyDictObject`, initialized by normal, split-copy, and subtype allocation
  paths; `PyFrozenDictObject` is an internal alias. Mutable dictionaries grow
  by a hash field and any required padding.
* `dict.__freeze__`, used by builtin `freeze`, preserves exact-dict identity,
  detaches split keys/embedded values, and publishes the frozendict type while
  interpreter threads are stopped. Allocation failure leaves the original usable.
  Assignment and `setdefault` check for freezing after hash/comparison
  callbacks, preserving callback exceptions and releasing consumed refs.
  Split-value insertion propagates failures to its callers. `fromkeys` checks
  freezing when a constructor supplies an existing destination, including
  iterator callbacks and the direct dict/set source paths. Saved reading
  methods remain usable; a saved mutable-dict `copy` returns a mutable copy.
  Dictionary subclasses still need their own freezing implementation.
* Dictionary deletion checks freezing after hashes, comparisons, conditional
  deletion predicates, and watcher callbacks. `pop` clears its C result output
  on error; saved `popitem` rejects frozen receivers and checks again after
  result-tuple allocation. Original callback exceptions are preserved.
* Bulk dictionary updates reject frozen receivers, including empty `update`,
  reinitialization, `|=`, and saved `clear`. Mapping/sequence callbacks are
  checked before writes, including empty iteration and existing-key merge
  comparisons with override disabled. A value finalizer that freezes the
  destination stops subsequent entries from being inserted. Fresh frozendict
  construction still uses its private, unfrozen construction state.
  `clear` and merge paths check again with the destination lock held, so a
  type transition while waiting to acquire the lock cannot bypass the check.
* Watched dictionary freezing invalidates the keys version and emits the new
  `PyDict_EVENT_FROZEN` after type publication and world restart. Existing
  watchers stay registered, and watch/unwatch accept frozendicts. Module and
  optimizer callbacks accept the shared layout. Clear and instance-attribute
  writes stop when callbacks freeze their destination; GC clearing reacquires
  the current storage after a callback detaches a split table. The specialized
  attribute store falls back for watched dictionaries because its raw mutex
  cannot be suspended for a reentrant critical section.
  Free-threaded unique-ID cleanup and builtins-reference helpers recognize
  frozendicts, preserving per-thread reference accounting after conversion.
* Dictionary GC clearing releases unreachable `frozendict` contents through
  an internal path. The public `PyDict_Clear` API still leaves frozendicts
  unchanged. A C fixture constructs a frozendict/tuple cycle that only the
  dictionary can break; regression tests check that the object actually
  disappears from GC tracking, since cleared weakrefs alone do not prove
  reclamation. Both Unicode-key and general-key tables are covered.
* `threading.Channel`: a frozen Python wrapper around a synchronized native
  FIFO of TransferBoxes. `put()` copies before taking the queue lock; `get()`
  removes one box and claims it, raising `IndexError` on an empty queue.
  Concurrent producers and consumers share the same critical section for
  linking and unlinking entries. The queue supports GC traversal and clears
  its links before releasing values that may run finalizers.
* Introspection support for the new states: `inspect.getattr_static()` reads
  frozen instance dictionaries without running descriptors; `pydoc` excludes
  the implicit module self binding from imported-module listings. Native
  ThreadGroup and TransferBox constructors expose text signatures.
* Function keyword-only defaults are exact `frozendict` snapshots, including
  compiler-created functions, `FunctionType`, Python attribute assignment,
  `PyFunction_SetKwDefaults`, and legacy `PyEval_EvalCodeEx` construction.
  Replacing the whole attribute remains supported. The snapshot is shallow
  and bypasses dictionary-subclass mapping overrides. The bytecode's failure
  path releases both inputs if snapshot allocation fails. `inspect` also
  accepts frozen defaults on function-like objects.
* Function execution-attribute mutation emits `DeprecationWarning`:
  Python assignments to `__code__`, `__defaults__`, and `__kwdefaults__`,
  deletion of defaults, and the C API setters for defaults and closures.
  Warnings promoted to errors leave the function unchanged and release
  temporary references. Function construction does not warn. `namedtuple`
  now supplies defaults through the function constructor. The existing
  more-specific warning for replacing code with a different generator/
  coroutine kind is retained without emitting a second warning.
  `types.coroutine` retains its established in-place identity contract and
  suppresses only the new warning from its internal code-flag update; warnings
  for ordinary user assignments remain enabled.
* Python setters for code/defaults/keyword-defaults and C API setters for
  defaults/keyword-defaults/closures reject foreign local functions and
  functions declared immutable. They recheck after warning, audit, and
  watcher callbacks, inside the stop-the-world mutation interval. The check
  itself does not allocate; failure resumes other threads before creating
  the exception. Name, qualified name, docstring, module, annotation,
  annotation-callback, and type-parameter setters also reject illegal sharing
  states, including the C annotation setter. Namespace synchronization, the
  void vectorcall setter, and complete function freezing remain pending.
  Initial attribute-dictionary creation for synchronized functions now uses
  SynchronizedDict, for both `__dict__` reads and ordinary attribute writes.
  Local functions retain ordinary dictionary creation. Explicit namespace
  replacement converts an exact ordinary dictionary in place for synchronized
  functions and preserves delayed release of the old dictionary. Code/closure
  changes that make a function synchronized convert its existing ordinary
  namespace before publishing the new state. Local transitions retain an
  already synchronized namespace. Dictionary subclasses remain unsupported
  for synchronization.

The scheduler lives in `Python/ceval_gil.c`. Its native state is independent of
the Python wrapper, so deleting a detached thread state cannot invoke a Python
finalizer. A separate small mutex protects the current holder pointer while a
waiting thread requests a context switch. Waiters remain detached, allowing GC
to stop the world.

The free-threaded build retains PEP 703 reference counting and container locks.
It does not yet optimize these mechanisms for ThreadGroup-local objects.

## Remaining for PEP conformance

The dated sections below are an implementation journal. Earlier statements that
closures, protective locks, compound locks or synchronized namespaces are wholly
unimplemented have been superseded by later entries. Their implementation and
targeted tests do not establish complete PEP conformance.

Current outstanding work includes:

* Complete the native acquisition audit, including receiver lifetime before
  reading a result. PyThreadState_GetFrame now pauses remote stack acquisition,
  rejects foreign groups outside debugger pauses and checks its returned frame.
  Its caller must keep the target thread state alive. No-frame/error preservation
  and current/remote materialization allocation failures now have regressions.
  Broader native lifetime coverage remains; the existing policy of clearing
  frame-materialization allocation errors is currently preserved.
* Audit every heap-to-thread VM acquisition, active evaluation-stack reference,
  and callback/state-transition boundary. The compiler's conservative with-local
  checks need the appendix's dataflow optimization and coverage of all escaping
  protected references. Passing call/return regression tests is insufficient.
* Complete special allocation/state initialization, group-aware reference
  counting and reclamation, owner-affinity for native/weakref callbacks, and
  Main lifetime through final interpreter teardown. Deferred GC/non-GC
  finalizers and ordinary deallocation now use an allocation-free intrusive
  cleanup queue, retaining non-GC explicit-call counts. The current separate
  GIL/free-threading configurations
  do not yet constitute the unified implementation proposed by the PEP.
* Finish mutation and state-transition audits for functions, class/module
  subclasses, native-base instances, derived objects and existing aliases.
  Read-only closure classification, cell provenance and reflective invalidation
  now have implementations and tests; their broader transition/lifetime audit
  remains necessary. Native protection layouts and Python fallback lock behavior
  also remain outstanding.
* Verify scheduling/no-switch guarantees, synchronization of shared operations,
  interpreter/fork/shutdown integration, and complete public API/ABI coverage.
* Complete debug validation of stack-reference safety, native machine-code JIT,
  remaining platform configurations, and the appendix's performance work.
  TraceRefs/Tier 2 now has added-test and focused lifecycle coverage on aarch64;
  this is not a full CPython-wide or cross-platform validation.

The 49 added test files pass in all four configurations: 683 tests,
with two FT skips, eleven GIL/Tier 2 skips, and ten TraceRefs/Tier 2 skips,
using --fail-env-changed. The ordinary
LOCAL and protected-object finalizer regressions both pass without the earlier
unraisable cleanup exception. The SQLite fixture setup error is also corrected.
See the latest validation entry below for logs and scope. This is not a complete
C API audit, a clean CPython-wide suite, a portability result, or proof of race
freedom. The APIs remain experimental until the remaining gaps are resolved.

## Validation

Build with `./configure --with-pydebug --disable-gil` and `make -j2`, then run:

```sh
./python -m test test_threadgroup test_threading test_thread
./python -X gil=1 -m test test_threadgroup test_threading test_thread
./python -m test test_threadgroup -R 3:3
```

The parallel-execution test uses a C barrier that neither detaches its thread
state nor evaluates Python bytecode. Two threads must meet there while both
retain their groups' execution rights; simple Python-level interleaving cannot
satisfy this test. Other tests cover group identity, serialization, blocking
waits, exceptions, GC, group reuse, fork, and environment handling.

Use a separate source/build directory for testing the ordinary GIL build.
Do not rebuild an executable or its extension modules while its tests are
running, especially when those tests launch subprocesses.

The scheduler foundation was validated on Linux/aarch64 with debug builds
before the subsequent object-header changes:

* Free-threaded build: `test_threadgroup`, `test_threading`, `test_thread`,
  `test_capi`, `test_gc`, `test_sys`, `test_fork1`, and `test_embed` passed
  (2,104 tests, 358 skipped).
* Ordinary GIL build: `test_threadgroup`, `test_threading`, `test_thread`, and
  `test_sys` passed (398 tests, 12 skipped).
* Free-threaded build with `-X gil=1`: `test_threadgroup`, `test_threading`, and
  `test_thread` passed (296 tests, 6 skipped).
* The 15 ThreadGroup tests passed `-R 3:3`, including repeated creation and
  destruction of a subinterpreter. Interpreter shutdown reported
  `0 refs, 0 blocks` with `-X showrefcount`.

After the object-header changes, the focused suite (`test_shareable`,
`test_threadgroup`, `test_types`, `test_module`, `test_enum`, `test_sys`, and
`test_exceptions`) passed in both debug builds: 1,509 tests, 19 skipped in
the free-threaded build and 20 skipped in the ordinary build. The free-threaded
C API, GC, threading, low-level thread, embedding, and ctypes regression suite
passed 2,611 tests (404 skipped). `test_shareable` and `test_threadgroup` also
passed reference-leak checks with `-R 3:3` (25 tests).

After adding the freezing protocol and correcting set-intrinsic metadata,
`test_freeze`, `test_shareable`, `test_threadgroup`, `test_builtin`, `test_types`,
`test_module`, `test_set`, `test_compile`, and `test_marshal` passed in both
builds (1,275 tests; 17 skipped free-threaded, 18 skipped ordinary).
The 34 tests in `test_freeze`, `test_shareable`, and `test_threadgroup` also
passed `-R 3:3` in the free-threaded build.

After implementing ordinary instance freezing:

* Free-threaded debug: `test_freeze`, `test_shareable`, `test_threadgroup`,
  `test_types`, `test_module`, `test_sys`, `test_descr`, `test_capi`, and `test_gc`
  passed (2,097 tests, 348 skipped).
* Allocation-failure injection exposed an invalid assertion in managed-dict
  replacement. After fixing it and adding C API, cycle collection, and rollback
  tests, `test_freeze`, `test_dict`, `test_descr`, and `test_gc` passed
  (389 tests, 7 skipped).
* Ordinary debug: the same nine-file suite plus `test_dict`, including all new
  failure-path tests, passed (2,240 tests, 325 skipped).
* The 43 tests in `test_freeze`, `test_shareable`, and `test_threadgroup` passed
  `-R 3:3` in the free-threaded build.

Immutable builtin subclass freezing was subsequently verified for the eight
supported bases, including additional attributes, slots, dictionary aliases,
and cycles through frozen dictionaries. The ordinary-build suite covering
`test_freeze`, `test_long`, `test_float`, `test_complex`, `test_str`, `test_bytes`,
`test_tuple`, `test_set`, and `test_dict` passed (1,459 tests, 15 skipped).
The corresponding seven builtin suites passed in the free-threaded build
(1,296 tests, 12 skipped), followed by `test_freeze` and `test_dict` (163 tests).
After adding the subclass cycle test, all 22 freezing tests passed `-R 3:3`
in both builds. The free-threaded run reported one memory block within the
test runner's accepted tolerance; the ordinary run reported none.

Python class freezing was validated with `test_freeze`, `test_types`,
`test_descr`, `test_super`, `test_annotationlib`, `test_type_annotations`,
`test_type_cache`, `test_genericclass`, `test_typing`, `test_gc`, and `test_capi`:
2,944 tests (341 skipped) in the free-threaded debug build, and 2,942 tests
(317 skipped) in the ordinary debug build. After changing the public frozen
class `__dict__` result to a `frozendict`, the focused six-file free-threaded
suite also passed (546 tests, 1 skipped). All 33 freezing tests passed `-R 3:3`
in the free-threaded build, with one memory block within the runner's accepted
tolerance. Tests cover mutable bases, slot inheritance, metaclasses, retained
dictionary aliases, annotation-cache cycles, and allocation-failure rollback.

Module self references were verified with module, import, importlib, GC,
embedding, and C API regression tests. The ordinary debug build passed all
six files (3,089 tests, 344 skipped). In the free-threaded build, the five
non-importlib files passed; the four importlib failures were namespace
expectations, updated to assert the new self binding explicitly. The importlib
rerun passed (1,219 tests, 20 skipped). The extension-cache identity test now
runs in a fresh main interpreter, enabling the GIL in free-threaded builds
because its test extension requires it. All 42 module tests passed `-R 3:3`
in both builds, including that subprocess test. This adds the binding needed
by `freeze(__module__)`; module freezing itself is still pending.

The TransferBox implementation passed `test_transferbox`, `test_threadgroup`,
`test_threading`, `test_thread`, `test_copy`, `test_gc`, and `test_embed` in both
debug builds (547 tests; 17 skipped free-threaded, 12 skipped ordinary).
After adding synchronized sink updates, `test_transferbox` and `test_threadgroup`
passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (31 tests, with one ordinary-build skip).
The 16 TransferBox tests cover shallow copying, identity of shareable values,
group ownership at claim, rejected source access, destination validation,
one winner among eight parallel claimants, custom copy failures, aliases,
generic aliases, abandoned values, and GC cycles. As with the other foundation
tests, these do not establish complete VM access control or finalizer affinity.

The Channel implementation passed the same seven regression files plus
`test_channel` in both debug builds (559 tests; 17 skipped free-threaded,
12 skipped ordinary). The 43 Channel, TransferBox, and ThreadGroup tests also
passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (one ordinary-build skip). The 12 Channel tests
cover FIFO/empty behavior, frozen wrapper semantics, shallow copying, identity
of shareable values, copy exceptions and reentrancy, allocation-failure
rollback, cycles, rejected foreign inputs, and concurrent producers/consumers.
Cross-group tests use Channels to return results instead of mutating a shared
local results list. These tests still exercise ownership explicitly through
the C access predicate until general VM checks are connected.

Additional introspection regressions exposed missing constructor signatures,
the implicit module self binding in HTML documentation, and a `dict.get`
assumption in static attribute inspection of frozen instances. These were
fixed, with the existing exact pydoc expectations retained (its allmethods
fixture now excludes the new non-method `__shareable__` descriptor).
`test_channel`, `test_transferbox`, `test_threadgroup`, `test_inspect`, and
`test_pydoc` then passed in both debug builds (549 tests; one ordinary-build
skip). Static inspection explicitly tests frozen classes and instances while
ensuring properties are not invoked.

Keyword-only defaults were checked against function attributes, C API function
and legacy evaluation calls, function watchers, types, inspection, call binding,
opcode specialization, disassembly, peephole optimization, typing, GC, and
dictionary regressions. Those 13 files passed in both debug builds (2,147
tests; 9 skipped free-threaded, 4 skipped ordinary). The new subclass tests
exposed that the old internal dictionary-copy fallback invoked mapping hooks.
`_PyDict_CopyAsDict` now copies subclass storage directly, and instance freezing
uses that helper too. The raw key-table clone no longer requires a default
iterator slot; its dictionary/layout/locking checks remain in place. Public
dictionary-copy protocol behavior is unchanged.

After fixing that path, `test_kwdefaults`, `test_freeze`, and `test_dict` passed
`-R 3:3` in both builds (184 tests). The freezing suite reported one memory
block within the runner's accepted tolerance in each build. The nine keyword
default tests include construction, shallow values, retained aliases,
replacement after specialization, dictionary and frozendict subclasses,
C API setters/evaluation, cycles, and allocation-failure cleanup/rollback.
The 12 free-threading function-attribute stress tests also passed in the
free-threaded build; that module skips the ordinary build.

Function-mutation deprecations passed the keyword-default, function-attribute,
C API function/watcher, typing, and collections tests in both debug builds
(989 tests; 1 skipped, including the first six new mutation tests). The new
warnings initially exposed internal mutation in `namedtuple`: after migrating
it to constructor defaults, inspection, collections, and mutation tests passed
in both builds (512 tests; 1 skipped). The types/asyncio regression run had one
failure among 3,039 tests in each build: `types.coroutine` leaked the new warning
into an asyncio subprocess's stderr. After confining suppression to that
decorator's internal update, mutation, types, and eager-task-factory tests
passed in both builds (192 tests). All eight final mutation tests also passed
`-R 3:3` in both builds. They verify warning locations, unchanged attributes on
warning-as-error, invalid-input errors, silent construction, C API setters,
namedtuple defaults, and coroutine decorator identity with warning isolation.

Builtin set freezing passed `test_freeze_set`, `test_set`, `test_capi.test_set`,
`test_freeze`, `test_gc`, and `test_weakref` in both debug builds (906 tests;
10 skipped free-threaded, 5 skipped ordinary). Compiler and peephole regressions
also passed in both builds (331 tests; 2 skipped). After adding preservation of
exceptions raised by comparison callbacks, the 12 set-freezing tests and
18 C API set tests passed `-R 3:3` in both builds. Coverage includes saved bound
mutators and in-place operators, alias identity, hashes, weakrefs, existing
iterators, reentrant hash/equality/iteration, finalizers during reinitialization,
cyclic GC, rejected foreign freezing, and unique-reference C API insertion.
The original native-base rejection test remains in place for set subclasses.

The shared dictionary layout passed dictionary, views, defaultdict,
OrderedDict, size introspection, C API dictionary, GC, freezing, and
keyword-default regressions in both debug builds (708 tests; 13 skipped
free-threaded, 8 skipped ordinary). Dictionary and C API tests also passed
`-R 3:3` in both builds (175 tests).

The frozendict/tuple cycle test reproduced retained garbage before the GC
clearing fix. After the fix, dictionary, C API dictionary, GC, freezing,
keyword-default, size introspection, and OrderedDict tests passed in both
debug builds (680 tests; 13 skipped free-threaded, 8 skipped ordinary).
Dictionary and C API tests then passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (176 tests;
one memory block within the runner's accepted tolerance in `test_dict`).
Existing C API tests verify that public clearing still leaves frozendicts
unchanged. At that stage, publishing `dict.__freeze__` still required mutator
reentrancy, watchers, split-table detachment, and specialized global/attribute
accesses; subsequent implementation and validation are recorded below.

The internal in-place freezing primitive and assignment checks passed
`test_freeze_dict`, `test_dict`, `test_capi.test_dict`,
`test_capi.test_watchers`, `test_gc`, `test_freeze`, and `test_kwdefaults`
in both debug builds (350 tests; 6 skipped free-threaded, 1 skipped ordinary).
The 11 internal freezing tests and 33 C API dictionary tests also passed
`-R 3:3` in both builds. Coverage includes identity/hash/state, existing
iterators and views, embedded and separately allocated split tables,
foreign-group rejection, temporary watched-dictionary rejection, saved
assignment, reentrant hash/equality, callback exception preservation,
self-cycles, and split-conversion allocation-failure rollback. These checks
do not yet establish safety of all dictionary mutation paths, and the
internal primitive is not connected to `freeze()` or object/module freezing.

Deletion-path checks passed the internal freezing, dictionary, C API
dictionary/watcher, and GC suites in both debug builds (312 tests;
6 skipped free-threaded, 1 skipped ordinary). After adding explicit C API
reentrancy coverage, the 16 internal freezing tests and 33 C API dictionary
tests passed `-R 3:3` in both builds. Added cases cover saved deletion methods
on empty/nonempty dictionaries, hash and equal/unequal comparison callbacks,
conditional deletion predicates, callback errors, and C `PyDict_Pop` with
both a result pointer and a NULL result pointer. GC-triggered freezing during
`popitem` allocation still needs dedicated coverage. Watcher-triggered
freezing was not yet available at that stage; subsequent coverage is
recorded below.

Bulk-update checks passed the internal freezing, dictionary, C API
dictionary/watcher, and GC suites in both debug builds (318 tests;
6 skipped free-threaded, 1 skipped ordinary). After moving the clear/merge
checks inside their critical sections, the 21 internal freezing tests,
dictionary tests, and C API dictionary tests passed `-R 3:3` in both builds
(197 tests; one memory block within the runner's accepted tolerance in
`test_freeze_dict`). Added cases exercise empty saved bulk mutators, sequence
and mapping callbacks (including empty results and original exceptions),
freezing from a replaced value's finalizer, and equal-key callbacks during
override-disabled C merges using both concrete and generic mappings.

Watched freezing exposed three additional regressions. A watcher called from
the specialized attribute store deadlocked on its raw mutex; watched stores
now deoptimize to the normal critical-section path. Free-threaded module
dictionary reclamation left a unique-ID pool entry behind after the type
change, causing a crash after otherwise successful tests; unique-ID cleanup
and builtins reference helpers now accept frozendicts. Repeated module
attribute access then exposed an exact-dict assertion in the specialization
lookup helper; that read-only helper now accepts the shared layout too.

After the locking and reference-accounting fixes, internal freezing,
dictionary, C API dictionary/watcher, GC, opcode-cache, and module suites
passed in both debug builds (449 tests; 6 skipped free-threaded, 1 skipped
ordinary). After the final specialization fix and strengthening the module
access test, internal freezing and C API dictionary/watcher suites passed
`-R 3:3` in both builds (121 tests; one memory block within the runner's
accepted tolerance in `test_freeze_dict`). The 27 internal freezing tests cover
freeze-event identity and idempotence, watcher errors, continued GC/deallocation
events, freezing during combined/split mutations and optimized attribute
stores, warmed module/global access, nested function creation, and reclamation
of frozen builtins used by functions created before and after conversion.
At that stage, the public protocol was not yet connected. JIT-enabled
execution remains untested.

The public dictionary freezing protocol passed `test_freeze_dict`,
`test_freeze`, dictionary and C API dictionary/watcher suites, GC,
opcode-cache, module, pydoc, and inspection tests in both debug builds
(993 tests; 6 skipped free-threaded, 1 skipped ordinary). Public freezing,
dictionary, and C API dictionary tests also passed `-R 3:3` in both builds
(208 tests; one memory block within the runner's accepted tolerance in
`test_freeze_dict`). All 32 dictionary-freezing tests now use builtin
`freeze`, including allocation-failure subprocesses. New cases cover the
native method and saved-method idempotence, shallow unhashable values,
saved readers/copy/reverse iteration, and `fromkeys` constructors returning
an existing destination that is frozen by iteration or watcher callbacks.
Module freezing and integration with instance/class namespace aliases remain
separate unfinished work, as do dictionary subclasses and JIT execution.

Exact Python module freezing is now connected to `freeze(__module__)` and
preserves the actual namespace, including existing function globals. The
module, annotation, and sys suites passed in the free-threaded debug build
(277 tests; 7 skipped). Freezing, module, importlib, pydoc, inspect, and GC
regressions passed there too (1,906 tests; 26 skipped). The combined suites
passed in the ordinary debug build (2,128 tests; 27 skipped).

After adding initialization-time annotation, reentrant annotation-freezing,
and namespace-watcher coverage, all 16 module-freezing tests and 42 existing
module tests passed `-R 3:3` in both debug builds. Each run reported one memory
block within the runner's accepted tolerance. Tests also cover namespace and
function-global identity, saved mutators, shallow values, actual source-file
imports, delayed annotation errors and retries, GC cycles, unsupported
extensions/subclasses, explicitly declared immutable extensions, and
foreign-group rejection. Extension/subclass freezing, lazy-import resolution
in frozen namespaces, and instance/class namespace alias integration still
need further implementation and validation.

Instance freezing now freezes exact namespace dictionaries in place instead
of replacing them with snapshots. Existing aliases, views, and iterators
retain their namespace. Tests cover managed and traditional dictionaries,
immutable-builtin subclasses, already-frozen namespaces, independent instances
sharing keys, and two instances sharing the same dictionary after warmed
attribute stores. Dictionary subclasses still use the previous snapshot
fallback; preserving their aliases and class namespace aliases remains work.

Freezing, dictionary freezing, descriptor, opcode-cache, GC, and inspection
suites passed in both debug builds (765 tests; 7 skipped free-threaded,
2 skipped ordinary). This broader run found a module `dir()` test whose list
of default attributes omitted the new `__module__` binding; it now includes
that attribute and verifies its identity. The 36 general freezing tests and
32 dictionary-freezing tests passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (one memory block
per test file within the runner's accepted tolerance). Allocation-failure
tests continue to verify rollback to a writable local instance.

Python class freezing now preserves namespace identity too. It installs the
private annotation cache before freezing the actual dictionary, allowing
dictionary freeze notifications to read lazy annotations safely. The type
lock is released before dictionary freezing invokes callbacks. Allocation
failure restores the previous cache and the class's writable local state.
Existing C API dictionary references, mapping proxies, views, and iterators
continue to refer to the same namespace. Freezing during annotation evaluation
also caches the result in the new private cache.

Freezing, dictionary freezing, descriptor, type-cache, opcode-cache,
annotationlib, and GC tests passed in both debug builds (524 tests;
7 skipped free-threaded, 2 skipped ordinary). Tests include a failing
dictionary watcher whose unraisable-exception hook reads annotations and
attempts attribute mutation and recursive freezing during the transition.
General freezing and type-cache tests passed `-R 3:3` in both builds
(61 tests; one memory block within accepted tolerance in `test_freeze`).
Dictionary-subclass namespace aliases, native storage support, and JIT
coverage remain incomplete.

Initial function classification passed function-state, mutation-deprecation,
keyword-default, function-attribute, C API function/watcher, TransferBox, and
Channel suites in both debug builds (166 tests). Shareable, ThreadGroup, types,
and GC suites passed too (222 tests; 6 skipped free-threaded, 2 ordinary).
The free-threaded regression command additionally named a nonexistent
`test_funcobject` module; the intended `test_free_threading.test_functions`
suite was then run separately and passed all 12 stress tests. Function-state,
mutation-deprecation, keyword-default, and C API function suites passed
`-R 3:3` in both builds (38 tests).

The eight new function-state tests cover ordinary/generator/async/method
construction, explicit FunctionType construction, mutable closure owners and
readers remaining local, code/closure replacement, warning-as-error rollback,
explicit cross-group access checks, and identity-preserving transfer. These
tests do not validate ordinary VM access enforcement, read-only closure
classification, or safe calling through stale references after reclassification.

Execution-attribute mutation guards passed function mutation/state,
keyword-default, function-attribute, and C API function/watcher suites in both
debug builds (142 tests), plus all 12 free-threaded function stress tests.
Mutation/state and C API function tests passed `-R 3:3` in both builds
(33 tests). New test matrices exercise all six guarded setter paths against
immutable functions, foreign local functions, warning callbacks that declare
the target immutable, and modification watchers that do the same. Rejected
updates preserve the original attributes and release temporary references.

Function C API return checks and frozen-globals construction passed function
state/mutation, C API function, function-attribute, keyword-default, dictionary
and module freezing, and types suites in both debug builds (268 tests).
Function-state and C API function suites passed `-R 3:3` in both builds
(24 tests). Added cases repeatedly reject foreign local globals, module
metadata, and annotations without consuming borrowed references; allow
immutable results and missing attributes; preserve annotation callback
errors; and construct and execute a function against a frozen namespace.
This covers those C entry points only: function attribute descriptors,
unchecked internal macros, other C APIs, and VM loads remain to be connected
to access validation.

Public list/tuple element access checks passed sequence-access, C API
list/tuple, list/tuple, function-attribute, exception-group, and ThreadGroup
tests in both debug builds (258 tests; one skip in the ordinary build).
Format, importlib, and GC integration suites also passed (1,298 tests;
26 skipped free-threaded, 20 ordinary). Sequence-access and C API list/tuple
suites passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (41 tests).

New tests cover repeated foreign-element rejection and collection after
container release, immutable/synchronized elements, same-group identity,
index errors, and a code object whose docstring slot contains a foreign local
object. The latter exercises function-construction failure cleanup. Internal
unchecked helpers/macros and specialized indexing still bypass access checks;
the remaining public API call sites and extension callers need continued
error-propagation auditing. These results do not establish general VM safety.

The extension caller audit added missing NULL checks in resource limit
conversion, termios scalar/control-character conversion, thread-local weakref
cleanup, and decimal signal conversion. Windows COM argument extraction now
translates only the original missing-argument IndexError, preserving access
errors. Existing sqlite, zoneinfo, and datetime getter calls already check
failure; syslog's optional argv-derived label intentionally falls back when
retrieval fails.

Sequence-access, resource, termios, thread-local, ctypes, zoneinfo, and datetime
suites passed in both debug builds (2,135 tests; 117 skipped free-threaded,
118 ordinary). Sequence-access, resource, termios, and thread-local suites
passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (64 tests; one skipped). Added runtime cases
exercise both resource-limit tuple positions and all three termios extraction
paths while verifying that limits and terminal attributes remain unchanged.
The decimal case is included but skipped because `_decimal` is unavailable;
the COM branch still requires Windows validation. This audit does not cover
all extension APIs or establish access safety of thread-local reclamation.

Generic sequence access initially exposed a ThreadGroup startup deadlock:
Condition's deque indexing now checked its waiter lock, but that native lock
was incorrectly marked local. The child failed before setting its startup
event, leaving the parent waiting. A stack dump identified the failing
notification. Primitive native locks now initialize as synchronized; tests
verify metadata, uncopied transfer, and access/release from another group.
RLock classification and protective-lock behavior remain separate work.

After that fix, generic sequence access, Shareable, ThreadGroup, abstract C
API, iterator, enumerate/reversed, descriptor, and array tests passed in both
debug builds (1,468 tests; 2 skipped free-threaded, 3 ordinary). Native-thread
and threading suites passed too (281 tests; 5 skipped free-threaded,
4 ordinary). Sequence access, Shareable, and abstract C API tests passed
`-R 3:3` in both builds (74 tests; one skipped). Coverage includes native and
Python-defined indexing, negative indices, callback exception preservation,
foreign custom slice results, and accessible fresh slices containing
inaccessible elements. Specialized indexing and other VM/reference-return
paths are still incomplete.

Exact native RLock instances now initialize as synchronized; Python subclasses
remain local. The recursive-mutex depth field uses relaxed atomic reads and
writes in acquire/release, Condition save/restore, recursion introspection, and
repr. Previously repr could read the owner's concurrently modified depth using
a non-atomic access. The owner still serializes updates through the recursive
mutex; repr is an informational snapshot, not an atomic snapshot of all fields.

Shareable, ThreadGroup, native-thread, threading, and sequence-access suites
passed in both debug builds (320 tests; 6 skipped in each). Shareable and
ThreadGroup suites passed `-R 3:3` (30 tests; one skipped in the ordinary
build). Five internal mutex tests (basic, recursive, two-thread, counter,
and slow counter) also passed in both builds. New coverage checks exact versus
subclass metadata, uncopied transfer, group handoff with recursive ownership,
foreign-thread release rejection, and concurrent repr while saving/restoring
recursive state. ThreadSanitizer and non-Linux/other-architecture validation
remain pending, along with protective/compound lock semantics.

An exploratory `PyObject_GetItem` result check exposed another ThreadGroup
prerequisite: `threading.current_thread()` retrieves a Thread object owned by
its creating group. The general mapping-result check was reverted; Thread
state and its actual namespace must support sharing before enabling it.
The new native `SynchronizedDict` is the first prerequisite for that work.
It reuses dict storage/locking and supports shallow construction, the C API,
copy/pickle, cycle collection, parallel updates, and in-place freezing.
Views/iterators still lack the PEP state propagation, and Thread/function/module
namespace integration and `dict.synchronize()` remain unimplemented.

The initial dictionary regression run exposed a pre-existing regression from
keyword-default snapshotting: `_PyDict_CopyAsDict` had been changed to bypass
subclass hooks, which made class creation lose an OrderedDict's explicit
order. Raw storage copying is now a separate `_PyDict_CopyStorage` helper used
only for keyword-default snapshots. Other callers retain their original
subclass behavior. The existing `test_builtin.test_namespace_order` and
keyword-default subclass-hook tests cover both requirements.

After fixing the copy regression and adding a native five-item pickle reduction
(for protocols 0 and 1 as well as newer protocols), synchronized/frozen dict,
dict C API/watchers, ThreadGroup, sys, builtins, keyword defaults, function
sharing/attributes, types, and ElementTree suites passed in both debug builds:
1,020 tests, with 21 skipped free-threaded and 22 skipped ordinary. Synchronized
dict, frozen dict, and keyword-default suites also passed `-R 3:3` in both
builds (63 tests), with no reported reference or allocation leaks. New tests
include recursive pickle round trips, retained aliases and bound mutators
across freezing, freezing from another group, and four-group concurrent
`setdefault`/insert/pop operations. General heap-reference checks remain
incomplete, so these results do not establish PEP-wide race freedom.

`dict.synchronize()` now moves exact local dictionary contents into a newly
allocated SynchronizedDict. Source aliases and views observe an empty dict;
references among the moved contents remain shallow (including references back
to the original dict). Detaching split storage preserves instance namespace
identity while removing its attributes. Watchers remain attached to the source
and receive a clear event before the move; subsequent state/access validation
rejects a source frozen by a callback. A shared storage lifetime flag follows
the moved table for free-threaded deferred reclamation. Moving storage invokes
neither key hashing nor equality callbacks.

Dictionary synchronization/freeze, dict, dict C API/watchers, descriptors, and
types suites passed in both debug builds (605 tests; one skipped each).
Synchronized/frozen dict suites passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (64 tests), with
no reported leaks. Tests cover foreign-group rejection, allocation failure,
inline/split instance namespaces, warmed attribute/global caches, recursive
source references, shallow payload identity, and watcher ownership/reentrancy.
Subclasses and general synchronized object namespaces remain pending; this
step does not enable the deferred general `PyObject_GetItem` access check.

Python modules now support `module.synchronize()`, including the intended
`__module__.synchronize()` idiom during import. The module's actual namespace
changes type in place, retaining aliases and function-global identity. A
private dictionary helper detaches split storage if needed, invalidates its
keys version, and publishes synchronized state with the world stopped; it
revalidates the source after blocking. The module temporarily guards against
class changes/reinitialization and releases that guard on failure. Exact
modules with native extension state, module subclasses, foreign local modules,
and already synchronized/frozen modules are rejected. Reinitialization now
protects module-internal name updates with the module critical section.

Parallel reinitialization tests exposed a specialization assertion: the
module-attribute lookup path already holds the dictionary lock, but its raw
lookup helper accepted only exact dict/frozendict objects. It now also accepts
exact SynchronizedDict storage. The existing specialized module-load path
uses the same storage layout, keys-version guards, and free-threaded atomic
reference acquisition. This fixes that assertion without adding or claiming
the still-pending general VM access checks.

Module synchronization/freezing, synchronized dictionaries, module, importlib,
types, and C API watcher suites passed in both debug builds (1,517 tests;
20 skipped free-threaded and 19 ordinary). Module synchronization/freezing and
synchronized dictionaries passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (59 tests), with no
reported leaks. Coverage includes import-time synchronization, alias identity,
retained bound setters, shallow payload ownership, warmed global/attribute
loads, GC cycles, rejection/rollback, later freezing, and four-group attribute
updates and reinitialization. Thread/function namespaces, sys.modules/sys.path,
view/iterator states, and general VM/C API access validation remain pending.

The initial `sys.modules` registry is now a SynchronizedDict in every
interpreter. Import failure removal, interpreter shutdown traversal/clearing,
and pickle's module search recognize the exact synchronized type as native
dictionary storage. Custom replacement mappings retain their generic mapping
paths. New sys tests check registry state/owner and transfer identity,
subinterpreter isolation, and four-group insertion/removal of frozen modules.
The registry conversion does not change the sharing states of stored modules.

The broader pickle run also found an older test expectation that treated the
two PEP 805 exceptions as exceptions available in Python 2. They now join the
existing list of Python-3-only exceptions in the legacy mapping test. A new
test verifies the normal builtin-module name mapping and round trips both
exceptions, retaining type and arguments, through every pickle protocol.

Sys, importlib, import, import C API, module synchronization, ThreadGroup, and
embedding suites passed in both debug builds. After the exception-test update,
the full pickle suite passed in both (1,085 tests; 57 skipped). Together the
eight suites cover 2,674 tests (105 skipped free-threaded; 93 ordinary).
The three new registry tests also passed `-R 3:3` in both builds, with no
reported leaks. Embedding test executables were relinked with the changed
runtime before testing. `sys.path`, Thread/function namespace synchronization,
and general VM access validation still remain to be implemented.

SynchronizedList is now a native builtin list subtype, with the same storage
layout and list C API compatibility. Its C constructor accepts a size and,
like PyList_New, requires callers to initialize every NULL element before
publication. Python construction, list mutations, shallow copy, recursive
pickle (all protocols), GC, and four-group append are covered. The type is
currently exact-only; inherited iterators still need PEP state propagation.

Path configuration now creates sys.path as SynchronizedList. Tests verify
normal and `-S` startup, owner/state/transfer identity, and subinterpreter path
isolation. List/sys, list C API, sequence-access, importlib, site, and embedding
suites passed in both debug builds (1,619 tests; 40 skipped free-threaded,
39 ordinary).

`list.synchronize()` subsequently added shallow storage transfer into a new
SynchronizedList, emptying the original list while preserving element identity.
It retains the storage's deferred-reclamation flag in free-threaded builds.
It rejects foreign local sources, non-local sharing states, subclasses, and
sort-time transfer. Allocation failures preserve the source. Tests include
aliases/iterators, references back to the source, sort callbacks, C-declared
sharing states, foreign groups, and allocation-failure injection. The updated
list/synchronized-list/C API/sequence suites passed in both builds (112 tests;
one skipped each). This does not complete synchronized-container semantics or
general VM access enforcement.

The synchronized-list tests plus the new sys.path and subinterpreter checks
passed `-R 3:3` in both debug builds (14 tests), with no reported leaks.
Remaining work includes SynchronizedSet, iterator state propagation,
Thread/function namespaces, protective locks, and general VM/C API checks.

SynchronizedSet and its public C constructor now reuse set layout and locking.
Python construction, set algebra and mutation, the set C API, shallow copy,
recursive pickle (all protocols), cycles, and weakrefs are supported. The
current type is exact-only. Public freeze accepts it and retags the same object
as frozenset, retaining references and weakrefs; inherited mutation guards
reject mutations when a hash callback freezes the set.

`set.synchronize()` reuses the native storage-swap helper to transfer both
inline and allocated tables into an initially empty SynchronizedSet. The
source remains an ordinary empty set. No element is rehashed, weakrefs remain
bound to their original containers, and shared-storage lifetime flags follow
the tables. State/access validation is repeated after allocation and locking.
Tests cover empty/small/large tables, aliases/iterators, shallow element
identity, C-declared states, foreign sources, and allocation failure.

Synchronized/frozen set, set, set C API, builtins, and sys suites passed in
both debug builds (950 tests; 15 skipped each). Synchronized/frozen set and
set C API suites passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (43 tests), with no reported
leaks. Tests also exercise foreign-group freezing and four-group add/remove
operations. All three synchronized builtin container foundations now exist,
but subclass policies, iterator state propagation, Thread/function namespaces,
protective locks, and general VM/C API access enforcement remain incomplete.

New dictionary views, mapping proxies, and forward/reverse list iterators now
inherit their container's mutable sharing state and owner. Wrappers of an
immutable container instead initialize local to the creating group. The helper
reads metadata with the container lock held, before publishing the wrapper.
This is creation-time inheritance; updating pre-existing wrappers after their
container changes state remains pending.

Synchronized list iterators acquire both iterator and list locks before reading
an item and advancing their position. Acquiring these separately could suspend
the first critical section and let another next() reuse the position. Position
restoration also takes the iterator lock. Existing explicit list iterators use
the generic iteration slot rather than the direct-list FOR_ITER fast path, so
both C consumers and Python loops reach the synchronized next implementation.

Derived-state, synchronized-list, dictionary-view, iterator, types, ThreadGroup,
threading, and module freeze/synchronization suites passed in both debug builds
(526 tests; five skipped each). Derived-state, dictionary-view, and synchronized
list suites passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (35 tests), with no reported leaks.
Coverage includes creator-local views of frozen mappings, proxy/view alias
updates, copy/pickle/state restoration, mixed Python-loop/C consumption of one
iterator from four groups without duplicate/missing items, and concurrent
position resets. Dict/set iterator synchronization and all heap-reference
access checks remain incomplete; this does not establish general race freedom.

Set iterators now inherit the source's sharing state at creation. Their next
operation first retains the source under the iterator lock, then acquires both
locks together and rechecks exhaustion before scanning and advancing. This
keeps the set alive if another consumer exhausts the iterator while the pair
of locks is being acquired. References are released outside the critical
section. Length hints and reduction snapshots take the iterator lock; reduce
uses a separately allocated iterator instead of copying an object header and
mutex to the C stack. Copy/pickle retain their existing list-iterator results.
Size-change errors remain sticky even if the original size is restored.

Derived-state, synchronized-set, set-freeze, set, iterator, pickle, and set C API
suites passed in both debug builds (1,850 tests; 57 skipped each). The first
three suites and set C API also passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (53 tests), with
no reported leaks. Tests include mixed C/Python consumption from four groups,
concurrent copying and length hints during consumption, exhausted copies,
all pickle protocols, and persistent size-change errors. Dictionary iterators,
updates to already-created wrappers, and general heap-reference access checks
remain pending.

Forward and reverse dictionary key/value/item iterators now inherit the
container's sharing state. The synchronized path retains the dictionary while
acquiring both locks, checks size and exhaustion, and commits the position and
length before releasing the locks. Size-change errors become sticky before
error allocation; exhaustion detaches the reference under lock but decrefs
outside it. Item results use fresh tuples allocated after releasing the locks,
so result recycling and finalizers cannot suspend an unfinished position
update. Ordinary local iterators retain their existing iteration paths.

Dictionary iterator construction snapshots size and reverse position under the
dictionary lock. Length hints and copy/pickle state snapshots acquire the
iterator lock. Reduction allocates an independent iterator header, mutex, and
item-result tuple instead of copying these from the original object. Copy and
pickle still reconstruct a list iterator over the remaining elements.

Derived-state, synchronized-dictionary, dictionary-freeze, dictionary, views,
iterator, pickle, and dictionary C API suites passed in both debug builds
(1,425 tests; 57 skipped each). Derived-state, synchronized-dictionary, and
view suites passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (64 tests), with no reported leaks.
After adding a further test that inserts/deletes entries while forward and
reverse item iterators run, the final derived-state suite also passed `-R 3:3`
in both builds (17 tests), with no reported leaks. Coverage includes all six
iterator types, direct and view construction, split/unicode/general tables,
deleted entries, copy/pickle, sticky size errors, changed-key errors, four-group
