consumption, concurrent snapshots, and key/value consistency during mutation.
This closes creation-time state inheritance and synchronized advancement for
list/set/dictionary iterators. Changes to existing wrappers when the container
changes state, other iterator families, and general access enforcement remain
incomplete.

Function metadata mutation now checks ownership and sharing state for name,
qualified name, docstring, module, annotations, annotation callbacks, and type
parameters. Setters already using stop-the-world publication recheck state
after stopping the world, including after qualified-name watcher callbacks.
The C annotation setter follows the same validation and releases its retained
argument on rejection. Ordinary metadata updates do not emit the execution
attribute deprecation warning. Annotation replacement commits both annotation
fields before decrefing old objects, so reentrant finalizers observe the final
state instead of an intermediate pair of fields.

This is a prerequisite for synchronized function namespaces, not their
implementation: function `__dict__` creation/replacement and namespace changes
across local/synchronized function transitions remain pending. Lazy annotation
cache creation and full function freezing also still need auditing.

Function mutation/sharing, function attributes, type parameters, annotationlib,
function C API, and C API watcher suites passed in both debug builds (372
tests). Function mutation and C API suites also passed `-R 3:3` in both builds
(32 tests), with no reported leaks. New tests cover immutable and foreign-local
rejection, legal local/synchronized updates without deprecation warnings,
qualified-name watcher freezing, annotation finalizer reentrancy, and C API
validation/clearing. All namespace work noted above remains unfinished.

Initial function namespace allocation now selects SynchronizedDict when the
function is synchronized. The common non-managed dictionary creation path
covers both `func.__dict__`/`vars(func)` and first assignment of a function
attribute, including construction racing across thread groups. Local function
namespaces remain ordinary dictionaries, and attribute values are not copied
or reowned. Tests cover both allocation entry points, FunctionType construction
for local/synchronized functions, stable dictionary aliases, shallow payload
ownership, and four-group concurrent creation followed by 400 distinct writes.

Explicit `func.__dict__` replacement and LOCAL-to-SYNCHRONIZED transitions with
an already-created dictionary still need atomic namespace conversion. This
change handles initial allocation only; it does not complete the function
namespace policy or justify enabling all general heap-access checks.

Function sharing/mutation, function attributes, functools, types, and function
C API suites passed in both debug builds (559 tests; one skipped each). The
function-sharing suite passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (13 tests), with no
reported leaks. Existing namespace replacement uses delayed reference release
in `_PyObject_SetDict`; subsequent conversion work must preserve that lifetime
rule as well as state validation and dictionary aliases.

Function namespace replacement now routes both Python assignment and
PyObject_GenericSetDict through a function-specific helper. It checks function
mutability/ownership and access to the supplied dictionary, synchronizes exact
ordinary dictionaries in place for synchronized functions, and rechecks the
function after the conversion's blocking work. It preserves the generic path's
delayed reference release so concurrent attribute readers can finish using
references to the old dictionary. Local functions can retain ordinary dict
subclasses; synchronized functions currently reject those subclasses rather
than silently copying them. Already synchronized dictionaries retain identity.

This does not yet synchronize an existing namespace when a LOCAL function
becomes SYNCHRONIZED through code/closure replacement. Such state transitions,
subclass synchronization, and general attribute access checks remain pending.

The broader inspect regression exposed missing signatures on the three
synchronized container constructors. Their type documentation now includes
text signatures, as do the synchronized list/dictionary custom reduction
methods. Inspection also found that the inherited list initializer silently
ignored keywords for SynchronizedList because it has a distinct tp_new.
SynchronizedList now has a keyword-rejecting initializer, with a regression
test verifying rejection leaves an existing list unchanged.

After the introspection and initializer fixes, the function sharing/mutation,
function attributes, functools, types, function C API, synchronized dictionary,
dictionary, inspect, synchronized list, and synchronized set suites passed in
both debug builds (1,151 tests; one skipped each). Function sharing and
synchronized list suites passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (31 tests), with no
reported leaks. Namespace replacement tests cover exact and split dictionaries,
alias preservation, local dictionary subclasses, invalid/immutable/foreign
rejection through Python and the generic C API, and concurrent readers and
replacement writers. The initial inspect failure is resolved in the final
regression runs.

Code and C closure replacement now prepare an existing function namespace
before making the function synchronized. The helper examines the prospective
code/closure and current namespace with the world stopped. When conversion is
needed it retains the namespace, resumes the world, synchronizes the dictionary,
and retries against current function fields. Only after the namespace is ready
are execution fields and sharing state committed in the stopped interval.
Namespace aliases, including split instance dictionaries, retain identity.
An already synchronized namespace remains synchronized when the function goes
back to local. Unsupported namespace conversion fails without replacing the
execution attribute.

Code replacement also revalidates closure size in the final stopped interval,
so a warning/watcher callback cannot make the earlier validation stale. It
retains the function name and resumes the world before formatting a mismatch
exception. The shared namespace conversion helper now accepts conversion
already completed by another caller while it waited. Closure classification
is still conservative; this does not implement modifiable-binding analysis,
full function freezing, or general heap-to-thread access enforcement.

Function sharing/mutation, function attributes, function C API and watchers,
synchronized dictionaries, module synchronization/freezing, and functools
suites passed in both debug builds (552 tests; one skipped each). Function
sharing/mutation and module synchronization passed `-R 3:3` in both builds
(52 tests), with no reported leaks. New tests cover code and C closure
transitions, retained namespace aliases, split dictionaries, transitions back
to local, conversion rejection preserving execution fields, and warning
callbacks that change closure shape before code replacement commits.

Thread integration audit found that the native `_ThreadHandle` wrapper was
still local despite serializing all mutable native state. It now declares
synchronized state after installing the native handle, before publication.
The wrapper's handle pointer is stable for its lifetime; ident/state use the
native mutex, completion uses PyEvent, join/set-done use the once flag, and
native ownership uses atomic reference counts. The type cannot be subclassed.
Tests check metadata and transfer identity for unstarted, existing-thread, and
current-thread handles, plus four-group concurrent join with completion and
identifier reads on a newly started thread.

Thread objects, their namespaces, Event/Condition state, and the broader
threading module sharing policy are still pending. Merely marking Thread
itself synchronized would leave these dependencies unsafe; the handle change
is a prerequisite, not completion of Thread sharing or general access checks.

ThreadGroup, low-level thread, and threading suites passed in both debug builds
(298 tests; five skipped each). The two new handle-state/concurrent-join tests
passed `-R 3:3` in both builds, with no reported leaks. These results cover the
native handle declaration and existing threading regressions, not the still
unimplemented sharing of Thread/Event objects and their namespaces.

Thread now inherits `_thread._ThreadBase`, which owns its actual synchronized
namespace, weakref list, and GC hooks. Its constructor declares instances shared
only when added instance storage consists of Python slots; opaque extra C
storage must opt in separately. The weakref descriptor retains references under
the weakref lock and handles references already being destroyed. Synchronized
non-managed namespaces are recreated as SynchronizedDict after clearing, and
generic dictionary replacement converts new namespaces while preserving delayed
release of the previous dictionary. It rechecks immutable/frozen state after
conversion. Managed-dictionary synchronization outside Thread remains pending.

The first new cross-group dummy-thread test exposed a pre-existing thread-local
cleanup problem: a cached callback-free weakref retained its creator's local
ownership and was rejected by the checked tuple getter in clear_locals. This
caused unraisable errors and leaked dummy threads. Exact native local objects,
their shared internal registries, cached control weakrefs, and native cleanup
callbacks now carry shared state; private TLS key/sentinel identity tokens are
immutable. Per-thread user dictionaries remain local, and ordinary local
subclasses are not promised cross-group access. The getter access check remains
enabled. This fixes the observed cleanup failure without suppressing it.

Thread namespace storage is now shared, but Event/Condition dependencies,
callback/closure classification, module/class sharing policy, finalizer affinity,
and general heap-reference checks still require implementation. This work does
not establish completion of all threading semantics under PEP 805.

Thread sharing, ThreadGroup, low-level thread, threading, thread-local storage,
types, weakrefs, GC, and object/type C API regression suites passed in the GIL
debug build (699 tests; 13 skipped). The free-threaded debug run passed nine of
the ten suites, but the existing `BarrierTests.test_barrier_10` timed out after
30 seconds during a run with high system load (699 tests; 18 skipped). Both
that test alone and the complete threading suite then passed with
`--fail-env-changed` (245 tests; five skipped for the latter). The timeout has
not been reproduced; these reruns do not establish its cause. Thread sharing
and thread-local suites passed `-R 3:3` in both builds (32 tests), with no
reported leaks. The earlier unraisable TLS cleanup errors and reference leaks
were absent from these final runs. `git diff --check` also passed.

Generic subscription now validates owned results from mapping slots and
`__class_getitem__`, releasing inaccessible references while preserving callback
exceptions. The existing sequence-slot path already checks its result. String
mapping lookup inherits the check through PyObject_GetItem. Tests cover ordinary,
synchronized, and proxy mappings, custom subscriptions, accessible shared and
new local results, and reference lifetimes after repeated rejected retrievals.
The resource test now constructs its foreign argument tuples in their owning
group so it continues exercising the C boundary rather than failing earlier
while assembling the arguments in Python.

Enabling this check exposed the runtime's use of foreign local builtin namespaces
and module objects through the public C import API. The builtins initializer now
synchronizes its real namespace, native functions, and module before publication.
Threading and copy opt into module synchronization, and Shareable members are
frozen, including their attribute dictionaries. These declarations are shallow;
other modules and mutable namespace payloads retain their own ownership. The
generic global-load implementation recognizes exact SynchronizedDict storage
and uses the same internal thread-safe lookup as ordinary native dictionaries.
General LOAD_GLOBAL result checking and specialization support for synchronized
global/builtin namespaces remain pending; this change does not claim to enforce
all VM reference acquisitions. Existing specialized subscription paths and the
direct-dictionary fast path in PyMapping_GetOptionalItem also remain to be
connected to access checks.

PyImport_Import now preserves errors other than KeyError when retrieving a
custom __import__ hook. A new regression verifies that an inaccessible hook in
a synchronized builtin mapping produces IllegalThreadAccessException and is
not called. Initial generic-subscription smoke failures and the subsequent
thread startup wait exposed the missing runtime sharing declarations and the
global-lookup path; those failures are resolved in the final runs.

Free-threaded debug regression suites for builtins, importlib, abstract C APIs,
module synchronization, threading, sharing metadata and sequence/subscription
access passed (1,714 tests; 34 skipped). Earlier targeted suites for ThreadGroup,
Thread storage, Channel, TransferBox, sequence access, sharing metadata and copy
also passed (167 tests; one skipped). The GIL debug build passed the combined
twelve suites (1,853 tests; 33 skipped). After adding the custom import-hook
regression, sequence/subscription access and sharing metadata passed `-R 3:3`
in both builds (30 tests; one skipped), with no reported leaks. The missing
optional decimal extension accounts for the access suite's skip.

The exact-dictionary fast path of PyMapping_GetOptionalItem now checks the
reference returned by PyDict_GetItemRef before publishing it. Access rejection
releases the new reference, sets the output pointer to NULL, and returns -1;
it cannot be confused with a missing key. This includes frozendict, whose
immutable container does not make a mutable value accessible. The string-key
variant and internal optional-mapping wrappers inherit this behavior. Direct
PyDict_GetItemRef callers are still a separate pending boundary.

The subscription tests now apply the same ownership/lifetime matrix to exact
dict, frozendict, SynchronizedDict and mappingproxy through ordinary and
optional lookup, with object and string keys. Existing C test wrappers assert
the output pointer is NULL on missing/error returns. Additional tests retain
the missing-key convention and arbitrary callback exception propagation.
Sequence/subscription access, abstract C APIs, importlib, ThreadGroup, Channel,
and sharing metadata passed in both debug builds (1,332 tests; 21 skipped each).
Sequence/subscription access and abstract C APIs also passed `-R 3:3` in both
builds (67 tests; one skipped), with no reported leaks. `git diff --check`
passed.

Owned dictionary lookup now checks results in PyDict_GetItemRef itself, so
PyDict_GetItemStringRef and PyMapping_GetOptionalItem use the same boundary.
The dictionary retains its value on rejection, while the newly acquired
reference is released and the output is set to NULL. The regression matrix
also exercises both direct dictionary getters for dict, frozendict, and
SynchronizedDict. Existing direct-getter C wrappers assert the output-pointer
contract on errors.

The internal lookup is factored as _PyDict_GetItemRefUnchecked. The two generic
attribute/method namespace paths use that internal operation: attribute access
still needs a single coordinated check across descriptor, inline-value, and
specialized VM paths, plus sharing support for Thread's Event/Condition and
context dependencies. An initial build with only the public getter change
partially checked dictionary-backed attributes and rejected Thread._started
before its startup notification, leaving Thread.start waiting. That experiment
was stopped. The internal split preserves the prior attribute semantics while
enabling the public dictionary boundary; it does not complete or claim general
attribute access enforcement. Borrowed getters and other dictionary APIs remain
pending too.

The direct-getter call-site audit checked existing handling of negative returns
in module/type metadata, argument parsing, compiler tables, thread-local storage,
OrderedDict iteration, and extension registries. A SQLite regression registers
a local callable adapter in Main, constructs its input in another group, and
verifies that fetching the adapter raises IllegalThreadAccessException without
invoking it or replacing the exception. Both debug builds passed dictionary and
abstract C API tests, sequence/subscription access, ThreadGroup, Channel, Thread
storage, sharing metadata, importlib, thread-local storage and SQLite (1,924
tests; 25 skipped each).
Sequence/subscription access and dictionary C API suites passed `-R 3:3` in
both builds (48 tests; one skipped), with no reported leaks. The final
`git diff --check` passed.

Specialized builtin subscription now checks owned results before publishing
them as stack references: list/tuple integer lookup, dictionary lookup, and the
Tier 2 dictionary known-hash variant. List lookup uses the common internal
owned-reference getter in both builds. This also makes generated stack spilling
consistent across build configurations before the access checker can allocate
an exception. Lookup misses still take the original deoptimization/error path;
access failures release only the new result and retain VM inputs for error
unwinding. The checker is exported for generated test-extension code as well.

The optimizer's frozendict subscription folding is restricted to constant
mappings whose values all have immutable state. Frozen storage alone is not a
proof that a result is safe in every group. Immutable values remain eligible
for the existing fold; other mappings retain the runtime check, with known-hash
optimization where available. Instruction cases and metadata were regenerated.
The Tier 2 optimizer source passes a compiler syntax check with `_Py_TIER2=1`,
but the current builds do not execute Tier 2, so runtime optimizer/JIT coverage
remains unverified.

The new subscription test warms each operation in a worker group, inspects its
actual thread-local bytecode using get_tlbc in the free-threaded build, and
asserts specialization before attempting 100 forbidden reads. It covers list,
tuple, dict, SynchronizedDict, and frozendict, then verifies allowed reads still
work and that no payload reference is leaked or stolen. Both debug builds
passed the subscription/access, list, tuple, dict, synchronized dict, ThreadGroup
and Channel regression suites (330 tests; one skipped free-threaded, two GIL).
The five existing opcache subscription tests passed in both builds, as did
`-R 3:3` for the new specialization/ownership test, with no reported leaks.
Custom Python __getitem__ inlining, other heap loads, and full attribute
enforcement remain pending. `git diff --check` passed.

A separate GIL debug build in `/tmp/pep805-tier2-source` now enables the Tier 2
interpreter (`--enable-experimental-jit=interpreter`); sys._jit.is_enabled()
returns true there. This executes optimized uops, but does not validate the
machine-code JIT backend. All 20 subscription-related optimizer tests passed,
including a new test that requires the known-hash lookup to remain for a
frozendict containing a local list, then attempts the read from another group.
The worker reaches a loop backedge before its first lookup so it can enter the
already attached executor. Existing immutable-value constant-fold tests pass
as well. The same eight subscription/container/thread regression suites passed
under Tier 2 (330 tests; two skipped).

Running the full optimizer suite exposed 18 failures from the previously
synchronized builtins namespace: global specialization and optimizer guards
still accepted only exact dict. These paths now recognize exact
SynchronizedDict storage, using the existing key-version guards, watcher
invalidation, and reference-acquisition machinery. Arbitrary dict subclasses
remain excluded. New tests assert LOAD_GLOBAL_MODULE and LOAD_GLOBAL_BUILTIN
specialization on shared namespaces and correct results after replacing both
global and builtin values. This extends native-layout optimization, not general
global-result access enforcement.

After the fix, the full Tier 2 optimizer suite and module synchronization suite
passed (321 tests; three skipped). Full opcache, module synchronization,
subscription ownership, and sequence-access suites passed in both ordinary
debug builds (110 tests; one skipped each). Module synchronization passed
`-R 3:3` in both builds (12 tests), with no reported leaks. The new Tier 2 test
needs fresh code on each repetition because foreign-access error exits alter
executor state; its cleanup invalidates executors and drains deferred deletion.
Its final `-R 3:3` run passed: no reference leak was reported, and memory-block
deltas [0, 1, 0] were within the runner's accepted tolerance. The earlier
repetition failure and block-growth report are not final validation results.
`git diff --check` passed.

Inlined Python __getitem__ calls now mark the callee frame for return-value
access validation. The normal return and return-monitoring paths check before
publishing the result; ordinary nested calls initialize their own flag to zero.
Frame copying preserves the flag, while generator-frame creation clears it
because returning the newly created generator completes the protocol call.
Generated Tier 1 and Tier 2 cases spill live stack references before the checker
can allocate an exception, and retain the callee frame and result for normal
error unwinding. This does not enforce access on arbitrary function returns.

Exact native method, classmethod, member, getset, and wrapper descriptors now
have immutable state. Their native definitions and owning-type references are
fixed after construction; the lazy qualified-name cache already uses the
existing descriptor critical section. This allows checked superclass lookup
during class creation in another group. It is shallow immutability: descriptor
field acquisition and arbitrary Python descriptor policies remain pending.

Subscription tests now require actual Python __getitem__ specialization before
rejecting foreign results, including a nested ordinary-call case that detects
incorrect flag inheritance. A monitoring test verifies that forbidden results
never reach the local PY_RETURN callback. The Tier 2 test requires an executor
containing the inlined subscription call, then enters it from another group.
Native descriptor state, ownership, access, qualified names, and worker-group
class construction have separate regression coverage.

All three debug builds passed the subscription and sharing suites (20 tests).
The ordinary GIL build passed the broader frame, generator, coroutine,
monitoring, sys, opcache, access, sharing, and descriptor suites (716 tests;
17 skipped). The free-threaded run initially failed only the frame-size test:
the added byte moves localsplus by eight bytes on this build. Its struct-format
expectation now includes the new flag, owner, and visited bytes; the final
sys rerun passed (106 tests; seven skipped). The other nine free-threaded suites
had already passed. Tier 2 passed the optimizer, monitoring, and frame suites
(476 tests; 11 skipped), including 21 subscription optimizer tests in the
separate filtered run. Machine-code JIT execution remains unverified.

Subscription and sharing suites passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary builds (20
tests each), with no reported leaks. The new Tier 2 inlined-return test passed
-R 3:3 with no reference leak; memory-block deltas [1, 1, 0] were within the
runner's accepted tolerance. git diff --check passed. General heap-load access
checks, protective locks, closure analysis, and the other outstanding PEP
requirements still prevent this branch from being a complete implementation.

PyDict_GetItemWithError now delegates to the checked strong-reference getter,
then releases the temporary reference before returning its borrowed result.
Access validation therefore runs while the result is kept alive. A rejected
result returns NULL with IllegalThreadAccessException; missing keys still
return NULL without an exception. The existing borrowed-reference concurrency
limitation remains documented. The internal string-with-error helper also
reaches this boundary; exception-suppressing public getters and private
known-hash/unicode lookup helpers are not yet covered.

The subscription lifetime matrix now exercises this API for dict, frozendict,
and SynchronizedDict, checking repeated foreign-value rejection, allowed
immutable values, and preservation and eventual release of container-owned
references. A separate test verifies that dir() on a synchronized module
propagates the access exception for a foreign local __dir__ callable without
calling it or treating it as missing. Existing dictionary C API tests cover
missing keys, hash errors, and invalid mapping types. Audited callers include
type creation, module dir, repr recursion state, compiler/symbol tables,
ElementTree, regex groups, pickle registries, and native extension lookups.

Both GIL and free-threaded debug builds passed ten regression suites covering
sequence access, dictionary C APIs, descriptors, threading, Thread storage,
ThreadGroups, modules, C ElementTree, pickle, and regex (2,062 tests; 74 skipped
each). The subsequent access/dictionary -R 3:3 runs include the additional
module-dir test (49 tests; one skipped each), with no reported leaks. The Tier 2
interpreter build passed those same 49 tests (one skipped). Documentation and
git diff --check pass. These results do not establish complete heap-load
validation or completion of the reference implementation.

PyDict_GetItem and PyDict_GetItemString now validate acquired values before
returning borrowed references. The temporary owned lookup reference (or an
explicit new reference in the GIL build) keeps the value alive during access
validation. Rejection releases that temporary reference, reports the exception
through the existing unraisable path, and returns NULL while restoring the
previously saved exception. This preserves these APIs' suppression contract;
callers needing an access exception should use PyDict_GetItemRef instead.

A new test covers both getters with dict, frozendict, and SynchronizedDict,
checking same-group success, foreign-value rejection, the reported exception
type, and container ownership and final payload reclamation. GIL and
free-threaded builds passed eight regression suites including importlib,
threading, thread-local storage, modules, descriptors, pickle, and dictionary
and access C APIs (2,831 tests; 82 skipped GIL, 84 free-threaded). Both builds
passed -R 3:3 for the dictionary/access suites (50 tests; one skipped), with no
reported leaks. The Tier 2 interpreter passed those 50 tests as well (one
skipped). git diff --check passed. Private known-hash and unicode dictionary
lookup helpers still require their own caller audit and access integration.

The borrowed _PyDict_GetItem_KnownHash helper now obtains an owned reference,
validates access, and releases only that temporary reference before returning.
Its Unicode-key wrapper inherits the check. Counter's native counting loop and
dictionary-item symmetric difference already distinguish missing keys from
lookup exceptions. Tests cover known-hash reads of foreign and immutable values
in dict, frozendict, and SynchronizedDict, including payload lifetime, and
verify Counter.update rejects a foreign count before invoking its __add__.
The Counter test constructs its empty counter and inserts the foreign value
directly so the intended update boundary is exercised.

The full collections suite exposed two earlier interface gaps: UserDict and
UserList lacked synchronize(), which their wrapped builtin types now provide.
Both wrappers now delegate the storage transfer to data.synchronize(), returning
the synchronized builtin container and retaining the emptied data object.
The wrapper's own state is unchanged. Tests verify output types, element
identity, and empty aliases; the existing interface-superset tests pass without
being weakened. The methods are documented in the collections reference.

All three debug configurations (free-threaded, GIL, and Tier 2 interpreter)
passed the six access, dictionary C API, dict, collections, ThreadGroup, and
Channel suites (345 tests; two skipped free-threaded, three each in GIL/Tier 2).
Access tests passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary debug builds (19 tests; one skipped),
with no reported leaks. git diff --check passed. Raw lookup/iteration paths and
general heap-load gates remain unfinished; this is not a completion claim.

The common PyIter_Next/PyIter_NextItem implementation now checks each owned
result and releases inaccessible values before returning an error. The error
path leaves the output NULL and reports -1 for PyIter_NextItem, preserving the
existing distinction between errors and normal exhaustion. Builtin next()
checks successful iterator-slot results as well; supplying a default does not
hide an access exception. Already acquired thread references used as defaults
are unchanged. The rejected item has been consumed; subsequent iteration can
continue according to the iterator's own state.

New iterator-access tests cover list, tuple, dictionary-value, generator, and
Python-defined iterator results through both C APIs and next() with and without
a default. They check rejection, subsequent allowed reads and exhaustion,
callback errors, same-group mutable results, and payload lifetime. All three
debug builds passed the iterator, generator, itertools, builtin, abstract C API,
derived-sharing, ThreadGroup, and Channel suites (522 tests; 13 skipped
free-threaded, 14 each GIL/Tier 2). The new test module passed -R 3:3 in both
ordinary debug builds with no reported leaks. API documentation and git diff
--check pass. Direct tp_iternext callers, FOR_ITER specializations and generator
inlining, and send/async iterator result checks remain outstanding.

PyIter_Send now funnels native am_send, tp_iternext, and Python send-method
results through access validation. Both PYGEN_NEXT and PYGEN_RETURN values are
checked. Rejection releases the acquired reference, sets the output to NULL,
and returns PYGEN_ERROR without suppressing IllegalThreadAccessException.
Existing callback errors and exhaustion retain their previous behavior.
The internal _PyIter_Send wrapper, generic SEND, and asyncio task stepping
inherit this boundary; inlined generator SEND and direct generator methods
still need separate integration.

A new test C wrapper asserts the result/exception contract. Tests exercise
native generators, fallback iteration, and send methods with both yielded and
returned values, checking same-group identity, cross-group rejection, allowed
immutable values, and payload lifetime. A generic yield-from test catches the
access error inside the delegating generator, proving the check happens before
its caller receives the value. The five iterator-access tests passed -R 3:3
in both GIL and free-threaded builds with no reported leaks, and passed in the
Tier 2 interpreter build. Broader asyncio validation follows below.

The broader send-boundary validation passed in all three debug configurations:
iterator access, coroutines, generators, async generators, the full asyncio
suite, and abstract C APIs (3,219 tests; 21 skipped each, including two
Windows-only test modules). This run preceded the additional generic
yield-from test, which is covered by the final five-test leak checks and
Tier 2 access run above. No machine-code JIT or Windows execution is claimed.
git diff --check passed. The remaining inlined generator, direct-slot,
attribute/global access, protection, and lifetime requirements remain active.

For-loop item validation now covers the common virtual/ordinary iterator
helper, instrumented loops, dedicated virtual-iteration operations in both
tiers, and Tier 2 direct iterator-slot calls. List/tuple specialization adds a
separate _CHECK_ITER_ACCESS operation after acquiring the item and advancing
the index. Its generated code spills the result and other live stack values
before the checker can allocate an exception, and leaves the owned result on
the stack for normal error unwinding. Range specialization creates immutable
integers and needs no check. Inlined generator iteration remains outstanding,
as do specialized virtual SEND operations that bypass the common helper.

New tests exercise cold and specialized loops over list/tuple, synchronized
lists, explicit list/tuple iterators, dictionary values, and sets. They require
actual list/tuple specialization in the executing thread, reject 100 foreign
reads, and verify allowed reads and payload lifetime. Fresh code objects keep
specialization counters independent across cases and refleak repetitions.
A Tier 2 test requires _CHECK_ITER_ACCESS in the executor and reaches the loop
backedge with an allowed item before reading a foreign value in that executor.
The full optimizer/monitoring/iteration suite passed (620 tests; eight skipped).

The broader free-threaded and GIL runs passed all iteration, itertools,
generator, opcache, ThreadGroup, Channel, and derived-sharing modules, but first
exposed two sequence-access fixture errors: one iterated a foreign class, and
one unpacked a table containing foreign native test functions. The class is now
created in the worker; the shared-element test invokes each of the same six
C getters directly. This preserves the tested boundaries while avoiding an
unrelated access failure during setup. The corrected sequence suite passed
(19 tests; one skipped) and -R 3:3 passed in both builds. The six iterator-access
tests also passed -R 3:3 in both builds with no reported leaks. Tier 2 passed
both final access modules (25 tests; one skipped). The new executor test passed
-R 3:3 with no reference leak; block deltas [0, 1, 0] are within the test runner's
accepted tolerance. git diff --check passed. These checks do not establish
complete iteration or general heap-load enforcement.

Specialized virtual SEND now validates acquired list/tuple items in Tier 1
and Tier 2 before transferring them to the delegating generator. Rejection
releases the temporary owned reference and enters normal error unwinding with
the input stack intact. Generated cases spill live inputs before the checker,
and Tier 2 metadata records that the operation can escape. Exhaustion still
returns the existing None result without changing its handling.

A new specialization test warms yield-from delegation, requires SEND_VIRTUAL
in the executing thread's bytecode, and catches 100 foreign-item failures
inside the delegate. This distinguishes rejection at SEND from a later check
by an outer iterator consumer. It also checks subsequent allowed values and
payload lifetime. The Tier 2 test requires _SEND_VIRTUAL_TIER_TWO in an executor
and enters the loop with an allowed value before encountering a foreign item;
the delegate catches the exception in both list and tuple cases. Fresh code
and executor cleanup isolate repetitions.

Both ordinary debug builds passed generator, coroutine, async-generator,
monitoring, iterator, opcache, and iterator-access suites (514 tests each).
The Tier 2 interpreter passed the full optimizer suite plus generator,
coroutine, monitoring and iterator-access suites (576 tests; three skipped).
The seven iterator-access tests passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary builds with no
reported leaks. The new Tier 2 test passed -R 3:3 without a reference leak;
memory-block deltas [0, 1, 0] were within the runner's tolerance. git diff
--check passed. Inlined generator yield/return transfer and direct generator
methods still need their own access boundaries, alongside the remaining full
PEP requirements.

Direct generator send, __next__, and throw now check yielded values and values
about to be placed in StopIteration. Coroutine wrappers inherit the same
public method boundaries. Generator close checks a returned value after the
frame has completed, while preserving the existing RuntimeError when a
generator yields instead of honoring GeneratorExit. Async-generator unwrapping
checks the contained value while its wrapper still owns it, before constructing
the StopIteration result consumed by the awaiter. These checks occur outside
the producer's evaluation: rejection does not become a throw into the producer,
and suspended generators can subsequently resume normally.

Tests cover direct send/next yield and return, throw yield and return, close
return, coroutine return, and asynchronous yielded values. They verify errors
at the caller, suspension/closure state, continued allowed reads, and payload
lifetime. All three debug builds passed the six iterator-access, generator,
coroutine, async-generator, monitoring, and asyncio-task modules (1,435 tests).
The combined command reported failure solely because it also named the removed
test_pep380 module. Its current replacement test_yield_from and the additional
test_generator_stop module then passed separately in all three builds (45
tests). The ten iterator-access tests passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary debug
builds with no reported leaks. Documentation and git diff --check pass.
Inlined generator frame transfers still bypass these public methods and
require separate integration; the full PEP implementation remains incomplete.

END_SEND now validates generator/coroutine completion values in the receiving
frame, after the producer has returned and its frame has closed. It is no
longer marked pure: its access check can raise and must survive optimization.
The instrumented form performs the same check before STOP_ITERATION callbacks.
Generated Tier 1 and Tier 2 cases preserve the receiver, index marker, and
owned completion value for normal caller-side error unwinding. The producer
does not receive a synthetic throw and is not reopened by rejection.

Tests require SEND_GEN specialization, repeatedly reject a foreign completion
value inside the delegating generator, verify producer closure and payload
lifetime, and check that local STOP_ITERATION monitoring receives only an
allowed value. A coroutine test verifies the same caller-side await behavior.
The Tier 2 test pre-creates coroutines so tracing reaches await rather than
stopping at an unspecialized coroutine-construction call; it requires END_SEND
in an attached executor and uses an allowed first completion to enter the loop
before a foreign completion is rejected.

Both ordinary debug builds passed generator, coroutine, async-generator,
yield-from, generator-stop, monitoring, and opcache regressions (484 tests
each). All three builds passed the 12 iterator-access tests. Tier 2 passed the
full optimizer, iterator-access, monitoring, yield-from and coroutine suites
(566 tests; three skipped). Iterator-access -R 3:3 passed in both ordinary
builds without reported leaks. The new Tier 2 test passed -R 3:3 without a
reference leak; block deltas [-1, 3, -1] were accepted by the runner. git diff
--check passed. Inlined yielded values, discarded for-loop completion values,
and other unconnected heap-load/protection requirements remain outstanding.

END_FOR now checks a completed generator's value before discarding it, including
before INSTRUMENTED_END_FOR publishes the value through STOP_ITERATION. This
matches the checked generic generator-next path even when FOR_ITER_GEN inlines
the producer. The producer has already closed when rejection occurs; the
consumer catches the access exception and ordinary unwinding releases the
owned value. The existing no_save_ip behavior is retained for POP_ITER's
branch monitoring. Generated Tier 1 and Tier 2 cases now describe the error
edge and keep the owned value on the stack until error unwinding.

The regression failed before the implementation and now checks same-group
acceptance, actual FOR_ITER_GEN specialization, repeated foreign-value
rejection, closed generator state, subsequent allowed completions, payload
lifetime, and monitoring callback exclusion. A separate optimizer test
requires END_FOR in an attached executor and reaches it from a loop after an
allowed first completion, verifying caller-side exception handling in Tier 2.

All three debug builds passed the 13 iterator-access tests. Both ordinary
builds passed the generator, coroutine, async-generator, yield-from,
generator-stop, monitoring and opcache suites (484 tests each). The Tier 2
interpreter passed its full optimizer suite plus iterator-access, monitoring,
yield-from and coroutine suites (568 tests; three skipped). Iterator-access
-R 3:3 passed in both ordinary builds without reported leaks. The new Tier 2
test passed -R 3:3 without a reference leak; block deltas [0, 2, -1] were
within the runner's tolerance. git diff --check passed. Native machine-code
JIT execution remains unvalidated, and the complete PEP implementation is
still pending, including inlined yield transfer and protection/lifetime work.

Inlined YIELD_VALUE now checks its result after suspending the generator and
switching back to the receiving Python frame. On rejection, the owned result
is on the receiving stack for ordinary error unwinding. The producer remains
suspended and can resume; the access exception is not thrown into its yield
expression. Interpreter-owned trampoline frames retain the existing public
generator-method checks instead of entering Python-frame exception unwinding.

Tier 2 inserts a conservative ownership/state guard before the frame switch.
Shared values and values local to the current group continue in the executor;
other states exit to the original YIELD_VALUE in Tier 1, which performs the
full check in the receiving frame. This avoids using a producer-relative
error target after changing frames, and keeps checks ahead of dynamic caller
guards and their side exits. Trace construction reserves the extra exit and
handles the new replacement explicitly, rather than applying the for/send
end-instruction target calculation. That missing distinction initially caused
a debug assertion during Tier 2 build initialization; the corrected translation
passes initialization and the targeted optimizer test.

The regression exercises both FOR_ITER_GEN and SEND_GEN specialization,
repeated caller-side rejection, continued allowed yields, payload lifetime,
and a producer that fails if the access exception is injected into it. The
optimizer test requires the new guard in an executor attached to the consumer,
uses an allowed first yield to reach the loop, and verifies that a foreign
yield is rejected while the producer can subsequently yield an allowed value.

All three debug builds passed the 14 iterator-access tests. The ordinary GIL
and free-threaded builds passed generator, coroutine, async-generator,
yield-from, generator-stop, monitoring and opcache regressions (484 tests
each). Tier 2 passed the full optimizer, iterator-access, monitoring,
yield-from and coroutine suites (570 tests; three skipped). Iterator-access
-R 3:3 passed in both ordinary builds without reported leaks. The new Tier 2
test passed -R 3:3 without a reference leak; memory-block deltas [1, 1, 0]
were accepted by the runner. git diff --check passed. Existing CALL_TYPE_1
unused-cache-variable and testinternalcapi dump_cache_item compiler warnings
were also present in the preceding END_FOR build; they are unrelated to this
change. Native machine-code JIT execution is still unvalidated.

Producer-side PY_YIELD/PY_RETURN monitoring, other heap-to-thread acquisitions,
protection, and lifetime/reclamation requirements remain incomplete. These
receiving-side generator checks do not establish full PEP conformance.

PyObject_GetIter and PyObject_GetAIter now validate owned iterator results after
their existing protocol checks. The sequence fallback preserves its existing
behavior and also uses the owned-result helper. GET_AITER performs the same
check on its direct slot result before pushing it. Rejection releases the
newly acquired reference and prevents the returned foreign iterator from being
advanced. Tests cover same-group identity, builtin and direct C API calls,
repeated rejection, payload lifetime, and synchronous/asynchronous loops.
The native test selects its C API callable directly rather than fetching the
Main-owned callable itself through checked iteration.

Enabling this boundary exposed a runtime issue under regrtest's audit hook:
the interpreter's private hook registry was a Main-owned list. Frame inspection
in worker groups triggered an audit event and failed while creating the
registry iterator. A debugger backtrace identified PyObject_GetIter from
sys_audit_tstate, rather than an error in the generator tests. The registry is
now SynchronizedList, and initial publication uses the existing audit mutex
with acquire/release pointer access. Allocation and decref occur outside the
mutex. A subprocess regression registers hooks from multiple groups and emits
an event in another group; all registered shared hooks receive it.

The Tier 2 regression requires GET_AITER in the consumer's executor. Each
async iterator yields an allowed value and the loop breaks normally, so the
outer loop can be traced without exhaustion exceptions. A worker first
completes an allowed iteration, then rejects a foreign async iterator in the
attached executor. Fresh helper-method code objects and explicit executor
cleanup isolate adaptive caches across refleak repetitions; without fresh
code, the reused test helpers retained some prior Factory types. The access
assertion and required executor opcode are unchanged.

All three debug builds passed the 17 iterator-access tests and the new audit
subprocess regression. The ordinary GIL and free-threaded builds passed 14
iterator, generator/coroutine, audit, ThreadGroup, sharing, synchronized
container and access suites (502 tests each; six skipped with GIL, five with
free-threading). The final Tier 2 optimizer, iterator-access, audit and
coroutine run passed 468 tests with seven skips. Iterator-access -R 3:3 passed
in both ordinary builds without reported leaks. The isolated Tier 2 test
passed -R 3:3 without a reference leak; memory-block deltas [0, 2, 0] were
within the runner's tolerance. C API and builtin iterator documentation was
updated, and git diff --check passed. Awaitable acquisition, remaining heap
loads, protection, and lifetime requirements still need implementation.

Awaitable acquisition now validates the input to _PyCoro_GetAwaitableIter before
invoking __await__, and validates an iterator returned by that slot. Native
and generator-based coroutines are checked before any execution. GET_ANEXT
also checks the intermediate result of __anext__ before invoking its await
protocol. Generic async-for and async-with error formatting preserves
IllegalThreadAccessException and UnprotectedAccessException, while retaining
existing TypeError wrapping for other invalid awaitable results.

Tests cover repeated local acceptance and foreign rejection for __await__
results, foreign awaitable inputs, and both intermediate and final __anext__
results. They assert that rejected objects' methods were never entered and
that acquired references are released. Native and generator-based coroutines
are rejected without execution in direct await and async-with entry/exit.
A protocol-error test checks both access exception classes and the unchanged
ValueError-to-TypeError cause relationship in async-for. The Tier 2 test
requires GET_AWAITABLE and GET_ANEXT in attached consumer executors, reaches
them after an allowed first iteration, and confirms rejected native iterators
remain unadvanced.

The broad asyncio regression exposed a pre-existing mismatch in the optimizer's
globals mutation counters: the fast paths accepted SynchronizedDict, but the
counter helpers asserted ordinary dictionary types only. Both helpers now
accept exact synchronized dictionaries, which use the same watcher field
layout. A focused regression reproduced the assertion before the fix and
checks repeated mutations of synchronized globals after optimizer attachment.

The ordinary GIL and free-threaded builds each passed the broad iterator,
coroutine, async-generator, asyncio, generator and asynchronous context-manager
regression run: 3,252 tests with 21 skips. The initial Tier 2 run exposed the
mutation-counter assertion described above. After the fix, the optimizer,
asyncio free-threading and iterator-access suites passed 362 tests with three
skips. The final optimizer suite, with await and async-for in independent
tests, passed 319 tests with three skips. The 20 iterator-access tests passed
-R 3:3 in both ordinary builds without reported leaks. The two new await
optimizer tests and synchronized-globals regression also passed -R 3:3
together without a reference leak; memory-block deltas [0, 1, 0] were within
the runner's tolerance.

During fixture development, optimizing both await modes sequentially in one
test retained an Awaitable type across refleak repetitions. Fresh helper code
and explicit executor invalidation did not resolve that combined fixture's
retention. Each mode passed independently, and they now have separate test
methods, preserving the executor-opcode, access-denial and iterator-position
assertions. This isolates the access regressions; it does not establish a fix
for the combined fixture's retention. Investigation of that retention remains
part of the outstanding optimizer/lifetime audit. Native machine-code JIT
execution was not validated.

LOAD_GLOBAL now runs a separate _CHECK_GLOBAL_ACCESS operation after obtaining
its value and before the conditional call-protocol null is pushed. The same
operation follows LOAD_GLOBAL_MODULE and LOAD_GLOBAL_BUILTINS, so Tier 2
promotion to an inline constant cannot remove the access boundary. It uses an
owned stack reference and ERROR_NO_POP, allowing normal exception unwinding to
release the rejected result. Other name-load instructions are not covered by
this increment.

Enabling the boundary exposed runtime globals used by threading. sys and
_thread now explicitly synchronize their namespaces and native entry points;
_thread declares its exported immutable native types immutable. The active
and limbo registries, including the post-fork active registry, use synchronized
dictionaries. time.monotonic and time.monotonic_ns explicitly declare their
native clock entry points synchronized. This does not declare the rest of
the time module or arbitrary extension modules synchronized.

Seven global-access regressions cover globals and custom builtins, generic
and actual specialized instructions, call-position loads, repeated foreign
rejection, local and shared acceptance, reference release and runtime entry
points. The Tier 2 test checks that the foreign object itself appears as an
inline constant in an attached executor, that the access-check uop remains,
and that another group is denied while the owning group remains allowed.
Existing iterator and subscription tests now transmit the worker's actual
specialized bytecode to Main for disassembly instead of accessing Main's
local dis module in the worker. Opcode assertions are preserved. The native
import regression explicitly shares its C wrapper so the rejection still
comes from the inaccessible import hook, not from loading the wrapper module.

All three native debug builds succeeded. The initial Tier 2 optimizer and
five global-access tests passed 325 tests with three skips. The final seven
global-access tests, 20 iterator-access tests and two subscription-access
tests passed together under both Tier 2 and the ordinary GIL build (29 tests
each). The seven global tests passed
-R 3:3 in both ordinary builds without reported leaks. The inline-constant
Tier 2 regression passed -R 3:3 without a reference leak; block deltas [0, 1, 0]
were within the runner's tolerance. The ordinary GIL and free-threaded builds
passed test_sys after the clock declaration and passed test_threading in the
broader run. Native machine-code JIT execution remains unvalidated.

The broader run is NOT clean. The final free-threaded test_threadgroup run
passes 15 of 17 tests: constructing a Thread from a worker still encounters
the Main-owned os module in Thread.__init__, and the existing exception test
installs a Main-owned bound-method hook. The sequence-access suite still has
ten error cases involving fixture/native-library sharing; a diagnostic traced
sqlite connection creation to the Main-owned _lru_cache_wrapper factory in
functools. These integration issues need resolution as the remaining sharing
foundations and acquisition checks are implemented. They are not waived or
counted as successful tests. An initial broad command also named the nonexistent
test_object_access; later runs used the actual sequence/subscription suites.
A temporary missing SHORT_TIMEOUT import in the fork-test subprocess was
corrected and that regression now passes. git diff --check passed.

The follow-up to global-load validation resolves the ten sequence-access error
cases rather than bypassing the new boundary. Native test wrappers used across
groups are explicitly declared synchronized; the Python sequence fixture class
is frozen. The unraisable-exception test installs a hook created in the worker
and records only the exception type comparison, restoring the original hook in
finally. The existing weak-reference, local-success and foreign-rejection
assertions remain in place. Shareable C API tests likewise publish their
specific native testing entry points as synchronized functions so they reach
the object under test rather than failing on the helper module.

Two runtime paths needed additional sharing support. First, __shareable__ now
returns an existing frozen Shareable singleton by name instead of invoking
Enum.__call__, which read Main-owned enum implementation objects. The Shareable
class itself is frozen. Second, the immutable native _lru_cache_wrapper type is
declared immutable and functools' private CacheInfo class is frozen. Generated
namedtuple constructors load tuple.__new__ from their globals, so __new__
wrappers created for managed static builtin types are explicitly synchronized.
The gate is _Py_TPFLAGS_STATIC_BUILTIN; this does not implicitly declare
extension-type constructors synchronized. Cache instances remain local.

Tests exercise repeated state queries in worker groups, publication of tuple,
list and dict constructor entry points, correct ownership of newly constructed
values, and creation/use/clearing of an LRU cache in a worker, including its
CacheInfo constructor. The exception-path test uses a synchronized function
with a shared result Channel instead of a Main-owned bound-method hook. The
native ExceptHookArgs type is frozen before publication so every group can
construct an exception record. The callback reports an immutable result and
the test still verifies that another thread can run in the same group after
an exception.

All three debug builds succeeded. The GIL and free-threaded builds each passed
624 sequence-access, Shareable, functools and threading tests (six and seven
skips, respectively). Tier 2 passed 699 sequence-access, Shareable, functools
and optimizer tests with five skips. Sequence-access and Shareable -R 3:3
passed in both ordinary builds without reported leaks. The new LRU-cache and
exception-hook tests passed -R 3:3 under free-threading and Tier 2; Tier 2's
LRU test had block deltas [1, 1, 0], within the runner's tolerance, and no
reference leak. Native machine-code JIT execution remains unvalidated.

The final ThreadGroup run now passes 16 of 17 tests. The remaining failure is
test_default_group_is_not_inherited: constructing a Thread in a worker reaches
the Main-owned os module in Thread.__init__. Its environment, name-counter,
class/factory and registry dependencies still need a complete sharing design.
This failure and the broader unfinished PEP requirements remain open; the
successful suites above do not constitute completion of the reference
implementation.

Python-defined classes now expose type.synchronize(). It converts the actual
namespace in place to SynchronizedDict, invalidates the type's caches before
publishing synchronized state, and preserves existing dictionary aliases.
__dict__ remains a mapping proxy so class writes continue through the normal
slot-update and cache-invalidation protocol. Native types and native metaclass
storage require explicit support. Synchronization remains shallow: bases,
instances and values are not recursively synchronized. A temporary frozen flag
blocks reentrant mutation during the namespace conversion. Class freeze now
checks and begins its transition under the class critical section, including
rechecking whether another group has already completed freezing.

Eight class-synchronization tests cover namespace identity, worker mutation,
Python metaclasses, shallow values, rejected transitions, freezing after
synchronization and coordinated slot updates between two groups. The Tier 2
regression first requires an executor that has folded the actual class
attribute into an inline constant, then synchronizes the class, changes the
attribute in another group and verifies the new result in both groups. This
checks invalidation rather than merely the presence of any executor.

threading's Python classes explicitly opt into synchronization. Thread-name
allocation now uses a Python counter protected by _active_limbo_lock instead
of a Main-owned count.__next__ bound method. The existing callable _counter
interface remains intact, including test_threading's name-counter mocks. A
four-group test allocates 400 names and checks uniqueness. The rejected-module
synchronization test now checks preservation of the original namespace type;
sys already legitimately has a synchronized namespace. Dictionary-subclass
freezing copies actual native storage with _PyDict_CopyStorage, avoiding an
overridden keys method. The existing callback-rejection test exercises this.

A further regression demonstrated that an object.__setattr__ audit hook could
freeze a class and the pending __name__ assignment would still succeed. The
special-attribute validation helper now rechecks mutability after the audit
callback. A subprocess regression checks six attributes (__name__, __qualname__,
__module__, __doc__, __bases__, and __type_params__), both ordinary assignment
and direct descriptor assignment, on local and synchronized classes. Each
assignment must fail and preserve the original attribute after the hook freezes
the class. This fixes that reentrant path; it is not a complete audit of
concurrent special setters or reentrant MRO changes.

The final three debug builds succeeded. The GIL and free-threaded builds each
passed all nine selected suites: test_freeze, test_synchronize_class,
test_freeze_dict, test_freeze_module, test_synchronize_module, test_descr,
test_type_cache, test_types and test_threading (679 tests, five and six skips,
respectively). Tier 2 passed test_freeze, test_synchronize_class and the full
optimizer suite (369 tests, three skips). Earlier class-synchronization -R 3:3
runs passed in both ordinary builds without reported leaks; the new optimizer
regression also passed -R 3:3 with block deltas [0, 1, 0], within the runner's
tolerance. Native machine-code JIT execution remains unvalidated.

Thread construction in a worker is still unfinished: Thread.__init__ reads the
Main-owned os module for PYTHON_PARALLEL. Dummy-thread creation additionally
reaches the Main-owned native deque factory in Condition, and the weak thread
registry and context dependencies still need a sharing design. The class and
counter changes do not claim to solve those failures. Existing mapping proxies
created before a class transition also still need state propagation. Protective
locks, full acquisition-boundary coverage, function closure analysis and the
other outstanding requirements listed above remain necessary for completion.

After the freeze critical-section and audit-hook fixes, test_freeze and
test_synchronize_class together passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary builds
(48 tests). No reference leaks were reported. test_freeze's block deltas were
[0, 0, 1] in free-threading and [1, 0, 0] with the GIL, both explicitly accepted
by the runner; class synchronization had no positive measured deltas.
git diff --check passed.

Class annotation name resolution now checks the result of
LOAD_FROM_DICT_OR_GLOBALS before publishing the stack reference. Its exact-dict
global/builtin fallback used _PyDict_LoadGlobal directly and bypassed the
existing checked mapping helpers. Two regressions failed before the fix,
returning a foreign value from an annotation function, and pass afterward.
They create both the namespace dictionaries and the annotation function in the
worker group; copying a shared namespace preserves the foreign value's owner.
This isolates acquisition of that value from access to a foreign function or
namespace. Both globals and builtins use exact dictionaries so the tests
exercise the fast path rather than the already-checked generic fallback.
Repeated rejected loads must raise IllegalThreadAccessException, local and
immutable replacement values must work, and a deleted name must still raise
NameError. Weak references verify that rejected loads release their references.

The bytecode source and generated interpreter cases were regenerated together.
This instruction is already excluded from Tier 2 because it has both popping
and non-popping error paths; the Tier 2 build falls back to the checked Tier 1
instruction. No new native machine-code JIT claim is made. The language-reference
note now includes global/builtin fallbacks in class annotation scopes.

GIL and free-threaded builds each passed 361 tests covering global access,
annotationlib, type annotations, type aliases, type parameters and scopes.
The full nine-test global-access suite passed -R 3:3 in both builds without
reported reference or block leaks. These changes do not cover cell loads,
general attribute acquisition or the other remaining boundaries.
The Tier 2 interpreter build passed the same suites plus the full optimizer
suite: 682 tests, three skips. All three builds succeeded, and git diff --check
passed. The full PEP 805 reference implementation remains incomplete.

Cell acquisition now validates values at LOAD_DEREF (both interpreter tiers),
LOAD_FROM_DICT_OR_DEREF, PyCell_Get and the cell_contents descriptor. Internal
raw cell helpers and the unchecked macro retain their existing contracts;
cell mutation ownership and readonly-closure classification remain separate
unfinished requirements. Empty C API cells still return NULL without an error,
empty descriptors raise ValueError and empty closure loads raise NameError.

The new cell-access regression constructs functions and cells in the worker,
then repeatedly rejects a Main-owned value held in those cells through each
of the four boundaries. Replacement with a worker-local object or immutable
integer succeeds. Empty cells preserve their original behavior and a weak
reference checks that rejected loads release their acquired references. The
C API test wrapper preserves the distinction between an empty cell and an
error. The global-access fixtures now freeze their shared Python class and
capture an immutable code object rather than a Main-local self-referencing
function, preserving their existing assertions under the new closure checks.

Initial generated LOAD_DEREF error handling spilled the output reference
before explicitly closing it, causing a double decref during unwinding. The
final implementation holds the acquisition in a separate temporary stack
reference and only assigns the instruction output after validation. Generated
Tier 1 and Tier 2 code was inspected and repeated rejection/lifetime tests pass.

A dedicated Tier 2 test warms a worker-owned closure while its cell contains
an allowed value, verifies the actual executor contains _LOAD_DEREF, then
replaces the cell contents with a foreign value and requires rejection. The
worker inspects its own executor and sends immutable opcode names to Main;
Main does not read worker-owned executor objects. Cleanup invalidates the
fresh code's executors and clears the executor deletion queue.

All three debug builds passed 362 tests covering cell/global access, scopes,
annotationlib, type annotations, aliases and parameters. Cell/global access
(10 tests) passed -R 3:3 in GIL and free-threaded builds without reported leaks.
The dedicated Tier 2 regression passed normally and with -R 3:3; its block
deltas [0, 1, 0] were within runner tolerance, with no reference leak reported.
These results do not establish a clean full suite. The full optimizer run now
reports 10 errors among 322 tests (three skips), and threading integration
also exposes foreign captured values. These failures are not waived: fixture
sharing, runtime exception-hook dependencies and remaining closure semantics
need follow-up before the reference implementation can be complete.

The verbose optimizer rerun identifies the ten errors in the earlier boundary
tests: for-iteration (list/tuple), GET_AITER, GET_ANEXT, GET_AWAITABLE, inlined
coroutine return, inlined for return, inlined generator yield, and virtual SEND
(list/tuple). Their worker setup still captures foreign values/classes before
reaching the boundary under test. Their assertions must be preserved while
making setup legal; they are not counted as passes. The new cell test passed.

The free-threaded integration run passed test_threading (245 tests, five skips).
test_thread_shareable has four errors: dummy-thread creation, namespace payload
ownership, parallel attribute writes and native local isolation. Inspection
shows the latter ownership tests capture the Main-owned _testinternalcapi
module; parallel writes capture a still-local Barrier instance. Barrier sharing
requires implementation, not just declaring an arbitrary instance synchronized.
test_threadgroup timed out at test_native_thread_handle_parallel_join. The
exception-reporting paths additionally encounter foreign captured values while
reporting worker failures. These are concrete outstanding integration work.
Final git diff --check passed. Native machine-code JIT remains unvalidated.

Condition, Event, Semaphore, BoundedSemaphore, Barrier and the Python RLock
fallback now use _ThreadBase's native synchronized attribute storage. These
implementations retain their existing lock protocols for compound operations;
this is not a blanket declaration that arbitrary Python object storage is
thread safe. Additional native subclass storage retains the existing local
fallback. Condition stores its waiters in SynchronizedList and resolves lock
acquire/release/locked and optional recursive-lock methods in the calling group.
It no longer keeps bound methods owned by its creator. The existing custom-lock
fallbacks remain. The list queue preserves notification order and timeout
removal behavior; large waiter queues still need performance evaluation because
removing the first item shifts list storage.

New tests check synchronized instance/dictionary state, transfer identity,
recursive Condition waiting across groups with native and Python RLocks,
restored recursion depth and semaphore handoff across groups. The existing
parallel attribute-write test again reaches its assertions using a real shared
Barrier. Ownership-check tests publish the specific audited C test functions
as synchronized instead of capturing the Main-owned testing module.

ThreadGroup test result buffers and the non-atomic read/modify/write counter
are synchronized lists. The counter test still requires group serialization:
individual list operations do not make counter[0] += 1 atomic. The attached-C
parallelism test publishes its native barrier function and its two-byte buffer
as synchronized: only that helper accesses the bytes, using atomic operations,
and exported buffers pin the storage. It still requires both attached native
threads to meet without releasing execution rights, preserving the distinction
between parallel execution and ordinary interpreter interleaving.

The free-threaded ThreadGroup run improved from six failures to one: 17 of 18
tests pass, including native-handle parallel join and group-release-on-wait.
Thread sharing improved from four errors to one: 12 of 13 tests pass, including
all three new primitive tests. Remaining failures are not waived. Constructing
Thread in a worker still reads the Main-owned os module for PYTHON_PARALLEL.
A diagnostic now traces dummy-thread creation to Thread.__init__ loading
_make_invoke_excepthook: that factory has cell variables but no captured free
variables, and the current conservative func_can_share classification marks
it local. Local cells created separately for each invocation need to be
distinguished from captured closure cells. The weak thread registry and later
constructor dependencies still need auditing after that correction.

Final validation rebuilt all three debug configurations. test_threading passed
in GIL, free-threaded and Tier 2 builds. The combined ordinary-build runs each
ran 286 tests, with the two constructor-related failures above still present;
they are not clean runs. Cell/global-access tests remained green. The Tier 2
combined run likewise retained those two failures among 276 tests. Six selected
primitive/ownership tests passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary builds without reported
reference or block leaks. A subsequently added worker-construction test creates
and exercises each primitive inside a new group; it passed -R 3:3 in all three
builds without reported leaks. That adds one passing test to the earlier
12-of-13 thread-sharing result. The optimizer fixture errors from the previous
turn remain outstanding; this turn addressed the runtime sharing prerequisite.
git diff --check passed. Native machine-code JIT remains unvalidated.

Function classification now distinguishes per-invocation cell variables from
captured free variables. func_can_share no longer rejects co_ncellvars: those
cells are created independently on every call and are not stored in the
function object. Nonempty attached closure storage and free variables still
keep a function local under the current conservative classification. This is
not the unfinished readonly-capture analysis. The construction and code/closure
replacement paths all use the same predicate.

A regression shares a factory with cell variables but no free variables across
four groups. Each group creates two mutable closures, synchronizes with a
Barrier and mutates one closure 101 times. The other closure and the other
groups' closures retain independent state. Returned closures remain local.
Code-replacement tests now require the factory function to remain synchronized
and preserve its synchronized namespace; they continue checking the locally
returned closure. Failed closure conversion still preserves code, closure and
namespace, including after a rejected code replacement with incompatible free
variable count. The C API documentation describes the per-call distinction.

Function tests publish their specific audited native helper entry points as
synchronized rather than capturing the entire Main-owned test module. Foreign
mutation tests now pass their existing references directly to a setter driver.
They no longer allow an earlier LOAD_DEREF failure inside the try block to
masquerade as a setter rejecting the target. Python and C setters must each
reject the foreign function/namespace themselves, and valid namespace updates,
alias preservation, metadata lifetime and warning tests remain intact.

All three debug builds succeeded and each passed 132 tests across function
sharing, function mutation, function attributes, scope, cell access and global
access. A direct dummy-thread diagnostic now passes _make_invoke_excepthook and
fails at _dangling.add(self), the Main-owned weak thread registry. Worker Thread
construction's os/environment dependency, that weak registry, readonly closure
analysis and the previously recorded optimizer fixture failures remain open.
Function sharing and mutation (42 tests) passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary builds
without reported leaks. The new factory test also passed -R 3:3 under Tier 2
without reported leaks. git diff --check passed. These results do not establish
completion of PEP 805 or validation of native machine-code JIT execution.

threading's _dangling registry now uses a private _ThreadRegistry with the
native synchronized namespace base and SynchronizedSet storage. Registration
creates weak references with a closure-free removal callback whose default
argument is the shared set. There is no captured Main-owned WeakSet instance.
Iteration snapshots the set, so registrations and collection callbacks can
run concurrently. copy, clear, update and equality preserve the operations
used by the test runner's environment tracking. Copies create their own weak
references/removal callbacks and do not keep the registered objects alive.
The existing fork enumeration continues through the iterable registry.

New exact weakrefs to synchronized targets are published as synchronized.
Their state is set before linking them into the target's weakref list, using
only atomic metadata access while the list lock is held. Taking an unrelated
object critical section there would create an unsafe lock-order dependency.
Weakref subclasses retain local state; proxies and propagation to already
existing weakrefs are not implemented by this change. Callbacks retain their
own sharing policies. This is a declaration for exact weakref native storage,
not for arbitrary extension storage.

A synchronized function can later become local when closure storage is attached.
Therefore both weakref calls and PyWeakref_GetRef validate the live target's
current access state before returning it. Rejected acquisitions release their
new reference; PyWeakref_GetRef clears its output and returns -1 rather than
misreporting the target as dead. Internal raw weakref helpers retain their
contracts. Legacy borrowed-reference APIs and remaining proxy boundaries still
need a separate audit.

Tests cover exact/subclass reference state, weak lifetime and duplicate
registration, copied-registry lifetime, four-group concurrent registration of
400 objects and callback removal, and repeated rejection after a shared target
becomes local. That last test checks both Python calls and the C API and verifies
the referent is collected afterward. The pre-existing dummy-thread test now
passes: dummy construction has crossed the previously Main-owned registry.
All 18 thread-sharing tests pass, including worker primitive construction and
native local isolation.

All three debug builds succeeded and passed test_thread_shareable, test_weakref
and test_threading: 402 tests, nine skips in free-threading and eight in GIL and
Tier 2 builds. All 18 thread-sharing tests passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary
builds; no reference leaks were reported. GIL block deltas [0, 1, 0] were within
the runner's tolerance, while free-threading reported no positive deltas.
The separate ThreadGroup run still passes 17 of 18 tests: ordinary Thread
construction in a worker retains the os/environment access failure. The full
optimizer fixture errors, readonly-closure analysis, protective locks and the
other recorded PEP requirements remain open. Native machine-code JIT remains
unvalidated. git diff --check passed.

The Python environment mapping now has synchronized instance storage, and
posix/nt environment snapshots allocate SynchronizedDict. On Unix, os.environ
and os.environb retain their shared backing dictionary; the Windows uppercase
mapping also uses SynchronizedDict. Encoding helpers no longer capture local
closure cells: they read the immutable filesystem-encoding string from the
module namespace, retaining the import-time encoding choice and original
one-argument callback signatures. _Environ's class and the os module namespace
are synchronized explicitly. Module reload preserves namespace identity and
avoids trying to synchronize an already synchronized module again.

Thread.__init__ continues reading the current os.environ mapping. It does not
cache PYTHON_PARALLEL or a particular mapping in threading. Tests coordinate
Main and a worker while changing the value through empty, zero and nonzero
values; groups are selected afresh each time. Another test replaces os.environ
with a shared mapping and verifies that the worker observes the replacement.
Tests also verify shared environ/environb storage and module reload. Existing
-E behavior is retained. Native environment mutation and reload functions
are not declared synchronized by this change; this enables safe Python-side
reads without claiming that libc environment operations are universally safe.
Windows code was updated but is not runtime-validated on this Linux host.

All ThreadGroup tests now pass, including the previously failing construction
of a Thread in a worker. This does not yet prove the whole child-thread
lifecycle: a separate diagnostic creates a child successfully in a worker but
its start() still reaches the Main-owned _contextvars module at the context
creation/copy step. Context entry points, context ownership and propagation
need further integration. The older optimizer fixture errors, readonly closure
analysis and remaining PEP requirements are still open.

Validation rebuilt the frozen os module in all three debug configurations.
The broader run passed 843 tests (ThreadGroup, thread sharing, os, posix and
threading) in each build, with 71 skips. After the final encoding-helper
adjustment, the relevant ThreadGroup/os/posix suites again passed in all three
builds: 580 tests, 66 skips under free-threading and 67 under GIL/Tier 2. The
22-test ThreadGroup suite passed -R 3:3 in both ordinary builds without reported
reference or block leaks (one GIL-only skip). git diff --check passed. Native
machine-code JIT and Windows execution remain unvalidated.

Context sharing now completes the previously blocked worker-created child
Thread start/join path. _contextvars publishes its audited copy_context
function and readonly native types with shared states and synchronizes its
namespace; the public contextvars wrapper also synchronizes its namespace.
New and reused native Context instances are synchronized. Their existing
critical sections retain snapshots of the immutable HAMT, and their native
entry flag still excludes concurrent entry. ContextVar instances and the
Token.MISSING singleton are immutable; Token instances remain local.

Sharing does not recursively change values. PyContextVar_Get validates the
selected stored/default value before returning a new reference and preserves
its NULL output on error. Context subscription/get and Token.old_value also
check their returned references. ContextVar repr checks both the name and
default before invoking their repr; HAMT equality checks values before invoking
comparison. Copying a context preserves its values' ownership. Identity-only
HAMT equality can still return without acquiring or invoking the values.

Seven new tests cover state declarations, worker-created child start/join in
both default and explicit groups, foreign stored/default/token values,
foreign-name repr callbacks, sequential and concurrent entry, and simultaneous
updates versus snapshot reads in separate groups. The existing context,
free-threading context, ThreadGroup and threading suites passed in all three
