builds (329 tests in the initial free-threading run; 330 in GIL and Tier 2 after
adding two tests, with the free-threading-only suite skipped there). The final
seven sharing tests passed -R 3:3 under all three configurations without
reported reference or block leaks. GIL and Tier 2 also passed the existing
context suite under -R 3:3 (64 tests together, one skip). Final free-threading
context suites passed 58 tests with one skip after synchronizing the public
wrapper. git diff --check passed.

This closes the recorded _contextvars startup failure, not the full PEP.
General callable/attribute boundaries, readonly-closure classification,
protective locks, existing optimizer fixture failures and the other listed
requirements remain open. Native machine-code JIT and Windows remain
unvalidated.

Restored the optimizer access fixtures after the LOAD_DEREF integration.
Foreign payloads now enter workers as existing argument references, so cell
loads cannot fail before the intended instruction. Await/async-iteration
fixture classes are frozen after fresh method code is installed; their wrapper
has no captured local cells. All opcode/executor assertions, rejection results
and post-rejection generator behavior remain checked. The full Tier 2 optimizer
suite now passes 322 tests (three skips), resolving the ten previously recorded
fixture errors. Its 12 access tests pass -R 3:3 without reported reference
leaks; memory block deltas [-2, 3, -2] have negative total and are accepted by
the runner. This does not validate machine-code JIT.

Specialized subscription fixtures likewise receive payloads as arguments,
freeze their helper classes, and explicitly synchronize the native thread-local
bytecode inspection helper instead of capturing its local module. Both tests,
including return-monitoring assertions, pass -R 3:3 in free-threading, GIL and
Tier 2. Tier 2's block deltas [0, 0, 1] are within tolerance; no reference leaks
were reported.

Public sequence/mapping C API fixtures now pass existing container references
as worker arguments. Previously some tests stopped at LOAD_DEREF; the foreign
sequence-element test could even count that earlier rejection as an API
success. Native test helpers are explicitly synchronized. Custom subscription
and slice objects are built in their worker, with payloads stored as attributes
rather than captured in cells. Native resource/termios/sqlite entry points used
by these tests are explicit shared aliases, and the Counter fixture explicitly
opts in its class namespace and native counting helper. This is test setup,
not a blanket declaration that the corresponding extension modules are safe
for cross-group use. Rejection counts, unraisable-error contracts, shared
results, unchanged OS state and weakref lifetime checks are retained.
All 19 sequence tests pass -R 3:3 in all three builds, with the unavailable
_decimal test skipped and no reported reference or block leaks.

A broader diagnostic still finds failures in test_iterator_access. Its older
fixtures require the same boundary audit; a green optimizer suite does not
establish that all access paths or all fixtures are correct. General call and
attribute checks, protective locks and the other full-PEP requirements remain
open. git diff --check passed.

The remaining iterator fixtures have been audited for premature cell/module
access failures. Workers receive existing foreign references as arguments;
producer generators/coroutines receive those references as parameters instead
of capturing them in cells. Iterator factories keep returned references in
instance storage. Fixture classes are frozen and stateless native test helpers
are explicitly synchronized. Opcode checks, rejection counts, producer
continuation/exhaustion checks, monitoring assertions and weakref lifetime
checks remain intact. The original 20 iterator tests passed -R 3:3 under all
three builds with no reported reference leaks; Tier 2's [0, 0, 2] block deltas
were within tolerance. All 41 iterator/sequence/subscription tests then passed
in all three builds (one unavailable _decimal skip).

Auditing anext exposed two distinct gaps. Its Python implementation first
failed even on worker-local iterators because its sentinel no-default marker
was Main-owned. After making that marker immutable, a diagnostic demonstrated
that anext returned a foreign __anext__ result without checking access. The
bootstrap module now receives a private native _check_access function before
its functions are published into builtins. anext checks both its input and
its __anext__ result, before returning or wrapping the result. This helper is
not added to the public builtins namespace and needs no startup-time extension
import. The existing generator-completion check rejects a foreign default
when the default wrapper completes.

Sentinels now implement shallow __freeze__. Freezing prevents writes/deletion
of __module__, including through the member descriptor, then publishes the
immutable state. repr and __reduce__ check returned name/custom-repr references;
string subclasses retain their own ownership. Ordinary sentinels remain local
and retain mutable __module__ until explicitly frozen. _pybuiltins freezes its
private sentinel at startup, preserving its name and representation. Two tests
cover sentinel state/idempotence, direct descriptor mutation rejection and
foreign name/repr acquisition. Two anext tests cover worker-local success,
exhaustion defaults, 500 input/result/default rejections, non-invocation of
foreign iterator/awaitable callbacks and weakref cleanup. The older freeze
callback-rejection fixture also now passes its object as an argument and uses
shared result storage, preventing an earlier LOAD_DEREF failure from replacing
the intended freeze-boundary test.

All three configurations rebuilt the frozen _pybuiltins module. Freeze,
iterator-access, builtin and async-generator suites passed 321 tests in each
build (eight skips). The five changed sentinel/anext/freeze-owner tests passed
-R 3:3 in all three configurations without reported reference or block leaks.
git diff --check passed. The recorded iterator fixture failures are resolved;
this does not establish full PEP conformance. General callable and attribute
boundaries, producer monitoring, protective locks, reclamation and the other
listed requirements remain open. Native machine-code JIT and Windows remain
unvalidated.

Public attribute acquisition now checks returned references in
PyObject_GetAttr/GetAttrString and PyObject_GetOptionalAttr/GetOptionalAttrString.
This covers both the legacy tp_getattr path and the optional API's independent
generic/type/module fast paths. A rejected owned result is released; optional
getters set their output to NULL and return -1 without suppressing the access
exception. HasAttrWithError inherits this error behavior, while legacy HasAttr
retains its unraisable-error report and false return contract. Ordinary getattr
and hasattr calls through these APIs receive the same checks.

The first regression run exposed import initialization reading an inaccessible
Main-owned __spec__ through the newly checked API, including while resolving
__shareable__. Exact module initialization now uses the internal module getter
for this bookkeeping reference and still checks _initializing separately. It
does not publish the spec to Python or change the spec's ownership. A regression
test imports threading and reads the current thread's sharing state in a worker,
then confirms that public getattr(threading, '__spec__') is still rejected.
Other custom module objects continue through the public optional API.

Four attribute-access tests exercise instance dictionaries, native slot members,
properties, custom getattribute/getattr, class attributes, module attributes,
and legacy native tp_getattr. The new test-only legacy type owns and traverses
its payload and returns an unchecked new reference, ensuring the public API
itself rejects it. Tests check 700 repeated ordinary getter rejections and 400
legacy getter rejections, allowed local/immutable values, missing attributes,
optional NULL-on-error assertions in the existing C wrappers, legacy HasAttr
unraisable behavior and weakref cleanup. No earlier closure lookup is allowed
to substitute for the tested boundary.

All three debug builds passed the final attribute, thread sharing, ThreadGroup,
context sharing, abstract C API and importlib suites: 1,323 tests, 20 skips per
build. The four new attribute tests passed -R 3:3 in free-threading, GIL and
Tier 2 without reported reference or block leaks. git diff --check passed.
This is not complete attribute coverage: PyObject_GenericGetAttr, independent
VM stackref/specialized attribute paths, direct slots and other acquisition
boundaries remain work, alongside the other listed PEP requirements. Native
machine-code JIT and Windows are still unvalidated.

Attribute validation now extends to PyObject_GenericGetAttr, the VM stackref
getter and the LOAD_ATTR family. A separate _CHECK_ATTR_ACCESS uop checks the
loaded attribute while preserving the optional method-call stack entry. It is
appended to ordinary and specialized attribute-load macros and remains present
when Tier 2 replaces a class attribute load with a constant. Property and
getattribute frames instead set check_return_access, reusing the receiver-side
return check and its monitoring ordering. Generated interpreter, executor,
optimizer and opcode metadata files were regenerated. The type stackref fast
path roots deferred references in a C stack reference while constructing an
access error, then clears or returns them without losing ownership.

Four new VM tests use native joinable threads and cell-free entry functions to
isolate instruction behavior from unresolved Thread callback storage. They
exercise cold and warmed instance/slot/module/class loads, specialized
attribute calls, inlined property/getattribute returns, explicit generic C
getter access, and deferred function-reference cleanup. Opcode assertions,
100 repeated denials, allowed-value recovery, callback non-invocation and
weakref cleanup are checked. All four passed -R 3:3 in free-threading, GIL and
Tier 2 without reported reference or block leaks. Two dedicated Tier 2 tests
verify the retained check after actual class-value constant folding and
inlined property/getattribute return opcodes, reaching the backedge with an
allowed object before a foreign return. They passed -R 3:3; block deltas
[0, 1, 0] were within tolerance and no reference leaks were reported.

The standard builtin, descriptor, opcache, monitoring and abstract C API suites
passed 558 tests (nine skips) under free-threading. GIL and Tier 2 runs including
the first three VM tests passed 561 tests with nine and 33 skips respectively.
The final deferred-reference change is covered by the four-test leak runs.
git diff --check passed. Native machine-code JIT remains unvalidated.

This change deliberately exposes substantial integration gaps that the earlier
unchecked attribute paths concealed. A free-threading diagnostic run of the
existing public attribute tests and ThreadGroup tests failed: four attribute
suite errors, then six errors and five failures in ThreadGroup. Direct native
worker probes confirm that a Main-created Thread's captured-function _target,
local _kwargs dictionary, _invoke_excepthook closure and _stderr object are
inaccessible; its default tuple _args is accessible. Do not treat previous
whole-ThreadGroup successes as evidence for the current branch. These failures
remain to be resolved through readonly-closure/function sharing and appropriate
Thread/internal-I/O state handling, not by removing the new attribute checks.
LOAD_SUPER_ATTR, direct slot and general call boundaries also remain, along
with protective locks, producer monitoring, reclamation and the other full-PEP
requirements. The implementation is still incomplete.

Code construction now derives a per-binding NONLOCAL_WRITE flag from original
bytecode and nested code constants. STORE_DEREF and DELETE_DEREF on free
bindings propagate through intermediate scopes to the owning cell. Child-owned
cells do not propagate by name, preserving lexical shadowing. The analysis
handles EXTENDED_ARG and skips instruction caches, and recomputes flags on
code.replace and marshal reconstruction. Frame locals tests use bit masks so
the added binding flag does not hide free variables.

Function creation and stopped-world code/closure replacement now keep outer
functions LOCAL when descendants can rebind their cells, as required by the
PEP's nonlocal example. The previous unconditional allowance for per-call
cells was too broad. Cells only assigned by their own function still permit
sharing the factory. Nonempty captured closures remain conservatively LOCAL:
this does not yet solve readonly captures, sibling/enclosing writer provenance,
direct cell mutation or the Thread integration failures described above.

The eight closure-analysis tests cover direct/transitive writes, class scopes,
shadowing, deletion, own-cell assignment, extended operands, reconstruction and
code mutation. Together with code, scope, frame and marshal suites, all three
debug builds passed 236 tests (17 skips each). The eight new tests and two
updated function-state tests passed -R 3:3 in all builds, with no reported
reference leaks. Tier 2 reported tolerated block deltas [0, 1, 0] for the
function tests. git diff --check passed. These targeted results do not resolve
the known whole-branch Thread failures; native machine-code JIT and Windows
remain unvalidated.

Super attribute acquisition now validates the descriptor returned by MRO
lookup before binding or invoking it. The direct super getattribute slot checks
its result as well. LOAD_SUPER_ATTR_ATTR and LOAD_SUPER_ATTR_METHOD retain
_CHECK_ATTR_ACCESS after lookup, preserving the callable/receiver stack during
errors and retaining the check when Tier 2 folds a method lookup to a constant.
The generic and instrumented paths use the checked public attribute getter.
Generated interpreter, executor, optimizer and opcode metadata were refreshed.

A native-thread test covers ordinary stored attributes, callable objects,
closure-bearing methods, local descriptors returning foreign attributes or
callables, and foreign descriptors that must never execute. Each case checks
cold lookup, an asserted specialized opcode, 100 denials, successful recovery
and weakref cleanup. Explicit super.__getattribute__ is also covered. Helpers
are defined outside a class to avoid an implicit __class__ capture, and payload
storage avoids earlier checked cell reads. Worker failure diagnostics now
include traceback function names and line numbers.

Free-threading passed the five attribute VM tests and 388 standard super,
opcache, monitoring and descriptor tests (one skip). GIL and Tier 2 passed
393 tests, with one and 25 skips respectively. The new super test passed
-R 3:3 in all three builds without reported leaks. A dedicated Tier 2 test
establishes the receiver type, verifies the actual foreign-method constant and
retained _CHECK_ATTR_ACCESS in the optimized trace, then verifies 100 worker
rejections without calling the method. It passed -R 3:3 with tolerated block
deltas [0, 1, 0] and no reported reference leaks. These results cover
Tier 2's interpreter backend, not native machine-code JIT. git diff --check
passed. The readonly-closure, Thread integration, protective-lock and remaining
full-PEP gaps above are still outstanding.

Cell mutation now validates access to the cell and its frozen state before
changing contents. PyCell_Set is the checked common path for the public C API,
cell_contents setter/deleter and frame-locals proxy cell updates. STORE_DEREF
and DELETE_DEREF check before stealing the new reference or removing the old
one; generated interpreter/executor code preserves the live input on failure.
Internal raw cell helpers and the explicitly unchecked PyCell_SET macro retain
their existing contracts. Cell comparison acquires contents through checked
PyCell_Get, releasing any first operand if the second operand is rejected,
while preserving empty-cell ordering. This prevents invoking comparison
callbacks on inaccessible contents.

These checks are a prerequisite for refining closure sharing, not a readonly
closure implementation. Cell provenance, enclosing/sibling writes and the
classification of functions built with replacement cells remain pending.

Class creation now propagates PyCell_Set failures for __classcell__ and
__classdictcell__ rather than discarding the return value. The internal
_CheckMutable symbol is exported through the internal header for generated
_testinternalcapi instruction tests; no public cell layout or C API signature
changed. An initial build exposed the missing export and skipped one test;
that build is superseded by the successful rebuilds and unskipped runs below.

Four native-thread tests exercise Python/C setters, STORE_DEREF/DELETE_DEREF,
frame-local cell writes, class-cell initialization and both comparison operand
orders. They verify repeated denials, unchanged original contents, allowed
same-group writes/deletes, empty-cell behavior, callback non-invocation and
reference cleanup. All three debug builds passed the new tests plus closure
analysis, scope, frame, function attributes and super: 200 tests, eight skips
per build (none in the four new tests). The four new tests passed -R 3:3 in all
builds without reported reference or block leaks. A Tier 2 test warms code
containing STORE_DEREF or DELETE_DEREF, asserts those uops in the trace, skips
the first loop iteration's mutation and tries replacement foreign cells. Each
case rejects 100 writes without changing the cell. It passed -R 3:3 with no
reference leaks and tolerated block deltas [0, 1, 0]. git diff --check passed.
Readonly closure sharing, existing Thread integration failures, protective
locks and the remaining full-PEP requirements are still incomplete; native
machine-code JIT and Windows remain unvalidated.

An experimental native sys.monitoring.StopTheWorld context object now holds a
global cooperative pause across its Python body in both build modes. Per-thread
depth permits nesting and grants access to foreign local/protected objects.
Global internal pauses reuse the outer pause; interpreter-internal GC/mutation
pauses maintain their own stopped flag/depth for existing assertions and lock
rules. Ordinary Python runs with the interpreter-internal stopped flag clear.
The thread attachment loop now releases the GIL and ThreadGroup mutex before
waiting for a suspended state, allowing the debugger owner to detach and
reattach without deadlocking on a parked thread. Attach/detach state transitions
are atomic in both builds. Stale stop requests do not suspend the pause owner.

The singleton and its type are immutable, and the monitoring module namespace
is synchronized; its other native functions were not blanket-declared shared.
Function mutation ownership checks recognize the debugger privilege while
still rejecting immutable/frozen functions. Fork and interpreter lifecycle or
cross-interpreter execution operations are explicitly rejected during a pause.
A direct creation probe initially hung because the cross-interpreter wrapper
cleared the current tstate before reaching the lower-level guard. The probe
was terminated; guards now precede that detach and tests exercise creation,
switch, destruction and ID-decref rejection plus normal operation after exit.

Seven tests cover nesting, GC, execution-attribute mutation, exception resume,
scoped foreign acquisition/cell mutation, pause stability across sleep in the
same and different groups, new threads during pauses, competing pause owners,
and unsupported lifecycle operations. All seven passed -R 3:3 in all three
debug builds with no reported reference or block leaks. Initial Tier 2 runs
showed repeatable block growth: sys._clear_internal_caches invalidated traces
but left the resulting deferred deletion list. Direct measurements established
that processing the list after invalidation stabilizes allocation counts. The
cache-clear API now does so, preserving active executors through the existing
thread-state scan. Its existing test now starts with fresh code on each run,
so invalidation backoff from earlier leak-test iterations cannot skip trace
creation. All four executor-invalidation tests passed -R 3:3 in Tier 2.

The existing monitoring, GC, thread and threading suites passed 440 tests per
build (11 skips free-threading, five GIL/Tier 2). Those commands also contained
an invalid test_capi.test_pystate name, so their overall results were failures;
no failures occurred in the four actual suites. The correct test_capi.test_misc
suite then passed separately: 312 tests/three skips in free-threading and
310 tests/three skips in GIL and Tier 2. git diff --check passed.

This context API is explicitly incomplete for PEP safety: references retained
in locals after leaving the pause are not yet revalidated by the compiler.
Implement the appendix's with-scope local-load analysis and complete remaining
call/reference-acquisition boundaries before claiming conformance. Lifecycle
restrictions, native machine-code JIT/Windows, cross-interpreter/finalization
coverage and the rest of the full-PEP checklist also remain. No claim of a
complete reference implementation or clean whole-branch suite is made.

### With-scope fast-local access checks

The compiler now tags STORE_FAST and nullable comprehension-restoration stores
emitted within the current scope's with/async-with fblocks. CFG preprocessing
records all affected local indices, restores ordinary store opcodes, and owns
and frees that mask. After uninitialized-local analysis, before superinstruction
fusion and borrowed-load optimization, affected LOAD_FAST/LOAD_FAST_CHECK
instructions become LOAD_FAST_MAYBE_UNPROTECTED. The instruction preserves
UnboundLocalError and checks access before duplicating the local reference.
LOAD_FAST_AND_CLEAR_CHECK checks non-NULL values before moving them out of a
local; an error leaves the local unchanged. Nullable comprehension restoration
and outer-function locals continue to work. New opcode tables, interpreter and
optimizer cases were regenerated, and the bytecode magic was advanced to 3706.
The long PEP-specified opcode name uses dis's existing long-name formatting;
test_dis's list of allowed long names now includes it. New tests check actual
formatted operands as well as the emitted opcode.

This is deliberately conservative rather than the appendix's optimized
flow-sensitive algorithm: every read of a marked local is checked, even inside
the context or after safe reassignment. This prevents an inside-context read
from accidentally suppressing a check after exit. Removing redundant checks
requires tracking scope exits and exception edges. Locals in nested function
compilations have their own fblock stack. This change does not fix values kept
on the evaluation stack across context exit (including return expressions),
manual context-method calls, or general call/return reference boundaries.

Ten new tests in test_with_local_access exercise normal, nested, exceptional,
branched and loop exits; with-as targets; async-with; EXTENDED_ARG local indices;
unbound variables; and comprehension saves, including preservation of the local
on an access error. The raw native worker helper isolates these boundaries from
the still-unresolved Thread callback/kwargs integration. test_capi.test_opt also
warms two actual optimized loops, requires the corresponding checked micro-op
in each trace, and verifies 100 forbidden worker accesses for each operation.

### Tier 2 stolen-tuple reference accounting

Resolved the substantial reference-count growth exposed by the preceding
with-local tests. Disassembly was only the trigger: reducing the reproducer to
a generator unpacking tuples yielded by another generator reproduced it.
Compilation alone, native worker execution, and execution without the uop
optimizer did not. Heap-block growth was much smaller than reference-count
growth, and existing GC-tracked objects and opcode-name strings did not gain
matching references.

The unique-tuple unpack micro-ops move all elements out and call
_PyStolenTuple_Free directly. That helper freed/recycled the tuple without
consuming its own last reference in the debug accounting. It also omitted the
Py_TRACE_REFS removal and the reference-tracer destruction event. The helper
now asserts sole ownership, records the decrement, sets the reference count
to zero, removes the tracing record, and reports destruction before recycling
or freeing storage. It deliberately does not run tuple_dealloc, since doing so
would incorrectly decref elements which have already been moved out.

A new optimized-loop regression exercises 2-, 3- and 4-element unique tuple
unpacking, requires the actual specialized micro-op for each size, and checks
that shared mutable elements survive the transfer. Fresh code in every test
repetition avoids invalidation backoff disabling the path in refleak runs.
The test failed before the fix in the diagnostic tracing build with reference
deltas [84039, 84039, 84039], and passed after the fix in both Tier 2 builds with
no reference growth. Both reported only the familiar tolerated block delta
[0, 1, 0].

* The original test_with_local_access suite passed -R 5:5 in both Tier 2

### Unpacked-reference access checks

Specialized UNPACK_SEQUENCE_TWO_TUPLE, UNPACK_SEQUENCE_TUPLE and
UNPACK_SEQUENCE_LIST now append _CHECK_UNPACK_ACCESS. It validates the extracted
stack references before any assignment can consume them. Error unwinding owns
all extracted values, including after the input container is released. Tier 2's
replacement of the extraction operation with unique-tuple stealing leaves the
following check intact; the optimizer preserves the array's symbolic values.
Generated interpreter, executor, optimizer and metadata files were regenerated.

Generic unpacking already obtains individual values through checked
PyIter_Next. UNPACK_EX now also validates all trailing individual values before
stealing any out of its temporary rest list. On failure, the list still owns
all of its elements and existing cleanup releases it exactly once. The starred
list remains shallow: retaining inaccessible elements inside it is permitted,
while subsequently acquiring such an element is rejected.

Four new tests in test_unpack_access cover cold generic paths, warmed two- and
four-element tuples and lists with a forbidden reference at every position,
repeated rejection and successful recovery, starred prefix/tail acquisition,
shallow starred containers, and ordinary arity errors. The Tier 2 regression
requires both _UNPACK_SEQUENCE_UNIQUE_TWO_TUPLE and _CHECK_UNPACK_ACCESS in an
actual optimized trace, then requires 100 worker rejections with the innermost
traceback in the function containing the unpack (not in an earlier producer).
Its pre-existing argument references deliberately isolate this acquisition
boundary from the still-pending general call-boundary work.

### Held-mutex registry foundation

PyThreadState now owns a dynamically grown array of held protective mutex IDs.
ReserveHeldMutex grows capacity before an acquisition attempt; PushHeldMutex
registers an acquired mutex without allocation or Python execution. HoldsMutex
searches most-recent-first, rejects ID zero, and examines the supplied thread
state, not its ThreadGroup. RemoveHeldMutex removes the most recent matching
entry, preserving recursive acquisitions and the order of other entries.
Thread-state destruction frees the raw-allocated array, including before
recycling the interpreter's embedded initial thread state. The existing
protected-object access branch now succeeds when the protecting ID is present;
StopTheWorld still provides its separate temporary bypass.

This is an internal foundation, NOT working public protective locks. No normal
Lock/RLock method registers an ID yet, and neither protect() nor compound locks
exists. The C unit test fabricates a private protected list and synthetic IDs
without exposing them to Python; real callers must only register IDs after
actually acquiring the corresponding mutex. Mutations of the registry belong
to the owning thread, or to destruction of its retired thread state.

The new C test covers rejection before acquisition and after final release,
acceptance while registered, recursive registration, removal below 64 newer
entries, capacity growth, preserved ordering, ID zero and missing-ID handling.
A second zero-initialized thread-state fixture shares the same ThreadGroup but
has no held IDs, verifying separation of the registries. The Python companion
runs this exercise in four real Main-group threads and joins them, exercising
allocation and destruction of their independent thread states. The synthetic
fixture is not a test of a real protective mutex's mutual exclusion.

#### Constraints for the next native-lock integration

The PEP's operation table requires a sole-reference LOCAL argument for
protect(), and its API prose describes a protected copy. Enforce both rather
than silently allowing aliases or converting an aliased object in place.
See https://peps.python.org/pep-0805/#allowed-operations and the locks/API
sections. copy is already a synchronized module in this branch, but custom
copy results, instance dictionaries and unsupported native layouts still need
explicit ownership handling.

Ordinary Lock.release() can be called by a different thread. Merely writing a
context owner after _PyMutex_LockTimed returns is unsafe: another thread can
release and reacquire the lock before that metadata write, causing a stale
context to be treated as the owner when protect() is called. Acquire/release,
context-owner metadata and the first protective transition must form one
serialized protocol. Preserve ordinary cross-thread release until the lock is
protective; then reject acquire/release and all private bypasses as required.

One implementation route is a metadata critical section with nonblocking
native-mutex acquisition, followed on failure by parking on the lock state and
retrying. Release must clear ownership and unlock under the same metadata
section, then wake the matching waiters. Do not mix this protocol with native
mutex handoff waiters on the same address. Preserve timeout, signal,
finalization and fork behavior. A pending-call handler during a wait can change
the held-ID count: recheck/reserve capacity before the successful attempt, or
roll back the physical acquisition if registration cannot be committed.

RLock needs recursive context accounting and an audit of _release_save,
_acquire_restore and _at_fork_reinit. Compound locks need canonical ordering,
duplicate/reentrant handling and rollback of partial acquisition. IDs must
come from the existing non-recycling owner-ID allocator shared with
ThreadGroups. Lock lifetime, thread exit, protected-copy ownership, native
subclasses and complete escaped-reference checks remain unresolved. The new
registry must not be used as evidence that these requirements are implemented.

### Native primitive protective locks and borrowed argument uniqueness

Primitive Lock now has a globally allocated, non-recycled mutex ID and context
owner metadata (ThreadGroup ID plus thread-state ID). Acquisition, release and
the first protective transition serialize physical state and this metadata
under the lock object's critical section. Waiters compare-and-park on the
native mutex byte and retry; unlock wakes these waiters without mixing in
PyMutex's ownership-handoff protocol. Held-ID storage is reserved on each
context acquisition attempt, including retries after pending calls.

Lock.protect() requires the owning context and returns a shallow protected
copy of an exact list, dict or set. The first successful operation registers
the current owner and permanently makes the lock protective. Subsequent
explicit acquire/acquire_lock/release/release_lock and _at_fork_reinit are
rejected. Context exit removes the registered ID and must run in the owning
thread. Before conversion, ordinary cross-thread release still works and
invalidates any previous context owner. New native bound methods inherit a
protected receiver's owner/state so that the existing checked-local path can
reject a saved bound method outside the context.

An initially failing regression showed that a refcount of one is insufficient
for the protect() input condition: LOAD_FAST_BORROW can pass an argument still
owned by a local without incrementing its refcount. The fix uses the existing
PyUnstable_Object_IsUniqueReferencedTemporary API, which checks both physical
uniqueness and ownership of the reference on the operand stack. This avoids
changing every native call path or attempting to infer ownership from locals.
Like that API, the check is conservative and can reject an actually unique
argument when its provenance cannot be established. A retained source is
never converted in place; protect() still creates a separate shallow copy.

### Native recursive protective locks

Native RLock now allocates a mutex ID and records the ThreadGroup/thread-state
identity of its context owner. Like primitive Lock, all physical acquisition,
release and protection transitions serialize metadata under the object's
critical section. Acquisition tries the recursive mutex nonblocking under that
section; contended callers compare-and-park and retry. Every final unlock,
including ordinary _release_save, wakes these waiters. No positive-timeout or
ownership-handoff acquisition is mixed into this protocol.

The protected-copy helper is shared with primitive Lock, including its
borrow-aware unique-temporary input check. One held-ID entry covers the entire
outermost RLock acquisition. Recursion does not register duplicate entries,
and inner releases cannot revoke access; only the final release removes the
entry. This works when protect() is initially called inside nested contexts,
when multiple values use the same lock, and with interleaved recursive locks.

Protection can begin only when acquisitions since the last complete unlock
have exclusively used context entry in the same Python thread. Explicit
acquisition/release or private ownership restoration invalidates eligibility
until the next complete unlock. This conservative rule prevents adopting
outstanding manual acquisitions whose corresponding release would become
illegal after conversion. Fine-grained provenance of mixed acquisitions and
resumed ordinary Condition contexts is not represented yet.

After conversion, acquire/release, _release_save, _acquire_restore and
_at_fork_reinit reject bypasses. Foreign context exit is also rejected. An
ordinary _acquire_restore still uses the normal acquisition path, then
revalidates physical ownership and non-protective state under the metadata
section before restoring the saved owner/count. Ordinary fork reset clears
context metadata. Existing RLock and Condition behavior is retained when the
lock has not become protective. Condition.wait on a protective RLock raises
instead of suspending protection.

  _decimal remains the same missing optional module, with no import failures.

  passed: 306 tests with 5 skips in free-threading, and 308 tests with 4 skips
  in each GIL build. The latter include two subsequently added tests for saved
  methods and interleaved locks; all final tests also ran in free-threading
  during the following leak check.
* All 20 final protective Lock/RLock tests passed -R 3:3 in all three builds,
  without reference or allocated-block leaks in the measured repetitions.
  Tests include four contending ThreadGroups, same-group foreign denial,
  nested transitions and exceptions, multiple protected values, borrowed input
  rejection, and private ownership/fork bypass rejection.
* git diff --check passed. Logs: /tmp/pep805-rlock-{ft,gil,tier2}-{build,suite,ref}.log;
  the final free-threading C build is /tmp/pep805-rlock-ft-final-build.log.

Remaining protection work includes compound locks, arbitrary object copies,
the private Python RLock fallback, mixed-acquisition provenance, protective
fork/lifecycle/abandonment and complete reference-escape enforcement. The
existing broader Thread integration limitations still apply. Trace-refs,
native machine-code JIT and Windows were not validated in this increment.

### Compound native lock contexts

Exact native Lock and RLock instances now implement addition. The result is a
synchronized, non-instantiable _thread._CompoundLock with a private immutable
tuple of members. Adding compound locks flattens their member tuples, merges
them in globally unique mutex-ID order and removes duplicates. No Python
comparison callbacks participate in ordering. Each distinct member is acquired
once per entry, so duplicate primitive locks do not cause self-deadlock and
duplicate recursive locks do not artificially increase recursion depth.

Entry uses the same native context-acquisition paths as individual locks,
allowing protect() through a component while inside the compound context.
Members are released in reverse order on exit or partial acquisition failure.
Rollback preserves the original exception while attempting every acquired
member's release. Successful entries publish a per-Python-thread context
record under the compound object's critical section. Exit removes that
thread's most recent record; a foreign thread, including one in the same
ThreadGroup, cannot consume it. Fully recursive compounds support nested entry.

Compound release additionally checks ordinary primitive-lock context ownership
so that an external release/reacquisition cannot make cleanup unlock a
replacement owner's lock. This stronger cleanup check does not change ordinary
explicit Lock.release() semantics. An exit failure still attempts the other
members' releases. Allocation and module GC traversal/clearing include the new
type and context records; member objects stay strongly referenced by their
compound.

  test_stop_the_world passed: 316 tests with 5 skips in free-threading and 317

  recursive rollback test.
* The final 10 compound and 20 protective tests passed -R 3:3 in all three
  builds, with no reference or allocated-block leaks in the measured runs.
  This includes the final same-ThreadGroup foreign-exit test and native-subclass
  rejection checks. No worker remained running after verification.
* Four ThreadGroups concurrently use forwards and backwards compound
  expressions, including shared compound instances. Interval-timer tests verify
  acquisition order directly before interrupting the blocked second member,
  then verify rollback, removal of protection and preservation of an already
  held outer recursive context. The timer tests require POSIX setitimer.
* git diff --check passed. Logs:
  /tmp/pep805-compound-{ft,gil,tier2}-{build,suite,ref}.log.

This does not establish complete protection safety. Arbitrary protected-object
copies, native subclasses/Python RLock fallback, mixed-acquisition provenance,
fork/lifecycle/abandonment and complete call/return/evaluation-stack validation
remain. Ordering applies within each compound entry; pre-held locks can still
introduce cycles, and recursive entry into a compound containing a primitive
Lock blocks as it would for the individual primitive. The existing Thread
integration limitations remain. Trace-refs, machine-code JIT and Windows were
not validated in this increment.

### Protected Python instances and concrete call-result escape regression

Lock/RLock protect() now accept Python instances whose native base is object,
in addition to exact lists/dicts/sets. Instance copies use copy.copy and honor
__copy__. The result must have the same type, be local, differ from the source
and have no other strong references. Its actual instance namespace is copied
separately, bypassing a virtual __dict__ property, so an aliased dictionary
returned by a custom copier is not converted in place. Slots retain shallow
copy semantics. Immediately before publication, under the lock's metadata
critical section, the implementation rechecks lock ownership and uniqueness
of the copy and its dictionary, then gives both the mutex's protected state.

Python bound methods now inherit a protected receiver's state, like native
bound methods. Generic instance namespace replacement requires a dictionary
protected by the same mutex; local/differently protected replacements and
deletion are rejected. This is a provisional restriction pending a fuller
namespace transition policy. Other native layouts are not supported yet.

Parallel creation exposed two copyreg boundaries: the dispatch table and the
copyreg module itself were local to the importing group. The default table is
now a SynchronizedDict and the module namespace is synchronized. _pickle's
module initialization accepts that exact synchronized table in addition to an
exact dict; its existing lookup path already uses PyDict_GetItemRef. Other
copyreg extension registries were not promoted, and registered callbacks keep
their own sharing states.

At this stage, the new test_protected_call_result failed (subsequently fixed
by the call-result work recorded below). A source
instance's __del__ can call the owning lock's __exit__ during native call
argument cleanup, after protect() has returned its copy. The expression
lock.protect(Example()).release_on_delete then reads the protected result with
the mutex already unlocked. The actual reproducer printed False and
locked: False, before the outer with exit raised RuntimeError. The test
requires UnprotectedAccessException at acquisition of the call result and
fails because it is not raised. Log:
/tmp/pep805-instance-call-result-regression.log. It is not skipped or marked
as an expected failure, and was run separately from the successful suite above.

The next priority identified here was call-result access checking AFTER all argument/callable
cleanup, covering generic, specialized, instrumented and Tier 2 paths as well
as inlined Python returns. Checking inside protect() cannot fix a finalizer
that runs after that C function returns. Finalizer affinity and escaping
evaluation-stack references are also still incomplete. The current instance
support is therefore an experimental foundation, not verified race-free PEP
805 execution. The private Python RLock fallback, other native layouts and
previous lifecycle/closure/Thread/ABI/performance work remain. Trace-refs,
machine-code JIT and Windows were not validated for this increment.


### Call-result validation after cleanup

Native-capable CALL, CALL_KW, CALL_FUNCTION_EX and intrinsic macros now run
_CHECK_CALL_ACCESS after argument/callable disposal and periodic callbacks.
Python returns validate the value before returning and after clearing the
callee's frame, because local finalizers can release its protecting mutex.
Instrumented PY_RETURN validates before exposing the value to the callback;
the ordinary return checks it again afterwards. C API entry frames validate
at INTERPRETER_EXIT rather than using Python error unwinding on an entry shim.
Tagged integer stack references are intrinsically immutable and bypass checks.

Tier 2 return errors after the callee is cleared exit directly with the caller
frame and instruction pointer. The ordinary uop error target still belongs to
the dead callee and is unsuitable there. Native call errors publish the live
result and updated stack pointer before unwinding. A GIL regression test caught
a real GC assertion in builtin min: generic CALL_KW had already disposed of its
arguments without adjusting its physical stack, so error unwinding previously
decremented those dead inputs a second time. Explicit stack publication on the
error path fixes this. SAVE_STACK/RELOAD_STACK preserve the generator's peek
input bookkeeping; SYNC_SP cannot clear that bookkeeping with live peek inputs.

The new four-test module covers source __del__, eight native call shapes,
Python direct/keyword/star calls, Python entered through a native forwarder,
and CALL/PY_RETURN monitoring. It consumes the result's __class__ before the
wrapper returns, so a wrapper return check cannot mask a missing native result
check. The optimizer regression warms native and inlined Python paths before
turning on the finalizer that releases their protecting lock. Immutable known
constant call results can omit _CHECK_CALL_ACCESS; protected values cannot.

protection during evaluation of later arguments or callbacks. Other C API
return boundaries and suspended frame lifetimes remain incomplete. This is not
a complete or verified race-free implementation. Native machine-code JIT,
trace-refs and Windows were not revalidated in this increment.

### Checked borrowed locals

Inspecting the cached-load-special failure showed that the missing
_POP_TOP_NOP came from x += 1 inside the with statement, not its None return.
The earlier conservative with-local compiler transformation forced an owned
load where the existing reference-lifetime analysis could safely borrow.
LOAD_FAST_BORROW_MAYBE_UNPROTECTED retains both the unbound and object-access
checks, then borrows from the local. The compiler uses the same support-killed,
stored-as-local and unconsumed-reference analysis as ordinary LOAD_FAST_BORROW;
a supporting local overwritten before use still requires the owned variant.
Tier 2 symbols preserve this borrowing. Bytecode magic is advanced to 3707.

The added regression checks denial after protection ends and keeps the owned
load when an assignment expression overwrites the supporting local. Existing
with-local tests accept both checked load forms while preserving their runtime
assertions.

The next concrete safety hole is call-input lifetime. A local diagnostic with
protected.append(unlock()) acquired the protected receiver before evaluating
unlock(), which called lock.__exit__ and returned 42. append then ran with the
mutex unlocked; reacquiring the lock showed [42]. Only the outer with exit
raised RuntimeError. Rechecking the returned None cannot detect this mutation.
All call families need input/receiver validation after argument evaluation,
and callbacks occurring during the call need a wider lifetime policy. This
remains a reproducible limitation, not a completed reference implementation.


### VM call inputs after argument evaluation

The direct protected.append(unlock()) regression above is now rejected before
append executes. All 31 non-intrinsic CALL/CALL_KW/CALL_FUNCTION_EX macros,
including specialized Python frames, validate their callable, optional receiver
and argument references. _CHECK_CALL_INPUTS and _CHECK_CALL_KW_INPUTS retain
their inputs and use _PyEval_CheckCallStack; tagged integers and the optional
NULL receiver are allowed. Recording uops remain first, as required by the
instruction generator. Instrumented ordinary/keyword calls revalidate after
the CALL callback, since that callback can release the protecting mutex.

Expanded calls check their callable and containers before converting an
iterable into a tuple, then _CHECK_CALL_EX_INPUTS validates the callable and
every resulting positional/keyword reference. Instrumented expanded calls
revalidate after their embedded CALL callback. Unary/binary intrinsics check
their operands too. Checks do not descend recursively into argument objects.
The generated error paths publish the stack around the helper call and retain
live inputs for ordinary unwinding; they do not consume inputs twice.

Five new tests exercise native method/descriptors, bound methods, explicit
arguments to a general native callable, positional/keyword/star Python calls,
a protected callable, an argument iterator releasing its lock and monitoring
callbacks. Warmed native and Python calls run the same code after safe calls
have enabled specialization; side effects must remain absent after rejection.
The Tier 2 test similarly warms native/Python loops before enabling the release,
requires _CHECK_CALL_INPUTS in an executor, and confirms neither mutation nor
entry into the Python callee occurs.

The earlier unique-tuple-unpack regression previously passed an inaccessible
object directly into a worker and callee. Correct call-input validation now
rejects that setup earlier. Its fixture now passes a SynchronizedDict and
builds a shallow tuple under StopTheWorld. The returned tuple is accessible;
its children become inaccessible when the pause exits. The unchanged checks
still require the unique-tuple stealing uop and _CHECK_UNPACK_ACCESS, 100 denied
unpacks with the innermost traceback in the tested function, and unchanged
foreign storage. This preserves the unpack boundary being tested instead of
loosening call validation or accepting an earlier error.

A concrete C-call boundary hole remains. Creating
indirect = functools.partial(value.append) while holding value's mutex, then
calling indirect(unlock()), still appends 42 with that mutex unlocked. The VM
sees an accessible partial and integer argument; the partial retrieves its
protected target and invokes it via C without the new VM check. General C API
call validation is still required. It must be integrated with readonly-closure
classification and Thread startup, rather than making foreign local callbacks
or captured data unconditionally shareable. Callbacks during a native operation
can also invalidate a receiver after the operation has started. Suspended
frames, context abandonment, native layouts, finalizer affinity, lifetime/ABI
and performance work listed above remain. This is still not a complete PEP 805
reference implementation.


### Partial's captured call references

The concrete partial(value.append)(unlock()) hole above is now rejected.
Native functools.partial validates its own accessibility and the captured
function/argument containers before retrieving their contents. Vectorcall
fast paths and the general vectorcall path validate the final function and
positional/keyword values immediately before forwarding the call, after
argument evaluation and keyword merging. The tuple/dict fallback validates its
merged arguments through _PyEval_CheckCallArgs. That helper now accepts tuple
and dict subclasses, preserving the existing C API calling convention while
still reading their built-in storage directly.

Keyword keys are checked before merging, since hashing/equality of string
subclasses can execute Python; final call-input checks catch a callback that
releases protection during the merge. Overridden captured keyword values are
not passed to the callee and need not be acquired, so replacing an inaccessible
old value with an accessible new one still works. Checks remain shallow.

Both dispatch paths validate their result after releasing temporary captured
references/argument containers. A target can replace its partial's state during
execution; the vectorcall snapshot then becomes the last reference to a native
bound method. Releasing that snapshot can run its receiver's __del__ and unlock
a protected result after the native method has returned.

The result regression uses partial(Target([value]).pop), an index callback that
replaces the partial's state, and Target.__del__ that releases the lock. Calling
it through list(map(...)) consumes the result entirely in C and puts it inside
an accessible outer list, so a VM call-result check cannot mask a missing
partial result check. The test explicitly records UnprotectedAccessException
before outer context cleanup: a RuntimeError from that cleanup must not mask
an assertion failure or an unexpected exception type. Other new rejection
tests use the same explicit flag. The result test failed in the pre-change GIL
binary and passed after the change; log:
/tmp/pep805-partial-access-old-gil-result.log.

This closes partial's captured call/reference boundary, not the general C API
call boundary. Other extension objects that acquire heap references and forward
calls still need checks, including native callback paths. Universal C API
integration must also address readonly closure/cell provenance and Thread's
_target/_kwargs/_invoke_excepthook/_stderr ownership without silently sharing
arbitrary local callbacks or data. Partial construction/introspection and other
native object lifetimes remain part of the wider audit. The full PEP 805
reference implementation is still incomplete.


### Thread invocation containers and exception dispatcher

Thread.start now snapshots its positional/keyword invocation containers when
its selected group differs from the starting thread's group. Positional
arguments become a tuple and keywords a frozendict, before the native thread
is published. This includes the default empty kwargs dictionary, which had
previously been Main-local and prevented even a synchronized target from
running in another group. A supplied list/dict remains local and is not
converted in place. The snapshots are shallow: argument values retain their
states and can still be rejected by call-input checks. Updates before start
are included; subsequent changes to the original containers do not alter the
invocation. Same-group starts preserve the existing container references and
behavior. This behavior is documented in threading.rst.

The standard _make_invoke_excepthook dispatcher no longer creates local closure
cells. It retains the previous hooks, exc_info, print and sys in one shallow
immutable tuple stored as a default argument. It acquires each object only when
needed, so an unused saved local hook does not prevent invocation of the
current synchronized hook. Capturing a hook does not change that hook's own
state. The dispatcher itself is now synchronized under the existing
closure-free function classification. This preserves its shutdown references
without pretending arbitrary user callbacks are shareable.

Eight new tests exercise a synchronized target with default arguments,
explicit synchronized keywords, ordinary list/dict snapshots at start,
same-group dictionary aliasing, a shared exception hook, an unused saved local
hook, a rejected mutable-closure target and rejected local argument values in
ordinary/synchronized keyword dictionaries and positional arguments. They use
ordinary public Thread.start/join, not a raw-thread bypass or ownership-state
promotion. They verify that rejected targets never execute and that errors
reach the shared hook.

### Thread exception output

The native default threading.excepthook now handles ownership/protection denial
when fetching sys.stderr or Thread._stderr by allocating a fresh PyStdPrinter
in the worker group. It writes to the process stderr descriptor without
accessing the rejected stream, acquiring its mutex, or changing its sharing
state. Other getter errors propagate. Accessible shared streams remain in use;
current and saved streams both set to None still suppress reporting. This is
an error-reporting fallback, not general TextIOWrapper synchronization.

Seven tests cover default stderr, rejected local/protected custom streams,
the saved stream, an accessible frozen custom stream, None suppression, and
repeated printer allocation/deallocation in the regrtest process. The latter
makes reference-leak measurements exercise the printer rather than just a
subprocess harness. The old GIL binary failed the default-stderr regression
with no output; the rebuilt binary passes.

### Ownership of zero-initialized static extension types

The broader stderr validation exposed a static-type initialization gap:
_testcapi.RecursingInfinitelyError omits PyVarObject_HEAD_INIT and relies on
PyType_Ready to complete its object header. PyType_Ready immortalized the type,
but ordinary immortality is not static immortality. The lazy ownership check
therefore left this LOCAL type with owner zero and denied even Main access.

PyType_Ready now assigns an unowned local non-heap type to the initializing
interpreter's main group while holding the type lock. An atomic compare/exchange
preserves an already assigned owner; declared shared states and heap types are
unchanged. It does not declare the extension type synchronized or immutable.
The C API documentation describes this ownership rule.

Two regression tests verify Main ownership and foreign-group denial without
promoting the type or the callback. Both failed on the old free-threading
binary while fetching the type. With the fix, both passed -R 3:3 in all three
debug builds. Free-threading and Tier 2 had no measured leaks; GIL reported
[0, 1, 0] allocated-block noise accepted by regrtest, with no reference leaks.
Logs: /tmp/pep805-static-type-old-ft-test.log and
/tmp/pep805-static-type-{ft,gil,tier2}-ref.log.

test_capi.test_exceptions and test_exceptions suites passed in all three
builds: 168 tests, three skips each. The recursion-normalization regression
now reaches its intended RecursionError. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-static-type-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite.log.

exception hook: all four workers raise IllegalThreadAccessException in
Thread.run before their target body, confirming the conservative closure
classification problem. Diagnostic log:
/tmp/pep805-thread-stderr-sys-diagnostic.log.

not tested for this increment. Read-only closure/cell provenance, user-hook

the other outstanding implementation work remain. No full-conformance claim
is made.


### Read-only closure conformance baseline

The previous turn fixed native exception output and static type ownership;
this turn audits the remaining closure classification gap against PEP 805's
function rule. test_readonly_closure adds seven behavioral requirements using
public Thread.start/join and ordinary function construction. It does not
promote callbacks, bypass access checks, or mark unmet behavior expected to
fail. Current free-threading baseline: seven tests run, six failures, with
only the sibling-writer-local test passing. Log:
/tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-baseline.log. These are intentionally unresolved
conformance regressions, not evidence of a completed feature.

The tests cover immutable and synchronized captured values, a captured local
value denied inside LOAD_DEREF (including traceback-site verification),
transitive read-only closures, sibling nonlocal writers, attaching a writer
with FunctionType, and replacing one reader's code with writer code. Dynamic
writer attachment must affect other readers of that same cell without
reclassifying an unrelated cell instance created from identical code.

Current implementation findings:

* func_can_share in Objects/funcobject.c rejects every code object with free
  variables, irrespective of whether those bindings can be written.
* code_closure_write_kinds already propagates descendant nonlocal writes,
  but code metadata alone cannot identify an existing cell shared by a reader
  and a separately constructed writer.
* MAKE_CELL currently calls PyCell_New without passing lexical provenance.
  Public CellType/PyCell_New cells likewise carry no binding provenance.
* SET_FUNCTION_ATTRIBUTE attaches closure storage without updating function
  classification. FunctionType's final closure assignment has the same gap;
  it is currently hidden by the conservative free-variable rule.
* PyFunction_SetClosure and code replacement already stop the world and
  update the changed function, but do not reclassify other functions that
  capture the same cells. A safe implementation must account for those
  dependent functions and invalidate applicable function versions/executors.
* Cell reads use internal locking and check acquired contents. This does not
  prove that a binding is immutable. Parent-frame STORE_DEREF, repeated loop
  assignments, cell_contents/PyCell_Set, frame-locals writes, debugger access,
  and dynamically constructed nonlocal writers must be accounted for before
  changing the conservative function rule.

No blanket promotion of closure functions was applied. The pending work is
binding provenance plus publication/mutation integration, followed by these
conformance tests and the earlier ThreadGroup/sys/optimizer regressions.


### Read-only binding implementation

The new closure conformance baseline is now implemented for VM-created
bindings. MAKE_CELL records whether the code analysis knows of a nonlocal
writer. Capturing a read-only binding can make a function synchronized, but
LOAD_DEREF still checks the captured value. Thus a function can be shared while
its captured local list remains inaccessible to another group. Sibling and
transitive nonlocal writers keep their shared binding local.

Replacing an initialized binding, through parent STORE_DEREF/DELETE_DEREF,
cell_contents, frame-locals mutation, PyCell_Set or PyCell_SET, marks the binding
mutable. Connecting writer code through FunctionType, code replacement or
PyFunction_SetClosure does the same. Existing capturing functions are found
with the world stopped and reclassified, including GC-frozen functions.
Their function versions are cleared. Mutable captures determine their owning
group, so constructing a new wrapper cannot re-own a foreign mutable cell.
If mutable cells have different local owners, the function has no valid group
owner and ordinary acquisition is denied. Two additional tests failed on the
intermediate binary before this ownership correction:
/tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-old-owner.log.

Closure publication and removal register/unregister their cell dependencies.
The binding byte encodes a saturating count of attached functions while the
binding remains read-only; saturation preserves conservative scanning and
cannot wrap into a mutable/unobserved state. With no surviving readers, an
atomic transition avoids both the world pause and heap scan. Initial versions
scanned for every cell rebinding and caused traceback formatting to take over
120 seconds in test_threading.test_print_exception_gh_102056. Dependency
counting fixed that regression; the focused test and final whole-threading
runs pass in all three builds. A 300-reader regression exercises saturation.

Frame-constructor functions are GC-tracked before the world resumes, so a
concurrent cell transition cannot miss an unpublished untracked reader.
Creation-time closure installation converts an existing function namespace
when necessary and releases any closure installed by reentrant callbacks.
The raw C setter now notifies binding mutation; generated DELETE_DEREF code
publishes the VM stack because that notification can stop the world.

Updated fixtures distinguish a read-only binding from a genuinely local
nonlocal writer. Per-call factory readers capturing local lists are now
synchronized, with their lists still private to each call. The parallel
namespace fixture snapshots loop bindings in defaults. The optimizer's
foreign-method fixture now actually writes a nonlocal cell and asserts that
its method is local. The sys.modules parallel-update fixture captures the
immutable ModuleType class and reads sys.modules directly, instead of trying
to acquire Main's local types/_testinternalcapi modules in the worker. A shared
exception hook confirmed that its former failure had advanced from target
acquisition into the worker's module access:
/tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-sys-diagnostic.log.

Validation on Linux aarch64 debug builds:

* Free-threading, GIL and Tier 2 interpreter builds completed successfully;
  _testinternalcapi imports successfully. The baseline missing _decimal module
  is unchanged. All generated cases were regenerated.
* All 16 read-only closure tests passed -R 3:3 in all three configurations with
  no measured reference or allocated-block leaks. Logs:
  /tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-{ft,gil,tier2}-final-count-ref.log.
* The final nine-file suite passed in all three configurations: 544 tests,
  20 skips for free-threading and 19 for GIL/Tier 2. Files:
  test_readonly_closure, test_closure_analysis, test_thread_invocation,
  test_sys, test_capi.test_function, test_funcattrs, test_scope, test_frame and
  test_threading. Logs:
  /tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-{ft,gil,tier2}-final-suite.log.
* The intermediate full Tier 2 optimizer run executed 331 tests, with 15
  errors and one failure (three skips). The failure was the now-synchronized
  foreign-method fixture described above. Its corrected focused test passed
  -R 3:3; [0, 1, 0] allocated-block noise was accepted by regrtest, with no
  reference leaks. Logs: /tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-tier2-opt.log and
  /tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-tier2-super-ref.log. The whole optimizer suite
  is not claimed green or rerun after the final dependency-count optimization.
* git diff --check passed. Native JIT, trace-refs and Windows were not tested.

Remaining failures are not suppressed. The broader function/cell boundary
suites still have drivers that pass foreign local arguments through a checked
Python call before their intended setter/read operation. The adapted
function_shareable run has one such namespace-ownership error, and
function_mutation has two foreign-local mutation-driver errors. Cell mutation
has four rejected-driver cases, and cell access has an empty-Channel error
from a worker rejected before entering its body. The optimizer errors include
related early-boundary fixtures. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-ft-adapted.log,
/tmp/pep805-readonly-closure-ft-failures.log and the intermediate suite logs.
These need proper C-boundary or locally constructed receiver fixtures, not
weaker access checks. The initial mutation fixtures also exposed a warning
registry owned by a worker when they mistakenly treated a shared read-only
function as local; cross-group warning-registry behavior needs its own audit.

Binding classification is conservative: once a binding becomes writable it
stays writable, and manually created cells start writable. Reader invalidation
still scans the interpreter heap when actual readers remain; a per-cell
reverse index and optimized registration are future performance work.
Cross-interpreter extension abuse, trusted manual declarations, active-frame
access after state changes, general C API ownership/lifetime, shared bound
methods, I/O synchronization, group reference counting, and the remaining
full-PEP requirements are not established by this increment. The reference
implementation remains incomplete.


### Warning registry ownership

A synchronized function emitting a native deprecation warning in a worker
created __warningregistry__ in its global namespace as an ordinary worker-local
dictionary. Later Main warnings failed while retrieving that dictionary, even
if the worker's warning had itself failed when accessing other warning state.
A standalone reproduction showed the worker denied on Main-owned state and
then Main denied on the worker-owned registry.

Automatically created per-module registries and the default once registry now
use SynchronizedDict, in both the native and pure-Python implementations.
Explicitly supplied registries retain their original state and ownership.
This changes bookkeeping allocation, not access enforcement for arbitrary
filters, warning contexts, callbacks or streams. No user dictionary is promoted.

Four regression tests cover worker-created bookkeeping followed by Main use,
non-promotion of a supplied local registry, the default once registry, and the
pure-Python allocation paths. The old binary failed both native allocation
regressions: /tmp/pep805-warning-registry-old-ft.log. Pure-module testing runs
inside a fresh context: otherwise each fresh import's ContextVar retains its
restored _GlobalContext in the test runner on context-aware builds. That test
fixture initially retained 626 references per repetition; isolating the context
removes the retention without changing warning behavior or hiding native
allocations in subprocesses.

Validation on Linux aarch64 debug builds:

* Free-threading, GIL and Tier 2 interpreter rebuilds succeeded.
* Four new tests passed -R 3:3 in all three builds. The free-threading run
  reported [0, 1, 0] allocated-block noise accepted by regrtest, with no
  reference leaks. Logs: /tmp/pep805-warning-registry-{ft,gil,tier2}-ref.log.
* test_warnings, test_warning_registry, test_thread_error_output and
  test_readonly_closure passed: 224 tests in each build; no skips in
  free-threading and six skips in GIL/Tier 2. Logs:
  /tmp/pep805-warning-registry-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite.log.
* git diff --check passed. Native JIT, trace-refs and Windows were not tested.

This is not complete cross-group warnings support. Imported warning modules,
filter lists and custom matcher objects, context values, callbacks and stderr
still need their own synchronization/access audits. A worker may continue to
receive an ownership exception on those objects; it no longer leaves its own
local registry behind to break later warnings in another group. The broader
C API, optimizer-fixture, lifetime and full-PEP requirements remain unfinished.

### Native warning filter access and lifetime

Native filter_search now checks the filter container and each acquired entry,
then checks action, matchers, category and line objects before using them.
Matcher results are checked before truth testing. Checks for later operands
remain after earlier callbacks, and the matched action is checked again after
callbacks. The loop rechecks the container before reading its size: a matcher
can release the protective lock even when its result does not match.

The search owns a reference to the filter list across callbacks. A reentrant
matcher can replace warnings.filters and issue a nested warning, replacing the
interpreter's cached reference as well. Previously neither the borrowed local
pointer nor its critical section kept that old list alive. Each entry is also
retained before access checking or reporting a malformed tuple, so exception
allocation cannot invalidate a borrowed entry through reentrant list mutation.

Lib/test/test_warning_filter_access.py adds four tests covering foreign-group
entries and their five fields, release of a later matcher's protective lock,
release of the filter container's lock, and reentrant replacement of the global
filter list. The replacement regression uses context_aware_warnings=0 to
exercise the formerly borrowed global path; it asserts the original list is
alive during the callback and reclaimed after the outer search. The old
free-threading binary failed five foreign-reference subcases; the potentially
unsafe old list-lifetime path was not deliberately executed.

Validation on Linux aarch64 debug builds:

* Free-threading, GIL and Tier 2 interpreter rebuilds succeeded.
* test_warning_filter_access, test_warning_registry and test_warnings passed
  in all three builds: 205 tests, with six skips each in GIL and Tier 2.
  Logs: /tmp/pep805-warning-filter-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite.log.
* The four new tests passed -R 3:3 in all three builds, with no measured
  reference or allocated-block leaks. Logs:
  /tmp/pep805-warning-filter-{ft,gil,tier2}-ref.log.
* git diff --check passed. Native JIT, trace-refs and Windows remain untested.

This does not make warnings fully shareable. Broader native warning input,
callback and cached-state lifetime checks remain to be audited, including the
borrowed default action and once-registry paths. No warning module, matcher or
user filter list is implicitly synchronized. Full C API and VM access/lifetime
coverage and the other outstanding PEP requirements are still unfinished.

### Warning action and once-registry cache lifetime

Two more reentrant warning paths used borrowed cached objects after callbacks.
A warning message's __hash__ can replace warnings.defaultaction or
warnings.onceregistry and emit another warning, dropping the cached reference
still in use by the outer warning. Weak-reference assertions in two regression
tests failed on the previous free-threading binary:
/tmp/pep805-warning-cache-old-ft.log.

get_default_action and get_once_registry now return owned references, including
when falling back to cached values. Updating either cache retains its incoming
value before releasing the old one, since the old object's destructor may also
reenter warnings. get_filter consistently returns an owned action for both
matched filters and the default path. warn_explicit validates the returned
action and releases that reference and any acquired once registry at cleanup.
User-supplied registries retain their existing ownership and state.

The two tests require the original objects to remain alive during the nested
warning and become reclaimable after the outer warning. Test cleanup restores
both module attributes and native caches. Restoring only the attributes left
an 'ignore' default in native state and caused subsequent fresh _warnings
imports to suppress warnings; the first combined run exposed that fixture
pollution. Cleanup now refreshes the caches through normal warning emission
and restores the original registry contents.

### Warning skip-file-prefix references

The native warn path now validates the skip_file_prefixes tuple before reading
its size, and validates each stored prefix before checking its string type.
An immutable tuple can contain a foreign-group LOCAL str subclass, so checking
only the call's tuple argument did not protect the native element acquisition.
A worker-created prefix regression failed on the old binary without the
required IllegalThreadAccessException:
/tmp/pep805-warning-prefix-old-ft.log.

The empty-prefix branch also incorrectly decremented its borrowed tuple
argument. The immortal built-in empty tuple concealed this bug; a mortal tuple
subclass exposed it. That decrement has been removed. A subprocess regression
checks that passing an empty tuple subclass preserves its reference count.
The two tests join test_warning_filter_access, now containing eight tests.

skips each in GIL/Tier 2. Logs:

This adds checks at prefix acquisition, not a complete warning-stack audit.
Frame walking, propagation of native filename-comparison errors, broader

### Warning frame-walk access and error propagation

Native warning context setup now checks code-object filename references before
using them, including the selected frame when stacklevel is zero. Internal
filename and skip-prefix predicates return a distinct error result. The frame
walk releases its owned frame on an error, propagates failures from frame or
filename acquisition, and does not silently substitute the sys context for a
failed walk. Prefixes are rechecked when acquired during walking as well as
validated initially. The fallback filename allocation is checked before registry
setup, avoiding continuation with an active allocation error.

One regression creates a shared function whose code retains a worker-owned str
subclass as its filename. It checks three current-frame stack levels and two
caller-frame paths, with warnings ignored so later display processing cannot
mask missing frame-walk checks. The worker constructs a separate SynchronizedDict
namespace: its first fixture attempted to retrieve a Main-owned globals dict
and was correctly denied before reaching the target path. With that corrected
fixture, the old GIL binary failed all five access checks:
/tmp/pep805-warning-frame-old-gil.log. The initial free-threading baseline was
a fixture error, not evidence of a native frame-walk result.

  passed in all three builds: 278 tests, eight skips in free-threading and
  fourteen each in GIL/Tier 2. Logs:
  /tmp/pep805-warning-frame-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite.log.

This does not finish the warning or frame C API audit. General native argument

Windows were not validated in this increment.

### Frame code/back C API receivers

PyFrame_GetCode and PyFrame_GetBack now validate the supplied frame before
reading its fields. PyFrame_GetBack also checks an acquired outer-frame
reference before returning it. The C API documentation now describes the NULL
with exception result for inaccessible frames. Public callers in warning
handling already propagated NULL; traceback output now does so too. Import
traceback trimming stops if frame metadata is inaccessible and preserves the
original import exception, since trimming is optional.

A native _testcapi helper deliberately extracts a frame from a tuple without
Python element-access validation. Two tests check foreign-group receiver
rejection and normal same-group results. The old free-threading binary exposed
the missing GetCode receiver check. Its initial GetBack test could be satisfied
by VM result checking, so the helper was tightened to return only a boolean for
that branch: it now tests rejection inside the C API rather than rejection of
its returned frame by the VM. Baseline log:
/tmp/pep805-frame-api-old-ft.log.

The two tests passed -R 3:3 on Linux aarch64 debug free-threading, GIL and Tier 2
interpreter builds. Free-threading reported [0, 0, 1] allocated blocks, accepted
by regrtest, with no reference leak. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-frame-api-{ft,gil,tier2}-ref.log.

This does not finish frame safety. Other public frame accessors, creation and
ownership when inspecting another thread, locals proxies and debugger/lifecycle
paths still require auditing. The full PEP objective remains incomplete; native
JIT, trace-refs and Windows are not validated by this increment.

test_import (22 skips overall): SinglephaseInitTests' multiple-interpreter
main_no_reset and reset_each cases. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-frame-api-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite.log.

An isolated comparison in the GIL build removed only the new GetCode/GetBack
checks and restored the old unchecked outer-frame return. Both import failures
still occurred, confirming they predate this increment. The stricter native

not mask the regression. Log: /tmp/pep805-frame-api-gil-baseline.log. The isolated
source and binary were then restored to the checked implementation. The import
failures remain outstanding; they were not skipped or weakened. All three
rebuilds and git diff --check succeeded.

### Frame locals/globals/builtins/generator C API boundaries

PyFrame_GetLocals, PyFrame_GetGlobals, PyFrame_GetBuiltins and
PyFrame_GetGenerator now validate their receiver before reading frame fields,
and validate the owned result before returning it to native code. The result
check matters independently: an accessible running frame can belong to a
shared function whose globals and builtins remain LOCAL in another group.
The relevant C API documentation describes access-error returns.

The native tuple-based frame test helper now selects named operations and
discards non-code results before returning a boolean, keeping VM call-result
checking from masking missing C API checks. The receiver test covers all six
object-returning accessors. A new test constructs a shared function with
worker-owned globals and builtins, runs it in Main, and probes its Main-owned
frame natively. Both namespace acquisitions must fail inside the C API.
Normal same-group field acquisition and existing test_capi.test_frame behavior
remain covered.

The old free-threading binary failed six subcases: four foreign receivers and
two foreign namespace results. Log: /tmp/pep805-frame-fields-old-ft.log.

This is not complete frame safety. Integer-valued accessors, variable lookup,
frame creation/ownership during cross-thread inspection, locals proxies and
interpreter lifecycle remain to be audited. The prior single-phase extension
import failures and the full PEP completion requirements remain outstanding.
