### Frame variable lookup access

PyFrame_GetVar now checks the frame receiver and the supplied name before
reading frame state, checks acquired code-local names before comparing them,
and validates the owned variable value before returning it. PyFrame_GetVarString
checks its receiver before allocating the Python name, and delegates to the
same checked lookup. Documentation describes input/result access failures.

The native frame helper now exercises both variable lookup entry points and
can acquire a supplied name directly from a tuple. The regression module adds
foreign-name rejection and inaccessible protected-value cases; its existing
foreign-receiver test now covers both variable APIs. The old free-threading
binary failed five subcases (two receiver, one name, two result):
/tmp/pep805-frame-var-old-ft.log. Additional cases exercise protected values in
active closure cells and in unstarted generators whose free variables are
initialized by lookup before COPY_FREE_VARS has executed. These tests call
native probes that discard the result, so VM call-result validation cannot
satisfy the expected denial accidentally.

This does not complete frame/closure lifetime safety. In particular, active or
suspended frames whose shared function/readonly cells subsequently become
writable still need an ownership audit. Integer-valued frame accessors,
creation during cross-thread introspection and interpreter lifecycle also
remain unfinished, along with broader PEP requirements and existing
single-phase extension import failures.

### Integer-valued frame accessors

PyFrame_GetLineNumber and PyFrame_GetLasti now validate their frame receiver
before accessing its state and return -1 with an access exception on failure.
Their callers distinguish an unavailable position (negative result without an
exception) from a failure. This includes the f_lineno getter, frame repr,
warning context setup, the existing _testcapi lasti wrapper and its C trace
recorder. C API documentation describes both outcomes.

The native receiver regression now probes both integer APIs; the old binary
failed to reject a foreign frame in either case:
/tmp/pep805-frame-position-old-ft.log. A separate regression uses an empty
line table to verify that an unknown line still returns -1 without an error,
f_lineno remains None, and repr continues to display the unknown line.
Normal same-group position reads remain covered.

This closes the public integer accessor entry checks, not the remaining frame
ownership/lifetime work. Cross-thread frame creation, active/suspended function
state changes, locals proxies and interpreter lifecycle still require audits.
The full PEP reference implementation remains incomplete.

### Ownership of lazily materialized frames

A debugger in Main could materialize a worker's frame while StopTheWorld was
active, leaving that frame owned by Main. After inspection, Main could continue
to access the worker's frame, while the worker was denied access to its own
traceback frame. A regression reproduced both incorrect outcomes on the old
free-threading binary: /tmp/pep805-frame-owner-old-ft.log. Its initial fixture
incorrectly called the StopTheWorld singleton; the corrected baseline uses it
as a context manager directly.

Each real _PyInterpreterFrame now records its initialization ThreadGroup ID.
Both normal and unchecked frame-push paths initialize it; frame copies preserve
it when creating generators or moving escaped frame data. Materialization sets
the PyFrameObject owner from that ID before publishing the object, including
before the free-threading compare-and-exchange. PyFrame_New applies the same
ownership to explicitly allocated frames. Synthetic entry/base frames use zero
and are not materialized. Frame-size expectations account for the added field;
InternalDocs/frames.md and sys._current_frames documentation explain ownership.

Two regressions cover inspection of an active worker's frame chain and first
materialization of a suspended generator frame. Both verify that the inspector
loses access after StopTheWorld and the worker retains access. The active-frame
test also checks denial after thread exit, when escaped frame data has moved
into owned storage. Existing same-group inspection remains covered by test_sys.

This fixes materialization ownership, not all cross-thread introspection. The
public thread-state frame getter's cross-group acquisition and error contracts,
generator transfer/lifecycle, and active frames whose closure bindings change
ownership still require auditing. Full PEP completion, existing single-phase
extension import failures and native JIT/Windows validation remain outstanding.

Logs: /tmp/pep805-frame-owner-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite.log.

### Generator code C API receiver

PyGen_GetCode now validates its generator receiver before accessing the embedded
frame's code. Its API documentation describes the NULL-with-exception result
for an inaccessible generator. The existing native caller already propagates
NULL. A new _testcapi helper deliberately retrieves a generator directly from
a tuple; since the result is an immutable code object, VM call-result checks
cannot detect a missing receiver check.

Two tests cover foreign-group rejection and same-group code identity before
execution, while suspended, and after exhaustion. The old free-threading binary
failed foreign-group rejection:
/tmp/pep805-gen-code-old-ft.log.

The investigation also inspected the deprecated PyGen_New/PyGen_NewWithQualName,
PyCoro_New and PyAsyncGen_New paths. Upstream documents these as impossible to
call with a properly constructed frame and scheduled for removal; they are not
the normal generator-creation path. No legacy constructor behavior was changed
in this increment. Their audit status must not be confused with completion of
live generator/frame ownership and lifetime checks. The full PEP remains
unfinished.

### Reading closure cells after reflective rebinding

An already-created generator retains its frame when reflective cell rebinding
makes its function local again. LOAD_DEREF and LOAD_FROM_DICT_OR_DEREF now check
mutable cell ownership when acquiring a captured binding. Read-only bindings
remain readable across groups. Frame variable APIs and locals proxies use the
same acquisition helper and propagate access errors instead of treating them
as missing variables. Hidden-local detection consequently has a tri-state
result, propagated by both callers.

The acquisition helper takes a strong reference before checking access: taking
the cell lock may suspend, so the binding state is checked after acquisition.
LOAD_DEREF also retains that strong reference across the value-access check;
the case generator publishes the frame stack around both helper calls. This
avoids retaining an unpublished deferred stack reference across error creation
and possible GC.

The regression creates two generators from an initially shareable closure,
starts one, then asks the owning group to rebind the captured cell. It checks
both resumed and unstarted generators, both frame-variable C APIs, and locals
proxy lookup, containment, length, keys, values and items. The old GIL binary
failed all eighteen rejection subcases; the earlier execution-only version
failed both resume subcases in the old free-threading binary. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-cell-read-old-{ft,gil}.log.

### Cell C API receiver access

PyCell_Get now checks access to the cell itself before acquiring its contents.
Previously only the acquired contents were checked, so native code holding a
foreign cell could read an immutable value or inspect an empty cell. The public
API now rejects both cases; accessible empty cells still return NULL without an
exception. The documentation distinguishes inaccessible receivers from empty
accessible cells. This does not change the internal read-only binding exception
used by frame reads.

A native test helper extracts the cell directly from a tuple to avoid VM
argument/subscription checks masking the API omission. The old free-threading
binary failed both filled and empty foreign-receiver subcases; the same-group
identity and empty-cell test passed. Log:
/tmp/pep805-cell-receiver-old-ft.log.

The existing cell-contents test had become unreachable after VM input checks:
it passed a foreign local object directly to the worker. It now transfers an
immutable holder tuple and uses a native helper to populate worker-owned cells.
The reads under test (closure execution, cell_contents, PyCell_Get and lazy
annotations) consequently reach their intended checks. Subsequent local-value
reads, empty-cell behavior and weak-reference cleanup are still exercised.
The initial obsolete-fixture failure is recorded in:
/tmp/pep805-cell-receiver-ft-initial.log.

Linux aarch64 debug free-threading, GIL and Tier 2 interpreter builds succeeded.
All three passed test_cell_access, test_readonly_closure, test_generator_access,
test_scope and test_funcattrs: 103 tests each. All three cell-access tests also
passed -R 3:3 in all builds. No reference leaks were reported; free-threading
and GIL each had a single non-repeating allocated-block fluctuation accepted by
the test runner, and Tier 2 had none. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-cell-receiver-{ft,gil,tier2}-{suite,ref}.log.
git diff --check passed. The full PEP implementation, general frame/proxy and
native API audit, native JIT, trace-refs and Windows validation remain unfinished.

### Restoring direct cell mutation coverage

An audit of test_cell_mutation found all four tests failed before reaching their
operations: the VM correctly rejected foreign local arguments to the worker.
The initial failure log is /tmp/pep805-cell-mutation-audit.log. Tests now pass
immutable holder tuples across groups, without weakening production checks.

A native helper extracts the receiver and invokes PyCell_Set or attribute
assignment/deletion, so VM subscription/argument checks cannot mask these API
checks. Class-cell tests prepare the namespace during StopTheWorld and call
type only after leaving that exemption. Comparison tests populate worker-owned
cells through the previously added native helper, then compare outside any
exemption, checking that foreign-value comparison callbacks never run.

STORE_DEREF, DELETE_DEREF and frame-locals writes use generator frames prepared
inside StopTheWorld, resumed or modified after leaving the context. Traceback
checks establish that rejection occurs in the writer/deleter body rather than
at its call boundary. Rejected values are checked for cleanup with weakrefs,
foreign bindings must remain unchanged, and equivalent local writes/deletions
must succeed. Local FunctionType fixtures now use fresh globals dictionaries
instead of the inaccessible main-group module dictionary.

### Broad added-test audit and immutable sys.flags snapshots

A free-threading audit ran all 45 added test modules with two workers:

the full implementation is not complete. The failure list and details are in
/tmp/pep805-added-audit-ft.log; the input module list is
/tmp/pep805-added-tests.txt. Several failures resemble obsolete boundary
fixtures, but they have not all been classified and must not be dismissed.

Tracing worker-created default Threads exposed access to main-owned sys.flags.
Flags are now declared immutable after full initialization, before publication,
in both configuration construction and single-flag replacement paths. Existing
snapshots remain untouched. The documentation records this shared snapshot
behavior. A regression reads flags in a foreign group, updates the integer digit
limit there, and verifies both old and newly published snapshots from Main,
including immutability and retained old values. It failed on the old binary:
/tmp/pep805-flags-old-ft.log.

Linux aarch64 debug free-threading, GIL and Tier 2 interpreter builds succeeded.
The new snapshot regression passed -R 3:3 in all three. No reference leaks were
reported; free-threading had one non-repeating allocated-block fluctuation
accepted by the runner, GIL/Tier 2 none. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-flags-{ft,gil,tier2}-ref.log.

The broader test_threadgroup, test_context_shareable, test_thread_shareable and

but the other three files still failed; this is not a passing suite. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-flags-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite.log. The next traced constructor failure
is threading.Thread.__init__ saving main-owned sys.stderr at line 1182. Flags
sharing is fixed, but worker-created child Threads remain broken at this later
boundary. The native exception-reporting fallback exists, yet construction
cannot currently reach it. Avoid promoting arbitrary streams or swallowing
exception output when fixing this. The remaining failures, full native/frame
and active-stack audit, native JIT, trace-refs and Windows validation are pending.

### Child Thread construction and saved diagnostic streams

Thread.__init__ now captures stderr through a private native helper. It retains
an accessible stream (including None) unchanged. Only ownership/protection
access errors select the existing locally owned raw-printer fallback; unrelated
errors propagate. Foreign streams are neither used nor promoted. This allows a
worker to construct children without acquiring Main's stderr and retains a
usable diagnostic path when sys.stderr later becomes None.

New subprocess regressions cover worker-created children in Main and another
group, a saved fallback after sys.stderr is cleared and the parent exits, and
construction while a protected stream's lock is not held. The first case failed
at Thread.__init__ on the old binary:
/tmp/pep805-thread-stderr-old-ft.log.

The saved-fallback case also exposed a default-argument problem: a Thread built
in one group and started in another retained a LOCAL empty kwargs dictionary.
The omitted-kwargs default is now an empty frozendict. Supplied kwargs retain
the existing snapshot-at-start behavior; this is not a blanket promotion of
user containers. Initial regression logs before this second fix are:
/tmp/pep805-thread-stderr-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite-initial.log.

Linux aarch64 debug free-threading, GIL and Tier 2 interpreter rebuilds succeeded.
All three passed test_thread_error_output, test_context_shareable,
test_thread_invocation and test_threading: 270 tests each, with five skips in
free-threading and four in GIL/Tier 2. The seventeen error-output/context tests
also passed -R 3:3 in every build without measured reference or allocated-block
leaks. Logs: /tmp/pep805-thread-stderr-{ft,gil,tier2}-{suite,ref}.log.
git diff --check passed.

The repeated free-threading added-test audit ran 556 tests across 45 files:

test_context_shareable and test_threadgroup now pass completely. The failures
remain authoritative outstanding work, not a green full suite. Log:
/tmp/pep805-added-after-stderr-ft.log.

This fixes constructor and saved-stream boundaries, not general I/O sharing or
Python exception-hook failure fallback. The full PEP, native JIT, trace-refs and
Windows validation remain unfinished.

### Attribute API acquisition coverage

The attribute-access audit found that foreign payload arguments were rejected
before the worker could build its carriers. The tests now pass immutable holder

all assertions run after leaving that exemption. This covers ordinary instances,
slots, properties, __getattribute__, __getattr__, modules, classes and the legacy
native tp_getattr fixture.

A native helper invokes a C callable and wraps its successful result in a tuple
before returning to the VM. Negative acquisition tests deliberately do not
unpack that result. A missing C API result check therefore cannot be hidden by
the VM's call-result check. Checked/suppressed hasattr variants still verify
exception propagation versus unraisable-hook reporting, and accessible/local
values and missing attributes retain their existing expectations. Weakrefs
continue to verify cleanup. Initial failures:
/tmp/pep805-attribute-audit-ft.log.

worker argument acquisition is obsolete too; its deferred class-attribute case
also uses a now-shareable read-only closure as an alleged foreign LOCAL value.
Its FunctionType fixtures pass globals() from a foreign-owned namespace, which
must also be reviewed. Specialized receiver tests must distinguish earlier
input/LOAD_FAST rejection from proof of an individual attribute opcode's check.
No specialization test was weakened or marked passing in this increment.

### Specialized attribute acquisition and getter cleanup

The VM attribute fixtures now pass immutable payload holders to workers and
install foreign attributes only while StopTheWorld is active. All cold reads,
warmup, specialized reads and recovery checks run after that exemption ends.
FunctionType uses fresh worker-owned globals. Allegedly foreign function values
now contain real nonlocal writes, so read-only closure sharing cannot turn a
negative fixture into a legitimately shareable function. Weakref cleanup and
specialized opcode assertions remain in place.

Stored-attribute and non-call super probes discard the acquired value and return
an immutable constant. This prevents an outer RETURN_VALUE check from masking
missing attribute acquisition checks. Local-value recovery is verified separately.

The former inlined-return receiver test now explicitly tests native entry into
a specialized accessor using PyObject_Vectorcall. Its foreign receiver can be
rejected at LOAD_FAST; this is not presented as isolated proof of a getter's
return guard. A separate new test warms property and __getattribute__ inlining,
then has getter-frame cleanup exit the owning protective-lock context before
returning self. The caller discards the attribute value and returns a constant,
so its own return-value check cannot hide a missing post-cleanup guard. It asserts
UnprotectedAccessException and verifies the lock was actually released.

### Subscription acquisition fixture reachability

The subscription tests now pass foreign values inside immutable payload holders,
then build the worker-owned containers only under StopTheWorld. The tested
subscription reads run outside that exemption. The specialized accessor discards
its acquired result and returns a constant, preventing its own RETURN_VALUE
check from masking a missing builtin subscription acquisition check. Worker
fixtures take per-case values as explicit arguments, avoiding closure rebinding
across the factory loop. All specialized opcode, rejection-count, recovery and
weakref-cleanup assertions remain.

Monitoring is configured in Main, where the native monitoring control functions
are accessible. Its callback uses a SynchronizedList collector. The test verifies
100 legal return events and no event exposing the foreign value; an earlier
attribute acquisition check may reject that value before the return event, so
this is not isolated proof of the instrumentation return guard.

The delegating __getitem__ fixture's nested attribute access now already rejects
its foreign value. Its obsolete fallback returned 42, a legal subscription
result, while expecting the outer subscription to reject it. The fixture now
records the nested rejection and re-raises; it asserts all 100 nested rejections
as well as the outer failures. This does not claim to isolate a nested ordinary
call's return guard.

A new regression warms BINARY_OP_SUBSCR_GETITEM while the protective context is
held. Getter frame cleanup then releases that context before returning self.
The caller discards the result, so only an earlier check can reject it; the test
asserts UnprotectedAccessException and verifies that cleanup released the lock.
No interpreter production code changed in this increment.

### Rejected generator yield at the interpreter C return boundary

Restoring the PyIter_Next/PyIter_Send foreign-result fixtures exposed a real
runtime abort: gen_send_ex2 asserted that GENERATOR_YIELD always had a non-NULL
result. INTERPRETER_EXIT can reject a foreign yielded object after the producer
has already suspended, returning NULL with an access exception. The assertion
therefore failed instead of reporting the access exception to the consumer.
Reproducer log: /tmp/pep805-iterator-native-ft-target.log (exit 134).

The GENERATOR_YIELD path now handles NULL with an existing exception by returning
PYGEN_ERROR and setting *presult to NULL. It preserves the generator's suspended
state. The exception is neither injected into the producer nor treated as normal
exhaustion. The existing recovery assertions verify the following next() returns
42 and then exhausts normally, including through both native iterator APIs.

Three iterator test fixtures now pass immutable foreign-value holders, construct
worker-owned iterators under StopTheWorld, and execute the operations outside the
pause. Factory/getter/mode inputs are explicit worker arguments, avoiding closure
rebinding across cases. PyIter_Next/PyIter_NextItem native results are wrapped by
the existing C helper before returning to Python, preventing an outer VM result
check from masking API behavior. PyIter_Send already returns (status, result).
Python fallback implementations can reject at their own attribute access; those
cases do not isolate individual native result guards. Identity, weakref cleanup,
positive values/statuses and exhaustion checks remain in place.

All three Linux aarch64 debug builds succeeded. The three repaired iterator
tests passed in free-threading, GIL and Tier 2. The standard generator,
generator-expression, coroutine, async-generator and yield-from suites passed
303 tests in each build. Logs: /tmp/pep805-generator-yield-{ft,gil,tier2}-build.log,
/tmp/pep805-generator-yield-{ft,gil,tier2}-{target,suite}.log.

All three repaired tests passed -R 3:3 in all three builds without measured
reference or allocated-block leaks. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-generator-yield-{ft,gil,tier2}-ref.log.
The full iterator audit still fails, now with 16 errors instead of 43:
/tmp/pep805-iterator-after-yield-ft.log. git diff --check passed.
The full PEP reference implementation and its remaining native API, active-stack
and platform audits remain incomplete.

### Async yield wrapper input validation

The restored async-generator result test exposed premature access rejection in
_CALL_INTRINSIC_1. Its general input check ran before INTRINSIC_ASYNC_GEN_WRAP,
raising IllegalThreadAccessException inside the producer at its yield expression.
The producer's rejection handler therefore fired, rather than leaving the async
generator suspended for its next yield. Evidence:
/tmp/pep805-generator-methods-ft-target.log and
/tmp/pep805-asyncgen-diagnostic-ft.log.

INTRINSIC_ASYNC_GEN_WRAP now skips that input check: the intrinsic only stores
an owned reference in its internal wrapper. The existing consumer-side
async_gen_unwrap_value check validates the actual value before exposing it via
StopIteration. Other unary intrinsics retain their input checks, and the wrapper
result still passes the ordinary result check. Generated Tier 1, Tier 2 and
internal-test cases were regenerated.

### Inlined generator yield/return and monitoring fixtures

The three inlined generator tests now construct generators with foreign values
only under StopTheWorld, using immutable payload holders as worker arguments.
Resumption, specialized reads and recovery run outside that exemption. The yield
fixture takes its mode explicitly and creates fresh worker-owned globals, avoiding
rebound captured cells and foreign globals across its two cases. The return
fixtures use fresh SynchronizedDict globals shared by their normal and monitored
worker invocations; they do not share the test module's namespace.

Monitoring control functions run in Main. After the unmonitored specialization
and rejection checks, a separate worker invokes the same accessor with local and
foreign return values while STOP_ITERATION monitoring is enabled. Synchronized
collectors record callback entry before inspecting the event value. Both the
single event count and its value 42 are asserted: a rejected value read inside
the callback cannot conceal delivery of an invalid event. Monitoring is cleared
in a finally block.

after leaving the pause. Async context managers are constructed before entering
the consuming coroutine, so a forbidden __init__ argument cannot stand in for
rejection while accessing __aenter__/__aexit__ results. Per-case mode/result inputs
are explicit worker arguments, avoiding rebound closure cells.

anext is a Python builtin implementation in this branch (Lib/_pybuiltins.py),

The frame API caller audit also found that Python/_warnings.c already distinguishes
NULL-with-error from no frame, whereas Python/errors.c's unraisable-exception
path currently relies on PyThreadState_GetFrame suppressing materialization
errors. Changing that API to propagate allocation/access errors therefore needs
an explicit best-effort error-clear at the latter call site. The private
_PyThreadState_GetFrame helper is an unguarded walk through current_frame and

plus thread-list-lock pattern for remote enumeration. No frame API fix has yet

The verbose interpreter-pool reproduction completed: 47 tests, three errors,
two failures, four skips. The common failure is importing datetime from a
subinterpreter after Main has imported it: `from _datetime import *` raises an
IllegalThreadAccessException for a Main-owned object, which causes unpickling
and executor initialization to fail. This is not yet attributed to a baseline.

A minimal direct reproduction confirmed the distinction: importing only
_datetime in Main, then importing it in a subinterpreter and reading its public
attributes succeeds. Importing datetime in Main first, then reading _datetime
attributes in a subinterpreter rejects UTC and all six public static types
(date, datetime, time, timedelta, timezone, tzinfo). Thus first-access/static
object state initialization is implicated; blindly synchronizing the entire
module is not an established fix. The full-suite process is still running.

### Explicit datetime static sharing state

_datetime initializes its six public static types through
_PyStaticType_InitForExtension, which intentionally does not declare arbitrary
extension types shareable. Its UTC and zero-timedelta singletons likewise had no
explicit sharing declaration. First access consequently bound these process-wide
objects to one interpreter's Main group, breaking later interpreter imports.

_PyDateTime_InitTypes now explicitly initializes the six immutable static types
and the UTC/zero-delta singletons as IMMUTABLE with no owner. This follows the
bootstrap state initialization used for core static types, without changing the
policy for arbitrary extension types or ordinary datetime instances. The static
zero-delta hash cache already uses atomic loads/stores.

New test_datetime_shareable starts a fresh process, first imports datetime in
Main, then creates/destroys two interpreters. Each imports datetime, verifies the
six types and UTC/zero-offset sharing state, exercises UTC identity, offset
hashing and date construction, and checks Main remains usable afterwards.
The regression fails on the unchanged free-threading binary with the original
import-time IllegalThreadAccessException: /tmp/pep805-datetime-old-ft.log.

both. Their datetime/interpreter-pool suites are running. The free-threading
full-suite audit still uses the pre-fix binary; it must finish before that build
is rebuilt and the fix is tested there. Do not interpret its later failures as
results for this new source change. Full PEP completion remains unproven.

### Native thread-state frame acquisition

PyThreadState_GetFrame now stabilizes another thread's stack using the global
stop-the-world mechanism before walking or materializing frames. It rejects
a target in another ThreadGroup unless the caller holds the debugger pause.
It acquires a strong reference before restarting threads and validates the
returned frame's access state. The caller remains responsible for target
PyThreadState lifetime. Current-thread calls avoid a global pause. The legacy
materialization-allocation-error clearing behavior is preserved, including
leaving a pre-existing exception alone when no frame exists. Unraisable-error
reporting clears best-effort acquisition errors before handling the original
exception. The C API documentation describes group checks and caller lifetime
obligations.

Two frame regression tests cover current-thread identity, remote same-group
acquisition, foreign-group rejection, debugger acquisition and rejection of
the resulting foreign frame after leaving the debugger pause. Native helpers
retain the target thread state only as a capsule; tests keep the worker alive
until inspection finishes. The native frame result is wrapped in a tuple so
a missing native check cannot be hidden by the VM's return-value check.
Allocation-failure and broader thread-state lifetime audits remain outstanding.

warning registries, sys and test_capi.test_misc): 442 tests, ten skips each.
Logs: /tmp/pep805-threadstate-frame-{gil,tier2}-suite.log. A subsequent tiny
correction preserved the no-frame pre-existing-exception behavior, followed by
rebuilds and the frame/WeakSet leak runs below.

### Retired-owner finalizer regression and GIL-wide audit

The dict-subclass namespace limitation remains unresolved. In-place replacement
with the exact SynchronizedDict type would destroy subtype behavior/storage;
a snapshot would disconnect existing aliases. No such workaround was applied.

A lifetime probe found a concrete production defect: a worker group creates
a local object containing its own local list, then returns an immutable tuple
containing the object. Once that worker exits, the main group drops the tuple.
The object's __del__ enters in the main group and fails when accessing its
local list. Both free-threading and Tier 2 produce
['entered', 'IllegalThreadAccessException'] instead of completing cleanup.
The existing native finalizer callback path supplies a foreign self to Python;
checking one returned value cannot fix finalizer execution context.

Lib/test/test_finalizer_access.py records this as an ordinary failing regression,
not an expected failure or skipped test. It requires finalizer cleanup to
complete and captures unraisable exceptions in a synchronized collector inside
an isolated subprocess. Confirmed failures:
/tmp/pep805-finalizer-access-ft.log and
/tmp/pep805-finalizer-access-tier2.log. No runtime fix has yet been made.
The prior 46-module added-test green baseline is therefore no longer a claim
about all current added tests; there are now 47 modules and this known failure.

A full GIL audit started with -j2 --timeout 600, using the already built GIL
interpreter. It is live in session 86792 and writes
/tmp/pep805-full-gil-audit.log. It collected 549 files before the finalizer test
was added. Do not rebuild that tree until the audit is terminal. Its current
progress must be checked from the session/log rather than inferred from this
entry. No finalizer test was copied into that running audit's source tree.

### Native entry into Python functions

The finalizer investigation exposed a more general input boundary gap:
_PyFunction_Vectorcall read function internals without validating access, and
_PyEval_Vector copied native argument references into a Python frame without
the bytecode call-input checks. Native vectorcall could therefore enter a
Python body with a foreign local argument or an unprotected argument.

The function vectorcall entry now checks the function before reading its code
and globals; _PyEval_Vector checks its function, keyword names and every
argument before creating frame references. This covers direct native entry
without claiming complete callback/GC/state-transition safety. Call-protocol
documentation describes the Python-function entry checks.

Lib/test/test_native_python_call.py tests positional and keyword arguments,
foreign local callables, and successful protected arguments while their lock
context is held. A nested native vectorcall wrapper reaches a foreign callable
without an earlier VM acquisition check masking the result. Before the fix,
the first two tests produced four failures in free-threading; all three tests
produce five failures in the still-old GIL binary:
/tmp/pep805-native-python-old-ft.log and
/tmp/pep805-native-python-old-gil.log.

Free-threading and Tier 2 were rebuilt. All three new tests pass -R 3:3 with
no reported leaks: /tmp/pep805-native-python-{ft,tier2}-ref.log. Nine related
suites ran 326 tests with passing test assertions, but test_protected_call_result
reported an environment change from an unraisable UnprotectedAccessException.
Its protected result is finalized after its source finalizer releases the
protective context; the new function-entry check now correctly denies that
unsafe finalizer invocation. This is not a clean suite result. No fixture
exception suppression was added. Logs:
/tmp/pep805-native-python-{ft,tier2}-suite.log.

The retired-owner regression remains failing, now recording only
['IllegalThreadAccessException'] rather than entering __del__ before failing on
its local field. Finalizer execution context for both local and protected
objects remains a production gap; entry validation does not implement it.
The regression was not weakened: /tmp/pep805-finalizer-after-native-ft.log.
There are now 48 added modules and known failures, so historical green counts
must not be used for the current worktree.

The GIL-wide audit remains live in session 86792 and its source/binary were
not changed for this native-entry fix. Synchronization, rebuilding and GIL
validation of Objects/call.c, Python/ceval.c and the new tests are pending
that audit's termination. All free-threading/Tier 2 sessions from this
increment are terminal. git diff --check passed.

### Native call result boundary and shared enum descriptors

_Py_CheckFunctionResult now validates the owned result after checking the
existing result/exception invariants. The no-keyword fast path in
_PyVectorcall_Call also routes through that validation; it previously returned
directly. This prevents a C caller from receiving an inaccessible result before
Python's later VM return checks. The regression uses a deliberately unchecked
native tuple-item return and a second native caller that wraps its result,
preventing the VM from masking a missing C boundary check. Foreign and
unprotected results failed to raise in the old binary (two failures):
/tmp/pep805-native-result-old-ft.log. Accessible local and protected results
inside the owning context retain identity.

The expanded added-test run exposed Shareable.name/value relying on local
enum.property descriptors inherited from Enum. Shareable now creates and
freezes its own enum.property instances, without freezing or synchronizing
Enum globally. Worker reads of both attributes are tested, along with
read-only descriptor state and the original AttributeError for class lookup.
A builtin-property draft was replaced to preserve those Enum semantics.

The broad C API run also exposed an error in the earlier empty-thread-state
test cleanup: PyThreadState_Clear(empty) could reclaim abandoned mimalloc
pages while a different thread state was attached, triggering an allocator
assertion. GDB located this at the test's Clear call:
/tmp/pep805-empty-tstate-ft-gdb.log. Cleanup now swaps to the empty state,
clears it, restores the original state, then deletes the empty state. Swaps
are outside assert expressions so they also run with NDEBUG.

### Native callable receiver validation

The common vectorcall and tp_call entry paths now validate the callable
before reading its vectorcall pointer or call slot. The new native-call
regression unwraps a foreign LOCAL C function inside native wrappers, avoiding
a Python bytecode access check that would mask the boundary under test. All
four call routes entered the C function before this change (see
/tmp/pep805-native-callable-old-ft.log). The public vectorcall pointer getter
and direct calls to saved slot/function pointers still require a separate
audit; this change does not validate all arguments to arbitrary C functions.

The first rebuilt test runs exposed a startup dependency: Thread.start passed
a LOCAL bound method into another group. Rejecting it left start waiting for
the worker startup event. The isolated 15-second diagnostic in
/tmp/pep805-native-callable-diagnostic.log records this failure. These initial
suite runs were terminated, not counted as passes. A closure-based bootstrap
passed native-call tests but failed the free-threading native parallelism
barrier: constructing a closure during start introduces a world stop while
another group executes the native barrier. That approach was removed.

Python bound methods now inherit synchronized receiver state only when their
function is also synchronized or immutable. Local receivers and local functions
still produce local methods, while protected receivers retain the existing
protection inheritance. Calls still validate the underlying function and self;
a later change in the function's sharing state cannot bypass those checks.
Thread.start keeps its existing bound-method bootstrap. The regression covers
cross-group invocation and checks that local receiver methods remain local.

immutable tuples. Local containers, generator/coroutine frames and closure
cells are prepared while StopTheWorld is held; the operations under test run
after leaving that context. This does not promote foreign values, disable

### Owner scheduler lookup and lifetime foundation

Local objects retain only an owner ID. Previously, deleting the last thread
state and ThreadGroup wrapper also freed that group's native scheduler, even
if a foreign immutable container still retained its local objects. A later
finalizer dispatch could therefore neither locate nor serialize against the
owning group. The PEP specifies legal access and reclamation behavior, but
not a particular destructor dispatch mechanism or OS-thread affinity.

Each interpreter now maintains a mutex-protected registry of its native group
states. Creation publishes a registry-owned reference; lookup by ID returns a
new strong reference and cannot resolve a mutex ID or a different interpreter's
group. Interpreter destruction detaches the registry and releases its native
references. Existing wrapper/thread references remain independent, including
for detached daemon states that can outlive interpreter teardown. Registry
cleanup never decrefs a Python object or calls a Python finalizer.

This deliberately retains native scheduling metadata until interpreter
teardown. It does not retain Python ThreadGroup wrappers. Releasing unused
metadata earlier requires tracking the lifetime of objects carrying each
owner ID; that reclamation/performance work remains. The lookup is currently
a linear scan. This is a prerequisite, not finalizer dispatch: existing
finalizer regression failures remain, and no ownership/access bypass has
been added. Tests cover retired workers/wrappers, separation from mutex IDs,
and isolation between interpreters.

The owner-registry change built successfully in all three configurations:
/tmp/pep805-owner-registry-{ft,gil,tier2}-build.log. The embedding helper was
also verified up to date against the newly built static library. ThreadGroup,
low-level interpreter, and embedding suites passed 186 tests per configuration
(nine FT skips, ten GIL/Tier 2 skips), including repeated initialization and
interpreter destruction: /tmp/pep805-owner-registry-{ft,gil,tier2}-suite.log.
All 27 ThreadGroup tests passed -R 3:3 in each configuration (one GIL/Tier 2
skip) with no reported Python reference leaks:
/tmp/pep805-owner-registry-{ft,gil,tier2}-ref.log. These leak checks do not claim
that retained native registry metadata is reclaimed before interpreter exit.
All build/test sessions in this increment are terminal. Finalizer dispatch,
protected-object cleanup and early registry metadata reclamation remain work
for subsequent increments; the goal remains incomplete.

### Reconstructing a Python group from its owner scheduler

Owner-based execution also needs the Python ThreadGroup identity used by
Thread.group and _thread._current_thread_group. The registry now keeps a
non-owning wrapper pointer, guarded by the scheduler holder mutex. Lookup
acquires a strong reference with _Py_TryIncref while the pointer's storage is
protected; free-threading publication enables the maybe-weakref refcount
state. Deallocation clears the pointer under the same mutex before freeing
the wrapper. Thus the registry still does not keep Python wrappers alive.

If the wrapper has disappeared, lookup constructs an immutable wrapper around
the retained scheduler, then rechecks under the mutex to select one winner
among concurrent publishers. The name is retained in raw UCS4 storage, which
preserves embedded NULs and lone surrogates and can be freed without an attached
thread state. Main initialization copies its name into its actual scheduler
and publishes the wrapper under the same lock. Name buffers are immutable
once published; native metadata and names remain retained until interpreter
teardown, as noted above.

Tests verify live-wrapper identity, reconstruction for None/empty/non-ASCII
names, resumed execution in the original owner group against an old local
payload, continued denial of that payload in Main, and concurrent wrapper
lookup. The initial 29-test focused runs passed in all three configurations;
the final 30-test ThreadGroup -R 3:3 runs also passed with no reported reference

### Dispatching ordinary LOCAL finalizers to their owner group

The ordinary refcount and cyclic-GC finalizer paths now share a helper that
routes a foreign LOCAL object's finalizer to its owning ThreadGroup. A private
native callable retains the object and validates access in the worker. The
caller joins the worker while releasing its group/GIL. The worker is registered
for shutdown; interrupted joins cannot leave a dangling stack argument. GC's
existing finalized marking is preserved, including for resurrection. Ordinary
access checks and the object's sharing state are unchanged. The dispatcher
preserves the caller's raised exception around dispatch errors.

This currently starts a native worker for each foreign finalizer. It is not a
complete finalization policy: protected objects, internal world-stop regions,
and interpreter shutdown still require work. Internal world-stop/shutdown or
missing/replaced _thread module failures are reported as unraisable errors;
those callbacks are not successfully dispatched. No claim of guaranteed
callback execution in those cases is made. Deferred dispatch and efficient
worker reuse remain outstanding.

Tests cover live and retired owner threads, local member mutation, group
identity, cyclic collection, resurrection without repeated finalization, and
preservation of the caller's handled exception. A separate in-process case
exercises reference accounting. Repeated FT testing exposed a test-fixture
reentrancy deadlock: delayed refcount merging could call __del__ inside an
Event.wait while that Event's condition lock was held, and __del__ tried to
set the same Event. Completion notification now uses a distinct Event; the
owner-group and member-access assertions remain intact.

Cross-thread finalization also exposed a debug ref-total accounting error in
_PyObject_ResurrectEndSlow: its owned-object explicit merge subtracted the
temporary reference a second time after _PyObject_ResurrectEnd had accounted
for it. The slow path now compensates for that debug-only duplicate decrement.
The actual object reference-count algorithm is unchanged.

### Retaining native protective mutex state independently of wrappers

Lock and RLock now own a separately allocated native state containing the
mutex, identity and context metadata. States for locks never used by protect()
are freed with their wrappers. Successful first protection also gives an
interpreter-local registry a native reference; subsequent protection does not
register again. The registry is keyed by the existing mutex owner ID, so it
cannot resolve a group ID or a different interpreter's mutex. Interpreter
teardown drops registry references without Python decrefs or callbacks; any
remaining wrapper/lookup references retain their state independently.

This foundation deliberately retains protective mutex state until interpreter
teardown. Earlier reclamation needs object-owner lifetime accounting and is
still outstanding, as is replacing linear lookup for scalability. No Python
lock wrappers or user objects are retained by this registry. Context metadata
continues to be protected by the unique wrapper's existing critical section;
a finalizer acquisition path will need a shared native metadata guard before
using this state independently of the wrapper. The state-lifetime change does
not yet acquire mutexes for finalizers or fix protected cleanup exceptions.

Tests exercise native-state lookup after wrapper weakrefs clear, repeated
protection without duplicate registration, continued access denial without
the lock, and interpreter isolation/teardown for both Lock and RLock.

### Acquiring protective mutexes for finalization

Ordinary refcount and cyclic-GC finalization now acquire a protected object's
native mutex if the current thread does not already hold it. This uses the
retained registry state directly and does not need a Python Lock/RLock wrapper
or the _thread module object. Existing ownership is reused without recursion
or an extra release. The dispatcher preserves the caller's raised exception.

Lock/RLock context operations now use the state's native metadata mutex rather
than a wrapper-specific object critical section. These guarded regions neither
allocate Python objects, call Python, nor detach. Waiting for the protecting
mutex still uses the existing parking and group/GIL release path. A successful
acquisition records a context epoch; cleanup only releases that same context.
Thus a finalizer may explicitly exit and reacquire the lock without cleanup
releasing its new acquisition or a different thread's context.

Contended finalization still cannot wait while the runtime is internally
stopping the world or finalizing the interpreter; such failure remains an
unraisable error pending deferred-dispatch/shutdown work. An immediately
available mutex can be acquired without waiting. Explicit debugger world-stop
access continues to use its existing authorization. This is not a complete
finalization, deadlock avoidance, or shutdown policy.

New tests cover Lock/RLock, live and retired wrappers, cycles, resurrection,
caller exception preservation, no leaked mutex access, already-held contexts,
explicit callback release/reacquisition, and a finalizer waiting for a lock
held by another group. In-process cases support native-path reference checks.
The existing protected-call-result regression is retained without suppressing
its unraisable exception. Validation results follow after completion.

### Deferring GC-enabled finalizers reached during an internal world stop

A new private C test helper drops a factory's last result reference during an
interpreter-local internal world stop, without granting debugger access. The
initial FT regression aborted for both local and foreign owner groups: the
local callback re-entered Python while world_stopped, and the foreign error
path re-entered Python through sys.unraisablehook. Log:
/tmp/pep805-stopped-finalizer-ft-regression.log. The helper's initial build
attempt referenced non-exported StopTheWorldAll symbols and failed module
import despite make returning zero; the corrected helper uses the exported
interpreter-local stop/start functions. Its final helper build succeeded:
/tmp/pep805-stopped-finalizer-ft-build2.log.

GC-enabled types now retain a strong reference in an interpreter-owned native
list when their finalizer is reached during an internal world stop. The
original caller still marks the object finalized. The pending-call checkpoint
detaches a batch and invokes the shared finalizer runner directly after the
world restarts, then releases each reference. This does not consume slots in
the bounded pending-call queue. Existing group/lock acquisition applies after
resumption; the finalized flag prevents repeated finalization on reference
release. Pre-finalization draining and pending-work checks include this list.
Explicit interpreter clearing cancels residual callbacks and releases their
references; late shutdown execution guarantees are still incomplete.

The implementation currently covers types participating in cyclic GC only.
Non-GC native tp_finalize handling still needs separate once/resurrection
bookkeeping. Raw queue-record allocation failure is currently fatal rather
than attempting an unsafe Python error hook while stopped; recoverable failure
handling remains work. Earlier native owner/mutex reclamation and deferred
callback scheduling/reentrancy audits remain outstanding. This is not a full
shutdown or finalization implementation.

### Deferring complete deallocation during internal world stops

A payload with no __del__ but a weakref callback reproduced the invalid Python
reentrancy assertion under an internal stop (exit 134):
/tmp/pep805-stopped-weakref-repro.log. Deferring only tp_finalize cannot cover
weakref callbacks, capsules or other native deallocators.

_Py_Dealloc now retains a zero-reference object before entering its deallocator
when the current thread owns an internal stop. The interpreter's raw deferred
cleanup queue distinguishes these records from explicit finalizer-only records.
At a pending-call checkpoint after restart, dropping the retained reference
resumes ordinary deallocation. The free-threaded queue reference is merged so
another draining thread can release it. The trace-refs bookkeeping follows the
existing resurrection protocol, but a trace-refs build has not been validated.
Finalizer-only records retain the existing GC finalized-flag protocol.

Internal stop depth is tracked on the thread state, avoiding a global lock on
every deallocation. Normal GIL no-op stops remain no-ops; debugger-authorized
execution remains allowed outside nested internal stops. Both parent and child
fork paths pair their runtime stop with a restart. Queue publication only sets
the current thread's eval breaker: real stop callers may already hold HEAD_LOCK.
The restart wrappers notify all interpreter threads after releasing stop locks,
avoiding recursive acquisition of the thread-list mutex.

The drop helper now exercises this held-HEAD_LOCK case. Additional regressions
cover weakrefs, non-GC capsule destructors, explicit GC finalization exactly
once, and nested debugger/internal stops in all configurations. The 1,000-object
regression drops independent references inside the stop, preserving its queue
capacity coverage rather than merely deferring the outer tuple's deallocation.

### Allocation-free explicit finalization and overlapping cleanup requests

The preceding turn changed and validated authoritative implementation state;
it was progress toward the persistent PEP objective, not completion.
A new isolated fault-injection regression reproduces abort in the remaining
explicit-finalizer raw allocation path:
/tmp/pep805-finalizer-intrusive-ft-baseline.log. The helper installs failing
RAW allocation hooks only for PyObject_CallFinalizer under an internal stop,
then restores them before allowing queued callbacks to execute. Its baseline
helper build completed successfully:
/tmp/pep805-finalizer-intrusive-ft-baseline-build.log.

Deferred cleanup now uses one intrusive object list for both ordinary
deallocation and explicit GC finalization. One queue reference pins each
object. A queued flag and a finalize-request flag are guarded by the existing
interpreter cleanup mutex; they use existing object-header padding without
increasing its size. Object allocation initializes the flags and link, and
consumption clears both before invoking a callback or dropping the pin.
No cleanup record allocation or corresponding fatal allocation branch remains.

An object waiting for deallocation can be reacquired through a weak reference
and explicitly finalized before the pending checkpoint. The queue therefore
promotes an existing record rather than inserting the same intrusive link
twice. This remains supported after a consumer detaches a batch: membership
and request flags stay live until that individual item is consumed. A C helper
and regression retain the reacquired object beyond the checkpoint and verify
that its requested finalizer runs exactly once while it is still alive.
The fault-injection and overlap regressions run inside a debugger pause with a
nested internal stop, exercising GIL and FT configurations alike.

A further regression promotes a detached-batch item from another object's
finalizer, while the pending-call handler is already active. Weakrefs verify
that queue pins are released after all three new scenarios. The test size
calculator now describes the additional flag byte within the old padding.

skip and ten GIL/Tier 2 skips, with --fail-env-changed:
/tmp/pep805-finalizer-intrusive-{ft,gil,tier2}-added.log.

### Preserving explicit finalization counts for non-GC types

The prior turn completed implementation changes and verified regressions; it
was progress, not completion of the persistent PEP objective. Inspection of
Doc/c-api/lifecycle.rst confirms that PyObject_CallFinalizer deduplicates GC
objects only. A native test type without Py_TPFLAGS_HAVE_GC reproduces the
internal-stop reentrancy assertion for explicit finalization, with and without
RAW allocation failure injection:
/tmp/pep805-nongc-finalizer-ft-baseline-verbose.log.
Its helper build completed successfully; the initial nonverbose test log is
/tmp/pep805-nongc-finalizer-ft-baseline.log.

The queue now records a size_t count of pending finalizer calls instead of a
single request bit. GC callers retain their existing finalized-bit deduplication;
non-GC callers retain each explicit request. Consumers detach and clear the
count together with queue membership, then execute that many callbacks after
restart. Non-GC objects still have their normal finalizer call on subsequent
deallocation, and resurrection retains the existing non-GC behavior. Queue
publication remains allocation-free. An explicit size_t overflow guard avoids
silently wrapping the request count.

This reference implementation adds one size_t word to the common object header
for the count, in addition to the existing cleanup link. This is a material
memory cost; compact storage and performance remain outstanding work rather
than an achieved PEP performance result. Allocation initializes the count to
zero, and the size-test format includes the actual added field.

New regressions use a truly non-GC native type, verify 1,000 explicit requests
with/without allocation failure, distinguish ordinary FT internal stops from
nested debugger stops, preserve GC deduplication under the same 1,000-request
load, and exercise native resurrection followed by repeated explicit requests
and final deallocation. Weak references to the native heap type verify that
queue retention does not leak its instance/type after cleanup.

ten GIL/Tier 2 skips, using --fail-env-changed:
/tmp/pep805-nongc-finalizer-{ft,gil,tier2}-added.log.

The related ten-file suite passed all configurations with --fail-env-changed:

Inspection of the reference-chain implementation showed unnecessary removal
and reinsertion during deallocation deferral. The hook runs before _Py_Dealloc
forgets the object, and _Py_AddToAllObjects is idempotent. Reinsertion allocates
a native hash-table entry (using libc allocation, deliberately independent of
Python fault-injection hooks). The deferral path now asserts that the tracing
record is present and retains it through temporary resurrection, so no tracing
record needs to be allocated at that point. Actual deallocation still removes
the record normally.

while an explicitly finalized GC object is kept alive after the checkpoint.

Weakref callback registration context
------------------------------------

Weakref callbacks now retain their registration ThreadGroup independently of
weakref/referent sharing state. This is a reference-implementation choice for
callback affinity, not an explicit additional requirement in the PEP text.
Previously, dropping the last reference from Main after a producer group exited
caused IllegalThreadAccessException in a callback accessing its local closure.
The minimal reproducer and before/after logs are
/tmp/pep805-weakref-owner-repro.py and
/tmp/pep805-weakref-owner-{before,after}.log.

Reference-count destruction and both cyclic collectors use the same callback
helper. Foreign callbacks reuse the native group-worker start/join machinery
used for finalizers; the worker retains both weakref and callback. Registration
context remains available after wr_callback is cleared, including for exact
SYNCHRONIZED weakrefs whose ordinary owner ID is zero. The weakref layout grows
by a uint32_t plus alignment padding; sizeof tests reflect the actual layout.
No access checks on callback or argument are bypassed.

New tests cover retired producer threads, LOCAL and SYNCHRONIZED referents,
cyclic and non-cyclic destruction, local closure mutation, exactly-once delivery,
reverse registration order, and continuing after a callback raises. An in-process
case additionally exercises worker/reference cleanup under refleak testing.
These tests exposed an existing free-threaded GC ordering problem: pushing the
newest-first weakref chain onto a stack reversed callback order. The collector
now reverses that worklist before resuming execution. A separate ordinary
weakref test covers this without ThreadGroups; the failing minimal example is
recorded in /tmp/pep805-weakref-order-before.log.

Group affinity does not preserve original thread identity/thread-local state,
or reacquire protective mutex contexts held when registering a callback. Worker
pooling, shutdown/module availability, interrupted joins and their ordering,
and complete native callback affinity remain unfinished lifecycle work.

  18, 21, 21 and 19. Logs: /tmp/pep805-weakref-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-suite.log.
* The in-process owner-lifetime case passed -R 3:3 in all four builds.
  FT reported the accepted block delta [0, 1, 0]; no reference growth was
  reported. Logs: /tmp/pep805-weakref-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-ref.log.
* FT threading, embedding and finalization tests passed with
  --fail-env-changed: 353 tests, eleven skips.
  Log: /tmp/pep805-weakref-ft-lifecycle.log.

Interrupted cleanup worker joins
--------------------------------

An interrupt while joining a group cleanup worker previously returned control
while the worker was still running. A signal-handler reproducer showed a second
weakref callback running before the first had finished:
/tmp/pep805-interrupted-callback-before2.log. The regression matrix also failed
all eight cases on the unchanged GIL binary:
/tmp/pep805-interrupted-callback-gil-before.log.

Internal cleanup joins now keep waiting after pending-call/signal-handler
exceptions, preserving the first exception for reporting once the worker has
finished. Subsequent signals still run their handlers; their exceptions are
cleared. Native join errors retain their normal handling and chain the saved
interrupt. Ordinary Thread.join and shutdown joins continue to use the existing
interruptible behavior. Cleanup still releases the caller's group/GIL while
waiting. This maintains synchronous finalizer completion and weakref callback
ordering; it does not provide cancellation of a callback executing in another
thread, and waiting for a callback that never returns can still hang.

The new isolated regression covers weakref callbacks and __del__, cyclic and
ordinary refcount destruction, and one or three signal-handler exceptions.
It verifies no overlap with following cleanup, reporting of the first exception,
and reclamation of every interrupt exception/traceback using weak references.
The regression requires SIGUSR1 and pthread_kill so interrupts target Main.
A process-directed signal can reach another native thread. Directly
addressing Main still reproduced delayed signal handling in Tier 2: a later
interrupt could be pending before the next native wait began. Cleanup joins now
use waits bounded at 50 ms to service such pending calls. This only changes the
internal cleanup mode; completion events still wake the waiter immediately.
The failing initial Tier 2/TraceRefs suite and focused logs are retained under
/tmp/pep805-interrupted-callback-*-suite.log and
/tmp/pep805-interrupted-callback-tier2-focus{,2}.log.


Final validation for interrupted cleanup joins:

  /tmp/pep805-interrupted-callback-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-build2.log.
* The eight-case regression passed -R 3:3 in all four builds, without reported
  reference/block deltas:
  /tmp/pep805-interrupted-callback-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-repeat.log.
  These are isolated subprocess cases; -R measures their parent harness.
  Exception lifetime inside each subprocess is checked explicitly by weakrefs.
* Finalizer access, weakref, GC, threading and finalization tests all passed
  --fail-env-changed in all four builds: 491 tests each, respectively 16, 18,
  18 and 17 skips:
  /tmp/pep805-interrupted-callback-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-suite2.log.
* git diff --check passes; changed sources match all four build trees. All
  build and test sessions from this increment are terminal.

This resolves the reproduced interrupted-join completion/order failure and
missed signal handling between cleanup waits. It does not close the remaining
PEP conformance work, native callback audit, shutdown scheduling, worker-pool
performance or native-JIT/Windows validation.

List subscription callback lifetimes
------------------------------------

Native list subscription could continue after __index__ released the list's
protecting context. A raw C call wrapped its result in a tuple, demonstrating
that an inaccessible protected list still yielded 42:
/tmp/pep805-list-index-lifetime.py. The initial ten read regressions failed on
the unchanged GIL binary: /tmp/pep805-list-index-gil-before.log.

List integer/subscript and slice paths now revalidate the receiver after index
conversion, before normalizing indexes or reading list storage. This includes
the dedicated _PyList_BinarySlice path used by value[start:stop]. Assignment and
deletion similarly revalidate after integer/slice conversion. Slice assignment
also revalidates after materializing its replacement iterable, which may execute
arbitrary Python. Failure releases the temporary replacement sequence and
returns without performing the native list mutation.

The audit also exposed the exact-list fast return in PySequence_Fast: it could
return an inaccessible replacement list after an index callback released that
list's separate protecting lock. That return now uses the standard nullable
access checker, preserving owned-reference/error semantics. Its C API docs
clarify access validation and the caller's responsibility to maintain protection
when subsequently using direct-access macros.

Six new test methods cover 46 cases across Lock/RLock: integer and negative
indices; slice start, stop and step conversion; extended slices; reading,
assignment and deletion; iterable materialization; binary slice bytecode; and
independently protected replacement lists. Mutation tests also verify that the
native operation leaves the destination unchanged after protection is lost.
Before the corresponding fixes, mutation/materialization produced 28 failures
and binary-slice/source-list paths produced six failures:
/tmp/pep805-list-index-mutation-before.log and
/tmp/pep805-list-index-additional-before.log.

These checks cover the demonstrated conversion/materialization boundaries.
They do not complete all list callbacks, allocation/finalizer reentrancy,
other native receiver lifetimes or the evaluation-stack audit.

  protected instances, C API abstract and list) passed --fail-env-changed in

  /tmp/pep805-list-index-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-suite-final.log.
* The six in-process callback-lifetime tests passed -R 3:3 in all four builds,
  with no reported reference/block growth:
  /tmp/pep805-list-index-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-final-ref.log.

List search and equality callback lifetimes
------------------------------------------

List membership, index, count, remove and equality could continue accessing a
protected list after an equality callback released its context. remove could
still delete an element. Optional index bounds could also release protection
before index normalized negative bounds using the list length. The unchanged
GIL build produced twelve assertion failures and four unexpected ValueErrors
across these lost-protection cases:
/tmp/pep805-list-compare-gil-before.log.

Search/count/removal now revalidate the receiver before subsequent storage
reads or removal. index validates the receiver after its clinic wrapper has
converted optional bounds. List equality validates both lists before reading
their fields and again after releasing temporary references for equal items,
before the next iteration or final length comparison. Checks after comparison
also cover a temporary item's destructor releasing the context. Original
comparison exceptions take precedence and are preserved.

Three new test methods cover forty cases: Lock/RLock; membership, index, count,
remove and equality with protection on either side; direct loss of protection;
original comparison exceptions; destruction of an item removed from its list
by its own equality callback; and optional index bounds. The destructor cases
separately failed eight assertions and raised four unexpected ValueErrors before
the fix: /tmp/pep805-list-compare-gil-finalizer-before.log. Tests verify that
remove does not perform a native mutation after protection is lost, while
retaining mutations performed by the callback itself.

Remaining audit work includes other list callbacks such as sorting, child-object
acquisition/comparison boundaries, allocation reentrancy, and the general VM
stack/native lifetime model.

Rich comparison acquisition and dispatch
---------------------------------------

Comparisons could inspect a foreign LOCAL operand through native slots. A float
subclass belonging to Main was successfully searched for inside another group's
list. The regression also demonstrated this through tuple, set and dictionary
operations. PyObject_RichCompare now validates operands before dispatch, checks
again after a slot returns NotImplemented, and validates its owned result before
returning it. This also protects PyObject_RichCompareBool before truth-testing a
native comparison result. Its existing identity shortcut is unchanged.

Tests cover nine container operations on a foreign float subclass, protected
native comparison results under Lock/RLock, and lost protection after left,
subclass-priority and right-hand NotImplemented callbacks. The initial combined
reproducer reported nine unittest failures:
/tmp/pep805-richcompare-before.log.
A small native tuple subtype deliberately returns its stored comparison result
without checking access. The object-result test wraps the API result inside its
C helper, before returning through generic native-call validation; both APIs
have positive controls with protection held. This is necessary because wrapping
a result only after PyObject_Call can mask a missing check in the called API.
Other native API tests using such generic-call wrappers still need a coverage
audit; passing them alone is insufficient evidence for the underlying API.

  /tmp/pep805-richcompare-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-added.log.

  build: 1,065 tests, four skips (default resources):
  /tmp/pep805-richcompare-{ft,gil,tier2,tracerefs}-standard.log.

  block delta [0, 0, 1]; no reference growth was reported:

* Changed sources match across the four build trees, git diff --check passes,
  and all build/test sessions in this increment are terminal.

Remaining work includes the native test-wrapper audit, other numeric and native
acquisition paths, full VM reference-lifetime checks, unified runtime/reference
counting, shutdown and platform/performance validation.


Direct native acquisition test coverage
--------------------------------------

The attribute, sequence and iterator acquisition tests now use
call_cfunction_raw_return_in_tuple. This helper invokes the C function pointer
directly and wraps its result before generic call-result or VM validation can
inspect it. It checks access to the native callable and argument tuple, but
deliberately leaves child arguments and the result unchecked so that the API
under test must enforce its own boundary. The original PyObject_Call-based
helper remains for tests of generic call validation.

New controls demonstrate that the raw helper preserves an inaccessible foreign
or protected result inside its returned tuple; Python indexing still rejects
that result outside the owning context. Calling-convention controls cover
NOARGS, O, VARARGS, FASTCALL, keyword-capable variants, module/instance/class/
static bindings, and METH_METHOD with the defining class retained for inherited
methods. Argument-count errors, native errors and foreign callable rejection
are also checked. The stronger acquisition tests pass without further runtime
changes in this increment.

This removes the identified generic-call masking from these acquisition tests,
but does not complete the native API audit. Numeric slot results and operand

Unary numeric C API acquisition
--------------------------------

PyNumber_Negative, PyNumber_Positive, PyNumber_Absolute and PyNumber_Invert
now validate their operand before native-slot dispatch and validate the owned
slot result before returning it. NULL input handling and native slot exceptions
are preserved. Rejected results release their owned reference through the
existing nullable access-check helper.

The new test_number_access suite invokes the APIs through the raw C function
helper, so generic call checks cannot mask the numeric boundary. A native tuple
subtype deliberately returns its stored child without validation. Tests cover
foreign int-subclass operands, foreign native results, protected native results
under Lock/RLock, protected Python operands, permitted access with protection or
debugger suspension, and native-slot errors. Before the runtime fix, the first
four tests reported 16 failures (the protected-operand test was added later):
/tmp/pep805-unary-before.log.

Binary, ternary, inplace and conversion numeric APIs still require their own
access/lifetime audit. This change does not complete that audit or the remaining
VM lifetime, unified runtime, shutdown, platform and performance work.



Binary numeric dispatch and sequence fallbacks
---------------------------------------------

The common binary numeric dispatcher now checks both operands before selecting
slots and checks its returned owned reference. After a slot returns
NotImplemented it revalidates both operands before trying another slot or a
sequence fallback. This covers the thirteen ordinary binary numeric APIs;
ternary power and native inplace slots retain separate, unfinished paths.
PyNumber_Add validates sequence-concatenation results. The shared repetition
helper validates inputs, revalidates the sequence after __index__, and checks
its result. This also protects the repetition fallback of inplace multiply
before it mutates a sequence whose protective lock was released by __index__.

Native tuple-subtype fixtures return an unchecked stored child from numeric
binary, sequence-concatenation and sequence-repetition slots. Raw-call tests
exercise left/right foreign operands, left/right native slot results, protected
results under Lock/RLock, NotImplemented callbacks releasing protection, and
repetition-count callbacks releasing protection. Repetition must leave its
protected list unchanged. The suite preserves the existing TypeError when a
Python instance on the left of inplace multiply has a sequence table without
repetition: that API does not try the right-hand sequence in this case, and
__index__ must remain uncalled.

The first reproducer reported 134 failures and eight errors:
/tmp/pep805-binary-before.log. Six errors were missing access exceptions; two
were the unsupported inplace case above, subsequently made explicit negative
controls. The final focused run passes 33 tests:
/tmp/pep805-binary-ft-focused.log.

Native inplace slots, ternary power, numeric conversions and their callback

Inplace binary numeric dispatch
-------------------------------

The common inplace binary dispatcher checks both operands before calling a
native inplace slot and checks its owned result before returning it. Its
NotImplemented fallback enters the already checked ordinary binary dispatcher,
which revalidates both operands before continuing. PyNumber_InPlaceAdd now also
checks its sequence-concatenation result. These changes cover the twelve binary
inplace APIs, excluding power, whose ternary dispatcher remains separate.

Native test fixtures now include inplace numeric, concatenation and repetition
slots. New raw-call tests cover foreign operands on either side, protected
right-hand operands, and foreign/protected native results. Positive controls
use held protective locks or debugger suspension. Callback tests verify that a
lost protective lock prevents fallback to __add__, while a callback's raised
LookupError is preserved. Sequence fallback coverage now includes inplace
concatenation and actual inplace repetition slots.

/tmp/pep805-inplace-ft-focused.log.

Power, numeric conversions and their callback lifetimes still need validation.

Power and inplace power acquisition
-----------------------------------

The ternary numeric dispatcher validates base, exponent and modulus before
selecting slots, validates owned slot results, and revalidates all three
operands after NotImplemented. This includes subclass-priority reflected slots
and the modulus operand's native slot. The inplace power dispatcher validates
all three inputs and its owned result; its NotImplemented fallback enters the
checked ordinary ternary dispatcher. Callback exceptions are preserved.

Native tuple-subtype fixtures deliberately expose unchecked stored results
from power slots or invoke a stored callback from the modulus slot. Tests cover
foreign operands in all positions, omitted and supplied modulus, protected
operands, foreign/protected native results from base/exponent/modulus slots,
subclass-priority callbacks that release protection, and left/right/modulus
callbacks returning NotImplemented or raising LookupError after releasing a
protective lock. Both Lock and RLock and both public power APIs are exercised.

/tmp/pep805-power-before.log. A sixth test then added explicit modulus-slot
callback coverage. The final focused run passes 50 tests:
/tmp/pep805-power-ft-focused.log.

Numeric conversions remain unaudited: _PyNumber_Index currently returns an

Index conversion and warning-handler lifetime
--------------------------------------------

_PyNumber_Index checks access to its operand before accepting an int subclass
or invoking nb_index. It validates the owned native slot result before type
handling or warning emission and validates it again after a subclass-result
deprecation warning. This prevents callers from copying, formatting or reading
an inaccessible integer subclass. The common helper also covers index fallbacks
used by PyNumber_Long and PyNumber_Float, without claiming to cover their direct
nb_int and nb_float paths.

A native index-only tuple subtype exposes an unchecked stored result. New tests
cover foreign integer inputs, foreign native results, protected Python index
operands, and warnings that end debugger suspension while a foreign result is
still held by the API. Tests verify that rejected results do not emit warnings,
that allowed conversions still produce the expected values and warning, and
that warning-handler LookupError is preserved. Tested consumers include
PyNumber_Index, PyNumber_AsSsize_t, PyNumber_ToBase and the index fallbacks of
PyNumber_Long/PyNumber_Float.

The initial reproducer reported thirteen failures:
/tmp/pep805-index-before.log. The final focused run passes 90 tests:
/tmp/pep805-index-ft-focused.log.

Direct integer and floating-point conversion
-------------------------------------------

PyNumber_Long and PyNumber_Float validate their input before selecting a native
conversion slot or a text/buffer fallback. They validate owned nb_int/nb_float
results before examining their type or emitting subclass-result warnings, then
revalidate subclass results after warning handlers before copying/reading the
numeric payload. Index fallbacks retain the previous checked implementation.

A native tuple subtype exposes an unchecked stored result through nb_int and
nb_float. Five new tests cover foreign int/float operands, foreign str/bytes
subclasses and bytearrays, foreign native conversion results, protected Python
operands, and warning handlers ending debugger suspension or raising LookupError.
Tests preserve successful conversion and warning behavior when access is allowed
and verify that rejected results do not emit warnings. The foreign-value helper
now creates either an int or float subclass in its worker group.

Low-level double conversion
---------------------------

PyFloat_AsDouble validates operand access before reading a float subclass or
calling a numeric slot, validates the owned nb_float result, and revalidates a
subclass result after warning handlers run. The established -1.0/error-indicator
convention is preserved. The second result check is confined to the warning
path; exact native float results are checked once.

Five new tests cover foreign int/float inputs, foreign nb_float and nb_index
results, protected Python operands, warning handlers ending debugger suspension
or raising LookupError, and Argument Clinic's double conversion through
_testcapi.float_pack. Allowed calls retain their expected scalar/packed-byte
results. The original reproducer reported five failures:
/tmp/pep805-asdouble-before.log. The focused run passes 101 tests (one skip):
/tmp/pep805-asdouble-ft-focused.log.

Direct integer C APIs, additional native numeric readers and text/buffer view

Classic integer scalar conversions
----------------------------------

The classic PyLong scalar conversion paths validate operand access before
reading integer tags, signs or digits. This covers AsInt/AsLong, both signed
AndOverflow variants, AsLongLong, unsigned long/long long, both mask variants,
ssize_t/size_t, double and void-pointer conversion. AsInt/AsLong inherit the
check from AsLongAndOverflow; the overflow indicators are cleared before access
validation. AsVoidPtr checks before choosing its signed or unsigned conversion.
Existing error return conventions are preserved.

Three new tests exercise thirteen APIs with compact positive/negative values
and positive/negative multi-digit values, native index results, and Argument
Clinic's integer argument conversion. With debugger access allowed, results
and OverflowError behavior are compared to conversion of the corresponding
exact int. The existing AndOverflow wrappers assert a zero overflow flag on
access errors. A small pointer-conversion wrapper returns pointer bits as an
integer without dereferencing arbitrary addresses. The foreign-value helper
now accepts a value as well as an int/float base type.

access denial was expected): /tmp/pep805-longscalar-before.log. The focused
post-fix run passes 150 tests: /tmp/pep805-longscalar-ft-focused.log.
